The compiler is split by concern: `Compile` is the orchestration, the per-host step (the admin-host rules included) is `SnapshotCompiler.Hosts.cs`, and upstreams, TLS/certificates and response headers have their own partials, each filling a small `CompileContext`.
- **Layering.** Per host: built-in defaults → global defaults → its profile (a missing profile adds no layer; the validator rejects it, `host.profile.unknown`) → the host; per route, its own subset on top of the host's result. A null field inherits. TLS merges field by field. `ForceHttps` is judged after layering. (M2 had an inline IP allow list that replaced as a whole; M7 replaced it with named access lists, §7.3.)
- **Access lists: host chain and route chains (M2's inline allow list, as M7 reworked it; §7.3).** The gate runs the host chain before the HTTPS redirect, fixed answers and route matching, so a route override can't *replace* a list that sits there alone. When every route ends up with the same list (none overrides the host's, or all override it alike) and it has no users, or the host has no routes, that list goes in the host chain only and guards everything the host answers. Otherwise each route carries its own effective list, and the host chain carries the **union** of the routes' address rules, dropping a refused request only if every route's list drops: a client no route admits is blocked on every path, over plain HTTP too (no redirect, no 403-vs-404 to map the host by, and a dropping host still looks dead), while one that some route admits is judged again by the route it hits. If some route's list can't refuse by address (or a route has none) the host chain carries no union, since anyone may reach that route. A list with users puts only its address rules in the host chain and the whole list (passwords too) in each route's chain, so a password is never asked for before the HTTPS redirect. A test drives the real gate over generated documents (scheme x path x client IP, lists with and without users) against a reference that reads the document directly.
- **Rule sets: host chain and route chains (M7; §7.3).** Same shape as the access list's rule, without the union: when every route resolves to one set, or the host has no routes, the set is in the host chain (after the host chain's address-only check or union; for a host with no routes, before a full list that has users), otherwise each route's own chain carries its set, in front of its list when that has users (the list's address checks included), because a route's set replaces the host's and a deny can't be lifted by a union.
- **Auth providers: route chains only (M7; §7.3).** The provider is not a chain entry the mapping builds: it is a route policy the compiler adds after the rate limit and before proof of work, because a forward-auth call is the one expensive thing a request can cost outside the upstream and a 429 should come first, and because a cookie must never be sent to an endpoint over plain HTTP (the route chain runs after the HTTPS redirect). So the mapping only resolves which provider each route has (`RouteSpec.AuthProvider`, with the route's own override marked), and an admin, redirect or respond host ignores the setting with a warning (`authProvider.ignored`).
- **Certificate binding (as built in M2; M3 resolves it against the inventory, §9.2).** Auto → no certificate of its own (the default one); Specific → its `CertificateId`; None → `HostSpec.Https = false`: no certificate-table entries and no `Alt-Svc`, and the gate answers HTTPS requests for the host 421 (a connection on the default or another host's certificate can't reach it). None with an effective `ForceHttps` is an error (validator: `host.certificate.noneWithForceHttps`; compiler at the host). Disabled destinations are left out; an upstream may have none left until an enabled host routes to it.
- **Compile errors carry paths.** `SnapshotCompiler` attributes each error to a host, route, upstream, destination (by typed ID) or the defaults/trusted proxies; `ConfigCompiler` maps that to a pointer into the document *as given* (`/hosts/{i}`, `/hosts/{i}/routes/{j}`, `/upstreams/{k}/destinations/{m}`, `/settings`, `/settings/trustedProxies`) with code `compile.failed`, each (path, message) once. An inherited value is reported where it is used (each host or route whose chain carries it); a duplicated ID points at its last occurrence. The mapping itself reports the few invalid values the model can't carry (an undefined certificate binding mode or load-balancing policy, a respond host without a content type). A test runs sample, edge and generated documents through the validator and the compiler: whatever validates must compile (certificate references aside, which only the compiler can resolve against the inventory, §9.2).

As built in M2 (`Paduraksa.Application.Commands.ConfigService`, commands and results in `Paduraksa.Contracts.Commands`):
- **Versions are revision numbers.** A revision gives every top-level object it changes (a route or destination change counts for its parent) its own `Seq` as the new version; untouched objects keep theirs, whatever a command sends. A version is therefore issued once, ever, and stays at most the latest `Seq`: a rollback to #37 committed as #52 gives what it changes version 52, so an editor holding any older version of those objects (including one from #37, or of an object deleted and restored) gets a conflict, while editors of objects the rollback leaves alone don't.
- **The empty config** is the default document with no revision. Startup writes nothing; the first command creates revision #1, and a whole-document command on an empty database names no base revision.
- **Errors** of whole-document commands point into the document as sent (a rollback: as stored). Object commands report the sent object's errors relative to it (`/names/1`), and errors the change causes elsewhere (a deleted upstream still in use) as related errors pointing into the canonical document after the change, like the change list. The sent object is validated last in its list, so a clash with another object is reported on it.
- **Commands that change nothing** (equal canonical config) commit nothing and return `unchanged`.
- **Audit:** commits in the commit's transaction; denied, invalid, conflict and not-found commands in a transaction of their own, written without the caller's cancellation once decided, a failed write logged rather than replacing the result; a publish failure after a commit as `config.publish` (failed), also when it happens after the command stopped waiting. Dry runs and no-ops aren't audited.
- **Publishing:** queued with the publisher under the writer lock, so snapshots go live in commit order; the command waits for the outcome outside the lock (bounded, then `pending`). A superseded publish reports the outcome of the newer publish that carries it. A failed publish is reported, logged and audited by the command whose publish it was; the commit stands, with no retry.
- **One writer.** This process is the only writer of the config database; the CLI writes identity and the vault, never config. A second server process on the same data directory is refused at startup (the file lock of §8.4), and if one got past it (a file system that cannot lock), its commit would fail on the unique `Seq` and parent indexes of `ConfigRevisions` rather than forking history.

As built in M2 (startup: `ConfigService.StartAsync`, `Paduraksa.Application.LastKnownGood`; composition: `Server/Hosting/ConfigStore`, `ConfigStartup`):
- **Composition.** The config database (`<data dir>/paduraksa.db`), its context factory, the compiler (`ConfigCompiler` on `PublicListenerSetup.CompilerOptions`), the `LastKnownGoodFile` and the one `ConfigService` are shared instances, registered into all three hosts. The factory and the service come from a small private container (EF's factory and the service are meant to be resolved), which `SharedServices` owns and which alone disposes them. Disposal runs in reverse creation order after every host has stopped: the container (the service refuses new commands), then the publisher (stops its writer and fails whatever is queued, so nothing goes live after it), then the last-known-good file, created before the publisher (waits for its pending write); so the file never trails the last revision that went live. Certificate bindings resolve against the certificate inventory (§9.2); the placeholder certificate is its fallback default (§3).
- **Startup recompile.** The core host's `ConfigStartup` creates the data directory and runs `StartAsync` under the writer lock: migrate (`ConfigDatabase.InitializeAsync`), read the latest revision (none: the empty config), compile, and queue the publish **without awaiting it** (as in M1: YARP attaches only when the public app starts). The outcome is logged when it arrives; a superseded startup publish (a commit's newer publish carries the config) isn't a failure.
- **File modes** (threat model G7). The config database is created 0600 and so are its `-wal` and `-shm` (SQLite gives them the main file's mode); `PrivateSqliteFile` (Storage, shared with the vault's database) creates it and, at every `ConfigDatabase.InitializeAsync`, takes group and other access away from an existing file and its WAL files and reports which it changed (a warning is logged once). It holds the users' password hashes, the session and token hashes and the audit log, so it no longer depends on the data directory's mode or the process umask. The data directory itself keeps the mode its creator gave it (the image's 0750, a bind mount's).
- **Last-known-good** (`last-good.json`): `{format, revision, seq, sha256, document}`, the document being the revision's canonical bytes verbatim, so the hash is the stored `DocumentHash`; references only, no secrets. Offered after every successful publish (the startup one and each commit's, before the command returns) and written in the background, never under the writer lock; the newest offer wins, older ones are dropped. Each write goes to a new temp file with a random name (`CreateNew`, mode 0600 on Unix; leftovers of a crash are removed first, a symlink as a link), is flushed to disk and renamed over the target, which replaces a symlink there instead of following it. The directory isn't fsynced (.NET can't open a directory): safe but not durable, a power loss right after a write may leave the previous, still valid, file. A failed write is logged and never faults the writer. Reading uses one bounded read, refuses a symlink, checks format, IDs and hash, and never throws: a damaged file is logged as a warning (also on a healthy start) and replaced at the next publish. The empty config isn't written: it is no revision. One data directory serves one server process at a time (enforced since M10 by a file lock, `run/server.lock`, §8.4).
- **Restore.** A database without revisions next to a usable last-known-good file has lost its config (a deleted or replaced `paduraksa.db`): the file's document is committed as revision #1 through the normal replace path (actor System, message "Restored from last-known-good #N (rev_...)", audited), logged at Critical, and startup goes on normally. If that commit is rejected or fails, the start is degraded on the last-known-good config (on the empty config if that doesn't compile either), accepting commands. To start over with an empty config, delete both files. A file newer than a database that has revisions (a restored backup) is only a warning: the database wins.
- **Degraded starts.** If the database can't be opened or migrated, or its latest revision can't be read (corrupt document, unreadable by this build, or columns a converter rejects: a damaged ID or timestamp), or it doesn't compile, the last-known-good document is compiled and published instead, logged at Critical, and `ConfigService.Status` says so (`Source = LastKnownGood`, the problem, the revision served). The store itself unusable: commands get `Unavailable` (`config.store.unavailable`, unaudited, since the audit log lives in that store) until the database is fixed or restored and the process restarted. Only the compile failed (say, after an upgrade made the compiler stricter): the database is fine, so commands are accepted and planned on the latest revision as always, and the first one that goes live ends the degraded state; refusing them would leave no way to fix the config short of editing the database.
- **A failed startup publish** (YARP rejects it or times out) leaves no live config. If the last-known-good file holds another revision and no command has committed since, that is published instead, degraded and accepting commands; otherwise the process stays up without a snapshot and `/readyz` says not ready, as in M1. (Tested with a publish the publisher refuses; every YARP setting the compiler emits is checked before YARP sees it, so no config makes YARP itself reject one.)
- **Readiness while degraded.** `/readyz` stays 200, with the body `degraded` instead of `ready`: the proxy serves traffic, and taking it out of rotation would turn a config-store problem into an outage. Keyword monitors can alert on the body; the cause is in the Critical log, and the admin UI shows the banner (M5).
- **Nothing to serve.** Neither a usable database revision nor a usable last-known-good document: `StartAsync` throws `ConfigStartupException` and the process exits 2 (startup failed), logged once at Critical by the host. An empty proxy that refuses changes would only look alive.
- **Change → live** through the whole process (real SQLite, YARP and Kestrel; one upstream and one host, two commits, until the first request for the new host comes back): median 11-15 ms on a dev machine with the rest of the suite running alongside.


**As built (cleanup, after M7).** An external review of the Application layer found four things; each was checked before it was acted on.
- **One writer, one process.** `ConfigService` carried a protocol for a second writer that doesn't exist (the CLI never writes config). The re-plan path for a commit that finds the head moved is gone, with its log line, its conflict fallback and its tests. What stays is what one process needs: the writer lock, the unique revision indexes as the backstop, and the whole recovery set (last-known-good, restore of an empty database, degraded starts, the fallback after a failed startup publish), because each of those happens with one process (a deleted database, a corrupt revision, a stricter compiler, a YARP refusal).
- **`ConfigService` is split by responsibility, not by partial.** `ConfigService` is the public surface and the writer lock; `ConfigPipeline` runs a command's steps (authorize, plan, validate, compile, guard, commit); `ConfigPublishing` carries a commit to live (queue, wait, last-known-good, warnings, validity timer, store status); `ConfigRecovery` starts the store and republishes the live config; `ConfigDrafts` serves the request simulator; `ConfigCommandPlanner`, `AdminLockoutGuard` and `CertificateBindingGuard` are pure functions of documents, tested on documents alone. `ConfigAudit` is how the writer records audit events.
- **The projection is dropped.** The seven tables (`GlobalSettings`, `Profiles`, `Upstreams`, `Destinations`, `Hosts`, `HostNames`, `Routes`) were written on every commit and read only by startup's own consistency check and by tests. `ConfigProjection`, the entities, the startup heal and the migration `DropConfigProjection` replace them; a test migrates a database created at the previous migration and keeps its revisions.
- **Authorization converged.** There were three typed authorizers (config commands, certificate operations, simulations, each with an allow-all default that identity registration had to replace in whichever order the two ran), an optional evaluator in the services outside the command pipeline, Admin endpoint filters and the dashboard's permission sets. The authorizers and their defaults are gone: `IPermissionEvaluator` is the one check and is required everywhere. Admin keeps its minimal-API filters (`RequirePermission`, with the existing conventions that every caller requirement has its filter and every route its expected permission); the dashboard's sets come from the same evaluator. This also fixed a real defect: the issuance services were built in a container that never registered the evaluator, so their own permission checks were silently off in production (the Admin filters still held). A host-level test now pins it.
- **No optional constructor parameters in Application.** Production dependencies are required (a nullable type only where null is a state of the world: no built-in certificates, no external account binding, no DNS provider), the ACME client provider's fallback registration is gone, and `ApplicationConstructorConventionTests` fails when one comes back. Tests get their defaults from shared builders (`TestConfigServices`, `TestPermissions`, the issuance harness).
- **Left as it was.** The recovery cases, the commit-time unique-index backstop, and Admin's attribute-free filters (a decision of the owner). The dashboard still hides what the user may not do from its own permission set, the evaluator's answer at the last re-check; the services refuse regardless.
---

## 7. Hot path

### 7.1 Budget & rules
- No DB, no `await` on anything except I/O to client/upstream, no locks (`lock`, `SemaphoreSlim`), no LINQ, no closures, no `string` building, no regex with backtracking.
- Per-request allocations in Paduraksa code: **0 B** steady state on keep-alive connections. Kestrel/YARP's own allocations are out of scope but measured. Enforced by benchmark tests asserting `GC.GetAllocatedBytesForCurrentThread()` deltas.
- Precompute everything at compile time: byte arrays for responses/headers, `SearchValues<string>` for substring sets, CIDR tries, country bitsets, redirect targets.
- Options validate themselves: an invalid value throws at `init` or construction, never later on the hot path (the snapshot compiler checks config first and reports it as an error).

**Spike S3 confirmed this** (`docs/spikes/S3-zero-alloc-gate.md`; .NET 10.0.13, YARP 2.3.0): the gate measured **0 B/request on H1 and H2** in every end-to-end run, taking 145-225 ns for the synchronous section. For realistic browser traffic, the costs we accept are:

| | B/request |
|---|---|
| Kestrel itself | ~1 KB |
| Routing + YARP hand-off | 152 B |
| Whole proxied request | ~4.1 KB on H1, ~4.4 KB on H2 |

Mechanisms M1 adopts:
- **Pipeline:** the public app is built on the **generic host with an explicit pipeline**: `Configure(app => { app.Use(gate); app.UseRouting(); app.UseEndpoints(e => e.MapReverseProxy(...)); })`, not `WebApplication`. YARP calls `AddAuthorization()` unconditionally, and `WebApplication` then auto-inserts `UseAuthorization()` *ahead of the gate*. That costs bytes and is a hazard if a `FallbackPolicy` is ever set. A guard test with a spy `IAuthorizationPolicyProvider` asserts it never runs.
- **Gate shape:** one synchronous gate middleware. It decides what to do, and the response body is written *after* the measured section. Responses come from precomputed bytes plus shared header values set through the typed header properties. The one exception is a policy that can't decide at once (an access list's password check, `PolicyAction.Defer`): the gate awaits it after the decision and resumes the chain after that policy (**As built (M7): access lists**); every synchronous path stays 0 B.
- **Per-connection state:** an immutable `ClientInfo`, set as a connection feature in `ListenOptions.Use`. It's shared by all H2 streams on the connection, so it must stay immutable. Never use `IConnectionItemsFeature.Items`, which is one non-thread-safe dictionary for all streams.
- **Per-request state:** one object cached in `IPersistentStateFeature` and reset each request. Stream reuse happens only on graceful end, so RST, cancels and Drop reallocate, and that's accepted.
- **Host lookup:** `Headers.Host[0]` (H2 `:authority` is already folded in), with the port stripped by span. Two `FrozenDictionary` tables (exact names, wildcard parents), `OrdinalIgnoreCase`, looked up via `GetAlternateLookup<ReadOnlySpan<char>>`: 11-22 ns, 0 B. `Request.Host.Host` allocates when a port is present. The compiler **rejects duplicate host names** (otherwise the last one silently wins) and canonicalises IPv6-literal keys.
- **Client-IP parsing:** our own span parser into `UInt128`. `IPAddress.TryParse` allocates 40/80 B. Ours is stricter than the BCL: no octal, hex, short forms, zone IDs or whitespace. M1 keeps the edge-case list plus a differential fuzz against the BCL.
- **Policies:** sealed `IRequestPolicy` classes returning a 16-byte `PolicyDecision` struct.
- **Tests:** `AllocAssert.Zero(state, static lambda)` for synchronous code, plus in-process Kestrel tests asserting the gate's decision, the access log's completion callback and the reapply response-start hook allocate 0 B. Kestrel and YARP numbers are tracked in `bench/`, not asserted.
- **Benchmarks:** they run under workstation GC, because BenchmarkDotNet's `Allocated` column is wrong under Server GC with the in-process toolchain. Non-monotonic `GC.GetTotalAllocatedBytes` samples are discarded.

### 7.2 Request pipeline

```
A. Connection (TCP: Kestrel ListenOptions.Use. QUIC has no such hook, so the same checks run inside the
   QUIC TlsHandshakeCallbackOptions callback, where Connection.RemoteEndPoint is available; see S1)
   • PROXY protocol (opt-in per listener, TCP only): parsed HERE, before TLS, so the real client IP
     feeds connection limits, IP deny and SNI-stage logic. v1 (text) and v2 (binary) both accepted.
     Only from configured trusted source CIDRs; a header from anyone else = connection dropped.
     Header must arrive within a short timeout. When enabled on a listener it is mandatory, never optional.
   • global IP deny (CIDR trie over UInt128) → abort before TLS. Normalise IPv4-mapped IPv6 first:
     on a dual-mode [::] listener TCP reports ::ffff:a.b.c.d while QUIC reports a.b.c.d
   • per-IP concurrent-connection cap (fixed-size table, CAS)
   • compute ClientInfo once per connection (address, source) → connection feature; the country is per request (As built (M7): country rules)
   As built in M1: UseClientInfo on both TCP listeners (HTTPS: through PublicHttpsListenerOptions.
   ConfigureTcpConnection, before UseHttps); on QUIC, QuicClientInfoGate attaches ClientInfo.FromEndPoint
   to the connection in the handshake callback, and H3 requests see it (verified end to end in Docker).
   IpKey stores IPv4 and IPv4-mapped alike, so TCP and QUIC agree. Global IP deny and the per-IP cap are
   a marked seam in that gate (M7); M1 acquires nothing there, so the QUIC ConnectionClosed gap can't leak.

B. TLS handshake (TlsHandshakeCallbackOptions on both TCP and QUIC → per-SNI SslServerAuthenticationOptions)
   • SNI → snapshot → PREBUILT SslServerAuthenticationOptions, deduplicated on (cert, host TLS policy, transport).
     The compiler builds RuntimeSnapshot.Certificates: each enabled host's DNS names (exact and *.wildcard;
     IP literals never arrive as SNI) map to its certificate (a CertificateId), or to the default certificate with
     the host's own TLS settings (min version, TCP ALPN, H3 on/off); certificate IDs resolve through
     IServerCertificateResolver (the vault's source in M3, a placeholder until then).
   • the handshake only sees the SNI's host, and clients coalesce connections across every name a certificate
     covers, so the host's TLS settings are re-checked per REQUEST in the gate (stage C, ConnectionTlsCheck)
     Each holds a shared SslStreamCertificateContext.Create(cert, chain, offline: true): the only way the
     intermediate is reliably sent. Without it, TCP may fetch it via AIA mid-handshake and QUIC sends the leaf only.
   • ApplicationProtocols ALWAYS set explicitly ([h2, http/1.1] or the host's ALPN on TCP, [h3] on QUIC).
     Kestrel writes ALPN into a returned options object whose list is null (both transports), which
     silently breaks the other transport if instances are shared. A unit test asserts non-null ALPN.
   • unknown SNI: default cert, or reject by THROWING from the callback (client sees EOF / TLS alert; server
     stays healthy). Count rejections ourselves: QUIC logs nothing.
   • cipher suites: OpenSSL's defaults, NO CipherSuitesPolicy. On Linux SslStream caches the OpenSSL context and
     resumes sessions only without one; with the M1 AEAD+ECDHE policy every new connection cost ~1.5x the CPU
     (5.3k vs 8.4k handshakes/s, bench `handshake`, 2026-11-10). A host that must refuse TLS 1.2's older suites
     sets MinimumVersion = Tls13; QUIC (TLS 1.3) is AEAD-only already.
   • client certificates (per host): chain built offline (no AIA/CRL/OCSP fetches: a client-controlled AIA URL
     would be SSRF); as built in M7, Authenticated Origin Pulls hosts use CustomRootTrust with the origin-pull CA (§7.6).

C. Gate middleware (first in pipeline), in this order (as built in M1)
   • snapshot = SnapshotStore.Current (one volatile read)
   • resolve client IP: the PEER is the PROXY-protocol source from stage A if present, else the socket peer;
     the trusted-proxy policy then applies to that peer as usual: only a peer in the trusted-proxies list
     may supply the client through X-Forwarded-For / CF-Connecting-IP (native Cloudflare handling in §7.6),
     otherwise the peer is the client. Built as TrustedProxyClientAddressSource: the connection's
     ClientInfo (else Connection.RemoteIpAddress) through the snapshot's global TrustedProxyPolicy, 0 B;
     header fallbacks are counted. The whole ClientIpResolution stays on the request state for policies
     and the forwarding headers.
   • resolve the trusted request SCHEME in the same step (ForwardedScheme, 0 B): from a trusted Cloudflare
     peer CF-Visitor (exactly {"scheme":"https"} or {"scheme":"http"}, anything else invalid), else its
     X-Forwarded-Proto; from a trusted generic proxy X-Forwarded-Proto only with TrustForwardedProto
     (default off); otherwise the connection's. The HTTPS redirect and the upstream X-Forwarded-Proto both
     use it, so a TLS-terminating proxy (cloudflared → :80, Flexible SSL) can't loop the redirect.
     Cloudflare over plain HTTP for a plain-HTTP visitor (Flexible) is counted and logged once.
   • plain HTTP: GET/HEAD /.well-known/acme-challenge/{token} → AcmeChallengeStore (a ConcurrentDictionary
     read through a span lookup, 0 B) → 200 text/plain, before everything below; a token that isn't published
     falls through to the normal pipeline (as built in M3, §9.3)
   • resolve host (span-based lookup, no string allocation); unknown → Default behaviour
   • the host's response-header list, stage 1 (as built in M6, one list since the settings refactor): its Server
     operation, 0 B, before the TLS check, so a 421 carries it too; the unknown-host answer and an ACME challenge
     follow the global defaults' list
   • HTTPS: the host's TlsPolicy against the request's connection (HTTP/3 = QUIC/TLS 1.3 vs Http3Enabled,
     HTTP/2 vs an HTTP/1.1-only ALPN, ITlsHandshakeFeature.Protocol vs the minimum version) → 421
     Misdirected Request when the connection is weaker, so browsers retry on a connection of their own;
     a few comparisons on per-connection data, 0 B. Authenticated Origin Pulls has its own step before this one (§7.6).
   • the host's response-header list, stage 2: its Alt-Svc and Strict-Transport-Security operations on the answers
     after the TLS check (forwarded or answered), 0 B (§7.5). Alt-Svc goes on TCP HTTPS only (not over HTTP/3) and
     only for hosts with names in the certificate table (not IP-literal-only hosts); HSTS goes only when the TRUSTED
     request scheme is HTTPS, and never for an IP-literal or loopback name (localhost, *.localhost, 127.0.0.0/8,
     ::1), decided by a span check on the Host header's name. Ours replaces an upstream's own header; with HSTS off,
     the upstream's passes through untouched.
   • HOST policy chain (D), so it also guards the redirect and fixed answers (blocked → 403, not 301)
   • the host's request limits (as built in M7, §7.3): the request target over its limit is 414, more header lines or bytes
     than it takes is 431, answered here, after the host chain (a client it refuses learns nothing from sizes) and before
     the redirect (which would echo the target). The host's body limit was put on the request when the host was found
   • HTTP→HTTPS redirect answered here from precomputed bytes
   • a managed robots.txt (GET/HEAD only) answered here from precomputed bytes, so an allow list still refused above and a
     plain-HTTP request still redirected first; before the fixed answer below and before route matching, so a
     Redirect/Respond host can carry one and the route chain never sees the file
   • Redirect and Respond hosts answered here from precomputed bytes
   • resolve route (per-host list, most specific first, on the canonical matching view of the path; §6.2) → 404 if none
   • ROUTE policy chain (D): the route's own policies only
   • the declared body (as built in M7): a `Content-Length` over the route's (else the host's) limit is 413, after the route's
     own chain and before anything reads the body
   • HttpContext.SetEndpoint(YARP Endpoint from the same snapshot) so the later UseRouting skips matching

D. Policy chains: one per host, one per route (IRequestPolicy[], sealed classes, evaluated in order;
   host policies run before a route is known, so State.Route is null for them)
   AccessList (IP and country allow/deny, then basic auth; as built, the host chain only refuses by address and country; geo is part of it) → RuleSet (path, method
   and user-agent rules; as built in M7 it comes after the host chain's address-only list policy and before a route's
   list that has users, address checks included, see §7.3) → RateLimit →
   [RequestLimits: as built, not a policy; the gate checks it, see below] → Robots → Auth (forward-auth / OIDC) → ProofOfWork →
   [future: bot heuristics, header rules]
   each returns PolicyDecision { Continue | Respond(status, page) | Challenge | Drop } as a struct

E. YARP (endpoint already set by the gate → affinity → load balancing → passive health → forwarder)
   with Paduraksa transforms
   • X-Forwarded-*, X-Real-IP, request ID, security headers, custom headers (precompiled transforms)
   • forwarding headers as built in M1 (YARP's own X-Forwarded-* transforms are off), always OVERWRITTEN:
     X-Forwarded-For, X-Real-IP = exactly one address, the resolved client (no chain is kept or appended;
       IPv4 dotted quad, also for IPv4-mapped peers; IPv6 without brackets); left out if unknown
     X-Forwarded-Proto = the trusted request scheme from stage C (the same one the redirect uses)
     X-Forwarded-Host = Host / :authority as received, port included
     Host = the client's Host / :authority (YARP's default would send the destination's address; fixed after M6,
       so name-based upstreams, absolute URLs and trusted-domain checks work). On an HTTPS upstream it is also the
       SNI and the name the upstream's certificate is checked against, unless the upstream's TLS trust is `Pinned` or
       `Any` (§5.1): `Any` is YARP's DangerousAcceptAnyServerCertificate, `Pinned` a validation callback the client
       factory installs from the cluster's metadata (UpstreamCertificatePins), and a changed pin list builds a new client.
       Still planned: the upstream's Host header mode (Original / Destination / Custom, §5.1), with SNI following it
       unless set apart.
     Forwarded, X-Forwarded-Prefix, X-Forwarded-Port = removed (only a client could have set them)
     every cf-* header (case-insensitive: CF-Connecting-IP, CF-Visitor, Cf-Access-Jwt-Assertion,
       Cf-Access-Authenticated-User-Email, cf-ipcity, ...) and True-Client-IP = removed unless
       CloudflareHeadersTrusted (Cloudflare mode and trusted peer); a trusted generic proxy doesn't qualify.
       One scan of the outgoing header names, 0 B. Otherwise a LAN client could forge Cloudflare Access SSO
       past a tunnel.
     The address string is cached on the pooled request state; the rest are constants or Kestrel's strings.
   • upstream errors / no healthy destinations → custom error pages

F. Telemetry (always on, lock-free): counters + latency histogram per host / status class / upstream
```

**As built (M6): the response headers.** The compiler stores one array per host (`CompiledHost.ResponseHeaders`): an
operation per header - <c>Alt-Svc</c>, <c>Strict-Transport-Security</c>, <c>Server</c> and the response
<c>X-Request-Id</c> - with its precomputed `StringValues` (or the request ID as its source), what to do with it (set,
replace the upstream's, remove, leave to the upstream, or not sent with the reason), when (after host lookup, after the TLS
check, or with the answer), and what has to hold (the connection is TLS; the trusted scheme is HTTPS; the name is no IP
literal or loopback; not over HTTP/3). A host that doesn't send a header still has its entry, so the trace can say why.
The list is capped at 16 operations (`ResponseHeaders.MaxResponseHeaderOperations`), which the compiler enforces with an
error: the gate's per-request outcomes live in a stack buffer of exactly that size, so a longer list can never reach it.
The gate applies a stage of that list and nothing else: `Server` right after host lookup, so gate answers (403, the HTTPS
redirect, Redirect/Respond, 404, 421) and forwards all carry it at 0 B; then `Alt-Svc` and HSTS, so a 421 carries neither.
The default is `Server: Paduraksa`, with no version, and Kestrel's own header stays off on both listeners. YARP 2.3's
`HttpTransformer.CopyResponseHeaders` special-cases only `Strict-Transport-Security`: a destination's `Server` is
concatenated with what is already there. So one response transform (`ResponseHeadersTransform`) loops over the same list
after the copy: `Paduraksa`/`Custom` replace the client-facing value, `Remove` drops it, `Upstream` leaves it, and the
response `X-Request-Id` is ours or gone as the host's propagation mode says. It also runs when the forward fails (the
502/504 path has no proxy response) and before a WebSocket upgrade is accepted. One YARP path clears the response without
re-running it: an upstream that sends headers and then fails before the first body byte gets `context.Response.Clear()` and
a bare 502. The proxy registers a static `Response.OnStarting` hook that checks YARP's `IForwarderErrorFeature` and, only
then, calls `GateResponseHeaders.Reapply`, which walks the whole list again, so the host's `Alt-Svc`, HSTS, `Server` and
response `X-Request-Id` all come back, 0 B. YARP sets that feature before the response starts on every answer that clears
or bypasses the transforms: no healthy destination (503), a failed request or timeout (502/504), malformed response
headers (502) and a response-body error (502). On a successful forward the transforms already applied the headers, so the
hook does nothing; a bodyless upstream response such as a 204 or a 304 starts at the end of the request, after the
transforms, and is no longer applied a second time. The response `X-Request-Id` is the one header the decision leaves to
the answer, because its value is the one string a request materialises; the trace reports it as such. An unknown host and an ACME challenge answer use the global defaults' list,
which carries neither `Alt-Svc` nor HSTS.

**As built (M7): the PoW gate.** `HostSettings.ProofOfWork` (`Enabled`, `Difficulty`, `TokenLifetimeHours`; built-in off,
16 bits, seven days) inherits through the layers, and `RouteSettings.ProofOfWork` carries only `Enabled`, which overrides
the host's resolved switch either way, so an `/api` route can be exempted and one path protected on a host that is otherwise
open. The compiled route gets a `ProofOfWorkPolicy` after its other policies (a Redirect/Respond host has no routes and is
never challenged); a route's values are the host's, because only the switch is overridable. `ProofOfWorkStats` are
process-lifetime `Interlocked` counters (challenges, solved, rejected by reason, 403s to non-browsers), registered beside the
runtime but not shown yet.

*Protocol.* The page (`GET`/`HEAD` accepting `text/html`) carries a challenge: base64url of
`version | issuedAt | nonce(16) | difficulty | tag(16)`, HMAC-SHA-256 over a `paduraksa-pow-challenge-v1` prefix, the
host's stable `HostId` bytes and the client IP masked to /32 (IPv4) or /64 (IPv6), valid ten minutes. Its JavaScript, a
pure-JS SHA-256 because `crypto.subtle` needs a secure origin, searches in `navigator.hardwareConcurrency` Web Workers
(1-8) for a decimal counter where `SHA-256(challenge + ":" + counter)` has `Difficulty` leading zero bits, then posts
`challenge`, `solution` and `return` to `POST /.well-known/paduraksa/pow`. The gate answers that endpoint itself, before
route matching, on any host where the gate is on for the host or a route (elsewhere the path falls through): it checks the
body (2 KiB), the challenge's tag and age, the solution and the challenge's difficulty against the host's current one, and
answers `303` to a local `return` target with `paduraksa_pow` = base64url of
`version | expiresAt | difficulty | tag(first 16 bytes)`, `Path=/; HttpOnly; SameSite=Lax; Max-Age`, and `Secure` on the
trusted request scheme. An expired challenge gets the same `303` without a cookie (a fresh challenge follows); anything else
is `403`. The valid-token path reads the cookie from the `Cookie` header span into `stackalloc` buffers, verifies with
`IMacKey` and allocates 0 B (`GateMiddleware.Decide` allocation test). The page's CSP is
`default-src 'none'; script-src 'sha256-...'; style-src 'sha256-...'; worker-src blob:; form-action 'self'; base-uri 'none';
frame-ancestors 'none'`, with the hashes computed once from the inline script and style; `Referrer-Policy: no-referrer` and
`X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff` ride along, and the host's response headers apply as on any gate answer.

*Exemptions and failure.* `GET`/`HEAD /robots.txt` and the verify endpoint are never challenged. The key is one vault
secret of kind `HmacSigningKey` (HMAC-SHA-256, 32 random bytes) under a reserved ID
(`ProofOfWorkKey.Id`), created at first start and opened into the process runtime by a core-host step after the vault and
before the public listener binds; rotation stays the vault's `ReplaceAsync` plus its `Verify` grace window. If it can't be
opened, every protected route answers `503` and one Error is logged: never a request through unchallenged. The challenge is
stateless, so a solved one can be redeemed again within its ten minutes; that only yields a cookie for the same host and
client address, which is the accepted trade-off (no table to grow, nothing a second solve would not give). A warning is
reported for a host with the gate on that answers plain HTTP without forcing HTTPS, where the cookie cannot be `Secure`.
`IMacKey.VerifyTruncated` compares a tag of 16 bytes or more as the prefix of the full tag (constant time), which is how
the 16-byte token and challenge tags are checked; `Verify` itself only takes whole tags.

### 7.3 WAF building blocks (all compiled per snapshot)

| Feature | Implementation |
|---|---|
| IP allow/deny | `CidrSet` (M1): merged, sorted, disjoint `UInt128` ranges (IPv4-mapped), binary-searched, immutable. Chosen over a radix trie: membership needs no longest-prefix match, and the arrays are smaller and need fewer dependent loads (S3: 7.7 ns for 2,000 CIDRs). IPv6 rate keys are masked to /64 (`IpKey.MaskTo`). |
| Access lists | A named object (`acl_`) with allow and deny `CidrSet`s, basic-auth users (each a username and a password credential in the vault, verified through its verifier and cache) and `satisfy` all or any; see **As built (M7): access lists** below. |
| Geo blocking | **M7: `CF-IPCountry` only**, as country rules of an access list (**As built (M7): country rules** below): a two-letter code from a trusted Cloudflare peer, compiled per list into a bitset (676 letter pairs plus Tor and unknown), one bit test per request. **Not built: a local country database** (DB-IP Lite: CC-BY, no key; MaxMind GeoLite2 optional with a license key; compiled into sorted range arrays and binary-searched, an auto-update job); the seam it plugs into is in the gap list of that note. |
| Rate limiting | Fixed-size open-addressed table (power of two, default 256 Ki entries ≈ 4 MiB). Each entry is two `long`s: a 64-bit **key fingerprint** (claimed with CAS, linear probing over at most 8 slots, else the oldest of those is evicted) and a packed **state** updated with a CAS loop. State layout: **bits 63-24 = last-refill time** in ms since process start (40 bits ≈ 34.8 years, so no wraparound handling needed); **bits 23-0 = tokens in 1/16 units** (unsigned 20.4 fixed point: max ≈ 1,048,575 tokens of burst, refill precision 1/16 token). The refill rate is a policy parameter in tokens/s, applied as `elapsedMs x rate x 16 / 1000` with saturation at burst. A background sweeper reads the **policy slot** from the high 16 bits of each key (the fingerprint itself can't be inverted, so the slot is stored alongside it). It looks up that policy's current full-refill window (burst / rate) and clears entries untouched for longer than that, or immediately if the slot no longer maps to a live policy. A 48-bit hash in a 256 Ki table makes false sharing between partition keys negligible (~2⁻³⁰ per probe). Bounded memory, so a flood of unique IPs can't exhaust RAM. Collisions only make limits slightly stricter. Policies per host or route: req/s + burst, keyed by IP / IP+path / header. Returns 429 + `Retry-After`. |
| Request size limits | `IHttpMaxRequestBodySizeFeature` per host/route. Header count/size and URL length are checked cheaply in the gate. Global Kestrel limits act as the outer bound. Built in M7: **As built (M7): request limits** below. |
| Rule sets | A named object (`rs_`): an ordered list of rules, each with path (exact, prefix, glob, regex), method and user-agent conditions and an action (deny, drop, allow). Exact and prefix paths in sorted arrays, a glob as a bit-parallel automaton, user-agent substrings in a `SearchValues<string>` (case-insensitive), a regex as an escape hatch (`RegexOptions.NonBacktracking`, validated when the config is written). Presets ("AI crawlers", "known scanners", "empty UA") are built in. See **As built (M7): rule sets** below. |
| Forward-auth | A named object (`authp_`): an endpoint every request to a host or route that names it is checked against before it is forwarded (a `GET` carrying the client's credentials and the original request in `X-Forwarded-*`). A 2xx lets the request through with the provider's identity headers copied upstream, a 401, 403 or redirect is relayed, anything else fails closed. Allows are remembered in a per-provider, per-configuration cache; the verdict that needs a call is `PolicyAction.Defer`. See **As built (M7): forward-auth** below. |
| Proof of work | A route policy: a browser without a valid `paduraksa_pow` cookie gets a small challenge page whose pure-JS SHA-256 workers find a counter with `Difficulty` leading zero bits; the verify endpoint returns an HMAC-signed, host- and client-bound cookie. `IMacKey`, no server-side table, 0 B on the valid-token path. |
| Robots.txt | Per host: passthrough / managed (preset: disallow all, block AI crawlers, custom text). Served from pre-encoded bytes. |
| Error pages | Per status (and "upstream down" / "blocked" / "rate limited" / "maintenance"). Templates pre-split into byte segments at compile time, with variables (`{status}`, `{requestId}`, `{host}`) written straight into the response `PipeWriter`. **Replacing upstream error bodies** (opt-in per host) is decided at **response-header time** from status + content type. The upstream response is disposed unread, never buffered or drained (so that upstream connection is not reused; see **As built (M8)**). An admin host never replaces its upstream's errors. It's never applied to `101` upgrades, `text/event-stream`, gRPC (`application/grpc*`), or responses whose headers have already been flushed. |
| Maintenance mode | Per host (layered like the rest), a toggle with a status, a page and a bypass named by an access list (**As built (M8): maintenance mode**). |

**As built (M7): the rate-limit engine** (`src/Paduraksa.Proxy/RateLimiting`). `RateLimitTable.TryAcquire(ushort slot, ulong partitionHash, in RateLimitParameters, long nowMs, out int
retryAfterSeconds)` is the hot path: 0 B, lock-free, two `long[]`s of a power-of-two length (256 Ki by default, the class
takes any power of two, `Paduraksa:RateLimiting:TableSize` is validated to 1024-16 Mi). The time is passed in, so tests
drive it; `RateLimitClock.NowMs` is `Stopwatch`'s ms since process start. `RateLimitParameters(double tokensPerSecond,
int burst)` validates itself and stores the rate in integer 1/16-token steps scaled by 1024 (so "one a minute" is a
policy, not a rounding error), plus `FullRefillMs` (burst / rate) for the sweeper. `retryAfterSeconds` is the ceiling of
the wait for one whole token, at least 1. A token count above the current burst is clamped the next time the bucket is
touched, which is how a lowered burst takes effect (§6.3).

**The accepted races** are listed on the table's type, each with its bound: k threads probing for the same absent key
in a full window can each evict a different entry for it (k buckets, at most (k - 1) bursts extra once, all but one never
found again), a token update can land in a slot another thread has just evicted and re-claimed (at most one burst, once), a
thread between another's key claim and its state write sees the previous occupant's state (a full bucket once), and the
sweeper's cleared state (0) is a bucket a request refills from zero. None is unbounded: every touch recomputes the tokens
from the state it read and clamps them to its policy's burst, and after four probes that each lost their slot the request
is let through without a token rather than stalled (one request per four lost probes, at whatever rate window churn
produces them). A clock sample older than the stored time earns no refill and never moves the time back, in a take and
in the sweeper: masked, it would read as 2^40 ms.

`RateLimitPolicySlots` maps a stable identity (a `string`; the compiler passes the policy and host typed IDs' string forms) to its
16-bit slot: `GetOrAdd(identity, nowMs)` on the compiler's cold path (one lock), `IsLive(slot)` and `Parameters` lock-free
for the sweeper. Slot 0 is "no policy"; retirement makes a slot non-live at once but reusable only after a grace period
(one minute, like the metric slots), and the reuse clears its buckets first, so a new policy never reads an old one's
tokens. `Publish` swaps an immutable `RateLimitPolicyParameters` (slot -> parameters) atomically. All 65 535 slots in use
throws with a message that says so.

`RateLimitSweeper` (a `BackgroundService`, every 30 s, `Sweep(nowMs)` public for tests) clears an entry whose slot is no
longer live, or whose last refill is older than its policy's `FullRefillMs`; an entry whose slot has no published
parameters is left alone. It clears with a CAS on the state it read, so a request that touched the entry in between wins
and the entry survives. A live slot's parameters are the ones published at that moment: a sweep across a publish may use
either set, which moves a clear by one pass at most.

`RateLimitPartition` hashes a partition key to 48 bits with FNV-1a and a murmur3 finalizer, keyed by a per-process
`RandomNumberGenerator` seed (a fresh `RateLimitPartition(seed)` exists for tests) and domain-separated for IP, IP+path
and header. IPv4 is keyed whole, IPv6 by its /64 (`IpKey.MaskTo(32, 64)`); the path is the canonical matching view the
gate uses. Constructing two seeds in a test shows the per-process part: the same key hashes differently.

Registered in `AddPaduraksaProxy` (`AddPaduraksaRateLimiting`): the runtime (table, registry, counters), the table, the
registry and the sweeper as singletons, the sweeper as a hosted service. `RateLimitTableBenchmarks` (bench/Paduraksa.Benchmarks) covers
one key, a 64 Ki working set and one contended entry.

**As built (M7): rate-limit integration.** A `RateLimitPolicy` is a top-level named object beside upstreams and profiles:
a typed `RateLimitPolicyId` (`rlp_`), a unique name, notes, `requests` per `second`/`minute`/`hour`, a `burst`, and a key of
`clientIp`, `clientIpAndPath` or `header` (with a header name required exactly then and validated as a token). The
validator judges `requests` and the derived rate against the engine's own bounds (`1/16384` to 1,000,000 tokens a second,
burst 1 to 1,048,575) with `rateLimit.requests.invalid`, `rateLimit.rate.invalid` and `rateLimit.burst.invalid`, so a
document that validates can't make `RateLimitParameters` throw; the header rules are `rateLimit.header.required`,
`.unexpected` and `.invalid`, and the references are `host.rateLimit.unknown` and `route.rateLimit.unknown` (reported where
the value resolves, with the layer named, like HSTS's preload). `HostSettings.RateLimit` is a nested `RateLimitSettings`
record (`enabled`, `policyId`) merged field by field through global defaults → profile → host, and `RouteSettings.RateLimit`
is the same record on top of the host: a route inherits the host's policy, names another, or turns it off with
`enabled: false`, and the reference is kept while off. The built-in switch is on, so naming a policy is the whole job;
naming none is no limit (the built-in default). Canonical JSON orders policies by ID, lowercases the header, drops a
record that sets nothing, and the document API has list/get/create/update/delete, with `config.rate_limit_policy.*` audit
actions; deleting a policy a host or route names is refused by validation, as deleting an upstream in use is.

The policy runs in the ROUTE chain after the route's access policies and before proof of work, so a client over budget
gets 429 rather than a challenge page. The effective policy is the route's own, else the host's. Redirect and Respond hosts
have no routes and are never limited; the admin host's implicit route carries the host's policy. **Gaps:** unknown hosts,
404s, ACME challenges and requests the proof-of-work verify endpoint answers are not limited by this wave; the verify
endpoint is answered before route matching, so there is no route policy to apply, and giving it one needs its own rule.

Each (policy, host) pair owns its buckets: the slot identity is `{policyId}/{hostId}`, built from stable IDs, so two hosts
that name one policy don't share a client's budget, a route that resolves to the host's policy shares the host's buckets,
and a route that names another policy gets that pair's. A compile that will be published calls `GetOrAdd` for every enabled
Proxy or Admin host's pair and puts the slot, parameters and identity in the snapshot's registrations; a draft, a dry run
or a simulation only looks an existing pair's slot up, and a new one compiles with slot 0 (the simulator reports its full
burst), so no compile that never goes live can leak a slot. Only the publisher publishes them: just before a snapshot
goes live it re-asserts each pair's slot (an older publish that retired a pair a newer one re-added must not leave the
live snapshot on a non-live slot), swaps in the parameters for that snapshot's slots and retires the pairs it no longer
has, so a draft or a dry run publishes no parameters and retires nothing, and the sweeper clears a retired pair's buckets.
A slot already rented to another identity is logged and the pair starts on a fresh slot at the next compile, so the two
never share buckets. A rate or burst edit keeps the slot and
therefore the buckets (the new numbers apply on the next touch, and a lowered burst clamps the stored tokens); deleting a
policy, or removing it from a host, retires the pair. `RateLimitRuntime` is the process singleton holding the table, the
registry, the partitioner and `RateLimitStats` (`Allowed` and `Limited`, process-lifetime `Interlocked` counters the
security page will read); `RateLimitPolicySlots` and the table are also registered for the sweeper and for tests.

The partition is the resolved client IP (IPv4 whole, and the hash of IPv6 by its /64), plus the canonical matching path for
`clientIpAndPath` (route matching ignores ASCII case, so the partition folds it too: `/API` and `//api;` spend one
budget), or the header's value. An absent or empty header falls back to the client-IP partition, in the
partitioner's other hash domain, so a header value can never collide with an address. A refusal is 429 with
`Retry-After: <seconds>` from `TryAcquire` and a short plain-text body through the gate's precomputed-response path, so
the host's `Server`, HSTS and `Alt-Svc` apply as on any gate answer. `GET`/`HEAD /robots.txt` is exempt before the clock
and the partition are touched, like the proof-of-work gate's exemption: a crawler reads the file even when the route's
bucket is empty, whether the host serves it or forwards it, and the cheap length and substring check keeps the canonical
match off every other path. The policy precomputes one string per possible
`Retry-After` value, so the allowed and refused paths both allocate 0 B; `GateMiddleware.Decide`'s Kestrel allocation test
measures a rate-limited route on both (the allowed path through a large burst, the refused one through a one-token
bucket), and the direct policy tests cover the verdicts, the bucket scope, the partition kinds and the simulation's peek.

The simulator peeks: `RateLimitTable.TryPeek` computes the same refill and clamp without a CAS, and reports `tokensLeft`
(after the token the request would take) or `retryAfterSeconds`. `RateLimitPolicy.Explain` reports "would allow, N token(s)
left" or "would refuse, retry after S s", the policy's name and the key kind, and a parity test proves two simulations in
a row leave the bucket where they found it. S6's rate-limit part is `RateLimitContinuityTests`: under load, with a publish
every 100 ms that changes unrelated config and the policy's label, the 2xx count stays within burst + rate x elapsed, 429s
keep coming to the end, and a lowered burst applies on the next touch.

**As built (M7): access lists.** An `AccessList` is a top-level named object like a rate-limit policy: a typed `acl_` ID, a
unique name, notes, `ip` (`allow` and `deny` as CIDRs or single addresses, `dropDenied`), `basicAuth` (a `realm`, defaulting to the
list's name, and `users`), `satisfy` (`all`, the default, or `any`) and `passCredentialsUpstream`. `HostSettings.AccessList` and
`RouteSettings.AccessList` are one nested record `{ enabled, accessListId }`, merged field by field through global defaults,
profile, host and route like the rate limit: a route inherits the host's list, names another or turns it off with
`enabled: false`, and the reference is kept while off (built-in `enabled` is on, so naming a list is the whole job). The old inline
`ipAllowList` is no longer part of the contract. Validation codes: `accessList.ip.dropDenied.withoutAddresses`,
`accessList.realm.invalid`, `accessList.users.tooMany` (1000), `accessList.user.username.invalid` and `.duplicate` (1-64 visible
ASCII characters, no `:`, case-sensitive), `accessList.user.password.missing` (a user with neither a password nor a credential),
`accessList.user.password.empty`, `.tooLong` and `.unhashed` (a plain password that reached the validator),
`accessList.user.credentialId.duplicate` (each user has a credential of their own, across the whole document), `host.accessList.unknown` and `route.accessList.unknown`, with an address that doesn't parse reported as `address.invalid`
at its entry (`/accessLists/0/ip/allow/2`). Canonical JSON orders lists by ID, users by name (ordinal) and CIDRs as the parser prints
them, and a list with no rules writes none. Country allow/deny lists sit under `ip` beside `allow` and `deny` (**As built (M7): country rules**).

*Passwords.* A user is *written* with a plain `password` (create and update through the API and the dashboard) and *read* as
`{ username, credentialId }`: the password lives in the vault (§8.2, **As built (M7): password credentials**), the document names its
credential and nothing else, so a caller with `config:read` can no longer attack a verifier offline (the exposure this section used to
state is gone). `AccessListPasswords` (Application) takes the plain password out of the command before it is planned, validated,
stored or audited and leaves the credential's ID in its place: the ID the list already has for that user name, else the one the
write sent, else a new one. The passwords are collected, not set: the pipeline sets them in the vault once validation, the compile and
the guards have passed and the command is not a dry run or a simulation, so a refused or previewed command changes no password,
and a compile that runs first is told what the vault doesn't hold yet (`PendingPasswords`): for a command that will commit, that its
credentials are about to be set (so a new user carries no warning the command is about to resolve); for a dry run or a draft, a handle per
pending password that carries the prepared, already-hashed password for that compile alone (`PrepareAsync`), which the mapping uses in
place of the vault's own, so nothing reaches the vault or its cache until a real commit. The validator refuses a password that is still there (so no write path can store it),
canonical JSON drops the property, and the audit entry holds the action and the ID only. A user written with neither a password nor
a credential keeps the one the list has for that name, which is how an edit avoids asking for every password again; a new user with
neither is `accessList.user.password.missing`. **A password is a change:** the document's users don't differ when only a password
does, so the pipeline reports the list as modified (field `users`) when a command carries one: it commits a revision, moves the
list's version (an editor holding the old one conflicts) and is audited like any change to the list, and the revision records that
the list changed and never the password. The API returns `credentialId` and never `password`; whether the vault holds the password
shows as the compile warning `accessList.user.noPassword`, in the result of the command and in the live warnings, which points at the
user's own entry (`/accessLists/N/basicAuth/users/M`, the position in the canonical document the pipeline compiles) and which the
dashboard's drawer reads, by that pointer, to say "No password is set, so this user can't sign in". **A rollback or an import** brings back the users
a revision named and gives them the passwords the vault holds then, which for a credential deleted since is none (the warning); the
lifecycle (deleted when the live config stops naming it) is in §8.2. **Gaps:** an existing bcrypt `htpasswd` line can't be imported
(PBKDF2 only for now); a dry run or a draft hashes each pending password (one PBKDF2, tens of milliseconds) so it can be simulated.

*Upgrade.* A stored document with the inline list (the live revision, a rollback target, the last-known-good file, an imported
document) is converted when it is read into the current model (`LegacyAccessListUpgrade`, on the JSON, before the typed read): every
layer that set `ipAllowList` gets `accessList: { enabled: true, accessListId }` (an empty list, which lifted what was inherited, becomes
`{ enabled: false }`), and each distinct content (the sorted, canonical addresses and the drop flag) becomes one access list named after
the first place that used it in document order (`global-defaults`, `profile-lan-only`, `host-example-com`,
`route-example-com-admin`, with `-2`, `-3` for a clash) and with an ID taken from a hash of the content, so converting a document twice
gives the same bytes, and the version is 1. At startup, when the live revision needed converting, one revision with the converted
document is written (actor System, a message naming the lists, audit action `config.upgrade`, every host, profile and list counted as
touched so their versions move); if it can't be stored it runs as converted and the next change stores it. A
write that sends `ipAllowList` is refused: the API answers 400 with a sentence that names access lists and `POST /api/v1/accessLists`.
`LegacyAccessListUpgradeTests` run old documents through the real gate and compare outcomes with the old semantics, drop included.

*Upgrade, passwords.* A stored document whose users still carry `passwordHash` (written by the build before passwords moved into the
vault) is converted the same way, on the JSON, before the typed read (`LegacyPasswordUpgrade`): each user gets a `credentialId` taken
from a hash of the list's ID, the user name and the verifier (a user that already names one keeps it), so converting twice gives
the same bytes, and the property goes. The conversion is lossy by design (the verifier does not stay in the document), so **every
place that makes such a document live imports its verifiers first**, into the vault as they are, no re-hash (`ImportAsync`, which
changes nothing for a credential that already has a password): startup (the latest revision, and the last-known-good file wherever
it is used), a rollback to an old revision (the verifiers its document carries are imported before the commit, so the user comes back with the password
they had then) and the head a command builds on (when the startup write below couldn't be stored), but only for a command that will commit: a dry run
or a draft imports nothing (it compiles against handles for those verifiers, like its passwords) and the next real command or start does it.
Reading a revision to show it or diff it imports nothing. At startup, when the live revision needed converting (either conversion), one revision with the converted
document is written as above (its message says what was converted: the inline allow lists and the lists they became, the passwords
moved into the vault, or both), and the vault's credentials nothing names are swept. A write that sends
`passwordHash` is refused: the API answers 400 with a sentence that says to send the plain `password` or leave it out.
`LegacyPasswordUpgradeTests` (the conversion) and `ConfigServiceAccessListTests` (import at startup, stable on a second start, a rollback
to an inline-hash revision, the sweep) hold it.

*Chains.* The mapping builds `AccessListPolicySpec`s. A route's chain carries the whole list; the host chain carries
`AddressOnly` (what the address alone can refuse), or, for a host whose routes name different lists whose address rules can all
refuse, one `AccessListUnionPolicySpec` of them; a host with no routes (Redirect, Respond, Admin) carries the whole list, with its
password deferred on plain HTTP when the host redirects to HTTPS (the redirect is what answers next). A compile warning,
`accessList.insecureCredentials`, says so for a host that asks for a password without forcing HTTPS.

*The gate* (`Paduraksa.Proxy.AccessLists`, 0 B on every synchronous path). Rules: a denied address is refused whatever it sends; a non-empty allow
list admits only its addresses (with `satisfy: all` and users the rest get 403, with `any` they are asked for a password instead); no
users means the address rules alone, no address rules means the password alone. `dropDenied` aborts the connection where the address
decided. `Authorization: Basic` is read from the header string into a `stackalloc` buffer (at most 64 + 1 + 256 decoded bytes, split on
the first colon, UTF-8), the name is compared in constant time with every user's (no early exit), and the password is checked by the vault's verifier
(`IPasswordVerifier`: a cache probe, then PBKDF2 against the credential's verifier, which the gate never sees); a name nobody has,
and a user the vault holds no password for, still pays one PBKDF2 against a dummy verifier, so neither can be told from a wrong password
by timing. A missing or wrong credential is `401` with
`WWW-Authenticate: Basic realm="<realm>", charset="UTF-8"` (precomputed per list, the realm quoted), `Cache-Control: no-store` and the
built-in `401 Unauthorized` page for `Accept: text/html` (plain text `authentication required` otherwise); the 403 is the existing
access-denied page. When the list accepted the credentials, `GateRequestState.StripAuthorization` makes the forwarding transform remove the
`Authorization` header before the upstream sees it, unless the list sets `passCredentialsUpstream`; a list that let an allowed address
through without asking consumed nothing, so a bearer token meant for the upstream still reaches it.

*The verification cache* now lives in the vault (`PasswordVerificationCache`, held by the vault's `PasswordRegistry`; §8.2 says what a
key is made of and why a password change invalidates it). It is a fixed table of two arrays (4096 entries, 64 KiB): a 64-bit key (the
first 8 bytes of SHA-256 over a per-process random key, the credential's stamp, the client address masked as the rate limit masks it
(IPv4 whole, IPv6 by /64) and the credentials as sent) and an expiry. A hit needs no PBKDF2 and **pushes
the expiry to five minutes from the hit**, but only once less than four minutes are left, so a hot entry is written about once a minute (one
lock-free store, 0 B) and an idle user drops out between four and five minutes after the last request, while an active one stays verified;
only a *successful* verification is ever inserted. Writers clear the expiry, store the key and then the new expiry, and readers
read the key, the expiry and the key again and believe it only if both reads agree, so a reader never pairs one entry's key with
another's expiry; a probe of four slots evicts the entry that would lapse first. A refresh stores its expiry with no further check, so it can
land after a writer has replaced the slot's key: that cannot make an unverified key live (a key only enters through `Add`, after PBKDF2), and
leaves a verified key with an expiry off by the skew of two clock reads, so the worst case is an entry living milliseconds longer or being
verified again early. Removing a user or changing their password changes the credential's stamp (or empties the credential), so the
old entries are never found again, with no recompile and nothing to sweep.

*The line for a check* (`AccessListVerificationQueue`). A cache miss no longer runs PBKDF2 inside the synchronous decision, and no longer
answers 429 when the slots are taken. `AccessListPolicy` returns `PolicyDecision.Defer` (`PolicyAction.Defer`; the decision is
`GateAction.AwaitPolicy`, with the policy and its place in the chain in `GateRequestState.DeferredPolicy/Scope/Index`); every other path (an
allowed or denied address, a cache hit, missing or unreadable credentials) is still synchronous and 0 B. `GateMiddleware` then awaits
`IDeferredPolicy.CompleteAsync` and **resumes the same chain right after that policy**: the rest of the host chain, the HTTPS redirect, the
robots.txt and fixed answers, route matching, the route's chain after the list (its rate limit, then proof of work) and the endpoint, by the
same methods `Decide` uses (`DecideAfterHostChain`, `DecideRoute`, which take the first policy to run), so nothing after the list is
duplicated or skipped and a request that waited is limited, challenged and forwarded (with `Authorization` stripped) exactly like one that
didn't. The continuation takes a place in the line, waits for a slot without holding a thread, looks at the cache once more, runs the PBKDF2
and answers 401 or lets the chain go on. The slots are `max(8, ProcessorCount)` (never fewer than 8: on a 2-core box a cap of 2 refused a
page's third credentialed request on a cold cache; over-subscribing only makes each check slower). The line is bounded three ways: **32
places per slot** (256 at the default 8: at tens of milliseconds a check, the last of a full line waits a few seconds, inside the wait cap
whatever the machine's size), **16 places per client** (queued and running together, by the masked address, as the cache keys it; a table of
1024 in-flight counters striped by a keyed hash, 4 KiB, and as many running counts) and a **10 second maximum wait**. A request that can't
enter (line full, or its client already holds its 16) or that waits the maximum is answered 429 with `Retry-After: 1`; one whose client went
away (`RequestAborted`) leaves the line at once, frees its place and is dropped without an answer. Two rules decide who runs next, so a place
is never wasted and nobody is starved: **one client runs at most 2 checks at once** (its other requests wait in the line, and a free slot goes to
the first waiter in arrival order whose client is under that cap, so others overtake them), and **identical credentials never run twice at
once**: each deferred request carries its attempt's key (the vault's opaque name for these credentials from this client, `PasswordCacheProbe.AttemptKey`; 0 for a name nobody has, or a user with no password, which have no identity to share),
and a waiter whose fingerprint is running waits for it, then finds the result in the cache and pays no PBKDF2 (a dozen identical cold requests
cost one check; identical *wrong* passwords are checked one after the other, each its own). The per-client share replaces the old "under
pressure" rule (a client holding two or more was refused only while half the slots ran). The first version of the share was 4 places, which
refused the fifth credentialed request of an ordinary HTTP/2 page burst, the very 429 the line exists to remove; places are cheap (a pooled
waiter), so the share only has to stop one source taking the whole line: a single source holds at most 16 of the 256 places and eats only its
own 429s, while every other client still gets a turn within the wait. The share is a concurrency limit, not a rate. Waiters are pooled
`IValueTaskSource` objects, one timer serves the whole line (arrivals wait the same maximum, so the head is always the next to expire), the
line's one lock is held for a few field writes and a scan of the line, never while a waiter is completed, a granted waiter's continuation goes
to the thread pool (never run inline on the thread that freed the slot, which would stack one PBKDF2 on another), and the pool hands a waiter
back only after its cancellation registration is disposed. **Allocation** (Release, 64-bit, `GC.GetAllocatedBytes`, on the distro runtime 10.0.14 and on Microsoft's 10.0.403 from
`scripts/test-ci-like.sh`, which both measured the same): taking and giving back a free slot is 0 B (`AllocAssert`), as is a refusal at the
door; a miss that finds a slot free allocates nothing beyond the 401 a missing credential gets (1137 B against 1144 B in a test context, which
is the answer's own cost); a miss that waits adds about 550 B (the boxed continuations, the cancellation registration, the thread-pool work
item; measured once, not asserted by a test); PBKDF2 itself is 0 B on both runtimes. The allocation tests of the synchronous paths (`GateAllocationTests`, `AccessListPolicyTests`)
include the deferring decision. Not yet pinned by a test: a queued waiter cancelled while an identical-credential twin waits
behind it (safe by construction: the twin is served once the in-flight check exits). `AccessListStats` (allowed, address refused, 401, cache hits, verifications, **queued, queue-full
refusals, timeouts**, and busy as the sum of the two refusals) are process counters the security overview shows. **Gap:** a spray spread
over many addresses (a botnet, or many /64s) can still fill the 256 places, and then first-time logins on every list get 429 until it
ends; nothing throttles failed attempts per address over time. A credential already in the cache still gets through, since a hit needs no
slot.

*Simulator.* The policy's trace step names the list, the address verdict (denied, allowed with the matching entry, not in the allow
list, unrestricted), the credentials verdict (not asked, missing, unreadable, unknown user, wrong password, no password (the user's credential holds none in the vault), ok, deferred, busy; a busy answer in the trace means the line was full or the wait ran out) and how
`satisfy` combined them, as a sentence and as facts (`accessList`, `address`, `credentials`, `satisfy`); the probe's `Authorization`
header is read like a real one, and neither it nor a typed name is ever echoed. A simulation neither reads nor refreshes the cache, moves
no counter and takes no verification slot or place in the line (it runs its PBKDF2 inline through `Verify(..., remember: false)`, as it always did), so it can't warm the gate for a later request or exhaust the verifier;
`AccessListSimulationTests` compare the simulator's outcome with the real gate's for every satisfy mode, address and credential.

*API and dashboard.* `/api/v1/accessLists` (list, get, create, replace, delete) has the same ETag, dry-run and 422 rules as the other
objects; deleting a list a host, profile, default or route names is refused by validation, with `config.access_list.*` audit actions.
(`CommandErrors` also learned the rate-limit and access-list lists, which an invalid object command needs to point its errors.) The
dashboard has Security > Access lists (a list of rules and users of each, one in use offers no delete) and an editor drawer: the allowed and
denied addresses one per line with an entry count, the drop switch, the realm, and users with a password box that stays empty to keep the
stored password and a Generate button that fills 20 random letters and digits and shows them once with a warning; the 'address and
password' / 'address or password' choice appears once there is a user. Inline validation runs the real validator with a credential ID in place of a typed password, so nothing reaches the vault from the
circuit. The review and the history show a list's passwords as the realm and the users by name
("alice (new password)", "alice (password changed)", "bob (removed)"; a revision whose users are the same says "a password was changed"), and the
'Combine' choice as "Address and password" / "Address or password", never a password, a credential ID or a wire value. The settings of a host, profile, the defaults and a route pick a list
by name (inherit, none, or a list), with a staged "New access list..." entry exactly like the rate-limit picker.

**As built (M7): country rules.** Geo in M7 is `CF-IPCountry` only. There is no local country database in this wave, so a country exists only
behind Cloudflare (Proxy or Tunnel mode) and is unknown everywhere else.

*Resolving.* `ClientIpResolver.Resolve` reads `CF-IPCountry` in the branch where it reads `CF-Connecting-IP`: a trusted peer in a
Cloudflare mode (`ClientIpResolution.CloudflareHeadersTrusted`), and from no one else, so a client's forged header, a trusted generic proxy's
pass-through and a peer outside the edge ranges all give an unknown country. The result is `ClientIpResolution.Country`, a `CountryCode`
(`Paduraksa.Core.Geo`): a compact slot (0 unknown, 1 Tor, then the 676 letter pairs), so a request carries no string and the parse is 0 B. Two
ASCII letters in either case are a country (assigned or not: an unassigned pair parses and matches no rule); `T1` is **Tor**, kept apart from
unknown because "block Tor" is a common wish; `XX`, a repeated header, a missing one and anything that isn't two letters are **unknown**. The
state exposes it as `GateRequestState.ClientCountry`. The unused `ClientInfo.CountryIndex` placeholder is gone: the country is per request,
and `ClientInfo` is per connection.

*The model.* `AccessListIp.Countries` is `{ allow, deny }`, codes being the ISO 3166-1 alpha-2 assignments (`Paduraksa.Core.Geo.Countries`,
249 codes with their English names, ASCII), `XK` (Kosovo, which Cloudflare reports and ISO doesn't assign), `T1` (Tor) and `XX` (unknown).
Canonical JSON writes codes upper-case, sorted, once, drops a list that says nothing and writes no `countries` at all for none, so a document
without them keeps its bytes (the golden hash is unchanged). Validation: `accessList.countries.code.invalid` (not in the list, reported at the
entry, `/accessLists/N/ip/countries/allow/M`), `.duplicate` (in either case) and `.conflict` (a code in both lists), and
`accessList.ip.dropDenied.withoutAddresses` counts countries as something to refuse. Warning `accessList.countries.noSource` (at the host's
`/settings/accessList`) when an enabled host uses a list with country rules while the trusted-proxy mode isn't `CloudflareProxy` or
`CloudflareTunnel`: every country is then unknown, so an allow list refuses everyone and a deny list nobody. The compile reports a code it
doesn't know too. A list compiles to two `CountrySet`s (678 bits in 11 words), and a check is one bit test.

*Semantics.* The country is part of the **address side**. Deny wins: a denied address or country is refused whatever the request sends,
a password included (and `dropDenied` aborts there). A non-empty allow list admits only its members, so **an unknown country is refused when
the list has an allow list, unless `XX` is in it** (and Tor only if `T1` is). When a list has both an address allow list and a country allow
list, a request must be in **both** (decided here: each non-empty allow list is a restriction, and the stricter reading fails closed; "this
address, or that country" isn't expressible). `AddressVerdictOf` gives one verdict (denied, allowed, not in the allow list, unrestricted) that
the policy, the host chain's address-only stage, the union of lists and `RefusesByAddress` (the compile-time "can the address alone refuse"
decision) all use. `satisfy` then combines it with the password as before: `all` asks for the password of an allowed visitor and refuses the
rest with 403; `any` lets an allowed visitor in without one and **asks the rest, a visitor from a country outside the allow list included, for a
password**, which lets them in; a denied country is still refused. The drawer says so under "How they combine".

*Gate and simulator.* 0 B on every path (`AccessListCountryTests` measures allowed, refused by country, unknown, Tor dropped, a forged header
and the password paths through `GateMiddleware.Decide`). `AccessListPolicy`'s trace step adds the facts `country` (`DE`, `T1`, `XX`) and
`countryRule` (`allowed`, `notInAllowList`, `denied`, `unrestricted`) when the list has country rules, and its sentence names the country and
the rule: "Germany (DE) is an allowed country", "France (FR) is not in the allowed countries (2)", "Tor exit nodes (T1) is a denied country".
`SimulationRequest.country` (a code, `T1` or `XX`) is added as `CF-IPCountry` to a request that arrives through Cloudflare
(`GateProbe.Country`); on a direct or generic-proxy arrival it is refused (`simulation.request.country`/`.conflicting`) like any field that
would change nothing, and a probe that carries the header itself on those arrivals shows the gate ignoring it (the client step says
`country: ignored` beside `countryHeader`). `AccessListCountrySimulationTests` run the real gate and the simulator over every arrival
(Cloudflare, a forged header, none), country (including lower case, garbage, `T1`, `XX`, missing) and satisfy mode.

*Dashboard.* The access-list drawer has a Countries section with two pickers (`CountryPicker`: a search by name or code in an autocomplete,
