chips for what is held; "Germany (DE)", Tor exit nodes and Unknown country first, a country the other side holds isn't offered). Its copy says
the country comes from Cloudflare, a one-line hint shows while the global mode isn't a Cloudflare one, and the list page's summary counts
countries ("only 2 countries", "blocks 3 countries"). The security overview's list cell says "countries only" or "addresses and countries only"
for a list with no users, and the simulator's Advanced panel takes a country for the Cloudflare arrivals. A browser test drives the picker
and keeps screenshots at 1280 and 390 px.

*Gaps.* (1) **No local country database.** A request not behind Cloudflare, or whose Cloudflare header is missing, is unknown. The seam
for DB-IP Lite or GeoLite2: the country is produced in one place, `ClientIpResolver.Resolve`, and carried as `ClientIpResolution.Country`;
a database would fill it when the header didn't (a lookup of the resolved client address in a snapshot-held, sorted `UInt128` range array
with a `CountryCode` per range, binary-searched like `CidrSet`, built by a background job and compiled into the snapshot), so rules, gate,
trace and dashboard stay as they are, and `accessList.countries.noSource` would stop applying. (2) The trusted-proxy mode is global, so a
host can't use Cloudflare for countries while another doesn't. (3) The country isn't in the access log or the metrics yet. (4) When Cloudflare sends no `CF-IPCountry` (its setting for it is off), every visitor is unknown, so an allow list refuses everyone; the trace's `country: XX` is the only sign, and the config can't tell.

**As built (M7): rule sets.** A `RuleSet` is a top-level named object like an access list: a typed `rs_` ID, a unique name, notes and
`rules`, an ordered list. A rule has `match` and an `action`. `match` states any combination of `path` (`kind` `exact`, `prefix`,
`glob` or `regex`, and a `pattern`), `methods` (a set, upper case) and `userAgent` (`contains`, case-insensitive substrings, and `empty`,
which also takes a missing or blank user agent); a rule needs at least one, because one with none would match every request, and
every condition it states has to hold. `action` is `deny` (403 and the built-in page), `drop` (abort, no answer) or `allow`. The first
rule a request matches decides, so an allow carves an exception out of the denies after it (it ends the set, and the rest of the
chain still runs); a request no rule matches goes on. `HostSettings.RuleSet` and `RouteSettings.RuleSet` are one nested record
`{ enabled, ruleSetId }` merged field by field through defaults, profile, host and route like the access list's. **One set per
layer** was enough: a second concern is more rules in the same set, in the order the operator wants, which one list a request
walks can express and two lists could not (an allow that must beat a deny sits in the same list as the deny). What it costs is that
a preset and an operator's own rules can't be stacked without copying the preset into a set of one's own, which "Copy to edit" does.
Validation codes: `ruleSet.id.reserved`, `ruleSet.rules.tooMany` (200), `ruleSet.rule.empty`, `ruleSet.rule.path.invalid`, `.encoded`
(write a path decoded), `.notCanonical` (the route-path rules, so a pattern that no request's canonical path could equal is refused),
`ruleSet.rule.path.glob.invalid` (at most 126 characters and wildcards, ASCII), `ruleSet.rule.path.regex.invalid` (the regex is built
at validation, NonBacktracking, and the parse error is the message), `ruleSet.rule.methods.invalid` (a token, at most 16),
`ruleSet.rule.userAgent.invalid` (printable ASCII, at most 100 of 128 characters), `host.ruleSet.unknown` and `route.ruleSet.unknown`.
A regex that backtracks catastrophically on a backtracking engine (`^(a+)+$`) is **accepted**: it runs in time linear in the path, so
it costs no more than a plain one. Canonical JSON orders sets by ID, writes methods upper case and sorted, and user-agent substrings
trimmed, unique ignoring case and sorted; a stored document without `ruleSets` reads as none.

*Presets.* `RuleSetPresets` (Contracts) has three read-only sets with reserved IDs, never part of a document (`ruleSet.id.reserved`
refuses one that tries), named in a setting like any set and compiled into a snapshot only when something names them: **Block AI
crawlers** (the user agents of `RobotsText.AiCrawlers`, one rule, so the robots.txt mode and the rule set can never disagree about
who an AI crawler is), **Block known scanners** and **Block empty user agent**. The scanner list is deliberately short and
conservative, one name each whose default user agent contains it and no browser's does: `sqlmap` (SQL-injection tool), `nikto`
(web-server scanner), `nmap` (its scripting engine sends "Nmap Scripting Engine"), `masscan` and `zgrab` (internet-wide port and
banner scans) and `nuclei` (template scanner). It removes noise, not attackers: a real one changes the header. The empty-agent
preset refuses scripts and some health checks. Like the crawler list they are refreshed with each release. "Copy to edit" makes a new
set (a fresh ID, "name (copy)") holding the same rules.

*Where it sits.* Before the access list that asks for passwords, so a refused request costs no PBKDF2 (the deferred check is the one
expensive thing in the chain) and no rate-limit token. Precisely: the mapping inserts the policy immediately **before the first
`AccessListPolicySpec` with `Stage: Full` whose list has users**, which is the whole policy, address checks included: on a route whose own list has
users, the list's address rules run *after* the set. What always runs before the set is the host chain's refusal by address, the `AddressOnly` policy or the
union of the routes' lists (cheap, and a denied address must still be dropped, not answered with a rule's 403). With no such list the set goes after
the access policies there are (a `Full` list without users is address rules only). So in a route chain: set, then the list (addresses, then
passwords), then rate limit, then proof of work; in a host chain: the address-only check or union, then the set (a host with no routes carries the whole list there, and the set goes before it when it has users).
Where it goes follows the access list's rule: when every route
resolves to the same set, or the host has no routes (Redirect, Respond, Admin), it is in the **host chain** and also guards the 404 for a
path no route takes and the HTTPS redirect (a denied client gets 403, not a 301 that maps the host); otherwise each route carries its own set
(a deny can't be lifted by a union as an address allow list can). `GET` and `HEAD /robots.txt` are never ruled on, like the rate limit and the
proof-of-work gate: RFC 9309 reads a 4xx on that file as "no restrictions", so refusing it would tell a crawler it may go anywhere. The unknown-host
answer and ACME challenges are answered before any chain and are not ruled on; the proof-of-work verify endpoint is answered after the host chain, so
only a set that sits there rules on it.

*The matcher* (`Paduraksa.Proxy.RuleSets`). Rules read the **canonical matching path** routes match on (`RoutePath`: `\`, `%2F`, `%5C`,
repeated separators, `;` parameters, dot segments and trailing dots resolved, case ignored), so no encoding trick or redundant spelling dodges
a rule; it is computed only for a set whose rules read the path, on the stack or in a pooled buffer. Exact and prefix patterns sit in sorted arrays
searched ignoring case, a prefix at each segment boundary of the request with the route's own segment-bound semantics (`/admin` takes `/admin` and
`/admin/x`, not `/administrator`); rules with no path, a glob or a regex are scanned in order, and the lowest index among everything that matched
wins, so the document's order is the precedence whichever index found a match first. A **glob** (`*` within a segment, `**` across segments, and a
`/**/` segment for none or more, so `/**/.env` takes `/.env`) is simulated as an automaton with one bit of state per token (a `UInt128`, so at most
126 tokens): each character is read once, nothing backtracks, a path can't be shaped to make it slow (a test runs the classic quadratic case over 8,000
characters), and it allocates nothing. User-agent substrings are one `SearchValues<string>` per rule with `OrdinalIgnoreCase`, over every `User-Agent`
header line (a request that sends two can't hide a name in the one the upstream reads). Methods compare ignoring case (over-matching only makes a deny
stricter). Inside a rule the cheap conditions run first and the path scan last.

*Allocation.* Every verdict is 0 B (the deny answer is precomputed, a non-canonical path is rewritten on the stack or in a pooled array): proved
through `GateMiddleware.Decide` for a pass, an allow, a prefix and exact deny, a glob drop, a method deny, a user-agent deny, a blank user agent, a path
canonicalised on the stack and in a pooled buffer and robots.txt, and on Kestrel (`RuleSetsAllocateNothingOnKestrel`). A **regex** is the one thing
that may not be: the NonBacktracking engine builds its automaton lazily, as it meets characters it hasn't seen, so a first match over a new shape can
allocate once. Measured here, a regex rule matched repeated path shapes at 0 B once warmed (`ARegexRuleMatchesWithoutAllocatingOncePathShapesAreKnown`,
on the distro runtime and on Microsoft's 10.0.403); a set without a regex never pays for it, and a rule runs its regex last, after the
conditions that could rule it out. The regex has a 50 ms match timeout, which is decided by the rule's action so it fails closed: a deny or a drop counts it as a match, an allow as no
match, and `RuleSetCounters.Timeouts` counts it (shown under the set on the overview once above 0), though the engine is linear in the path and a request
line is at most 8 KiB, so it should never happen.

*Counters and the overview.* `RuleSetCounters` (denied, dropped, allowed, regex timeouts) are process-lifetime `Interlocked` longs per set, kept by `RuleSetRuntime`
and carried by the compiled set, so they survive a publish and a simulation counts nothing. The security overview adds a **Rules** column: the set
a host applies (a document set links to its page, a preset to the list that shows the presets), what it has refused or dropped, and the routes that
apply another set, a preset or none. There is no global rule tile: the counts are per set, and a set that is deleted takes its history with it.

*Simulator.* The policy's step names the set, the rule (counted from 1, as the dashboard numbers them), what matched (`path starts with /admin, method
POST`, `user agent contains 'sqlmap'`, `no user agent`) and the action, as a sentence and as facts (`ruleSet`, `rule`, `matched`, `action`), and
says when none matched or that robots.txt is exempt. `RuleSetSimulationTests` put a matrix of paths (non-canonical spellings included), methods and user
agents through the simulator and the real gate and compare the outcomes. The lock-out guard now sends the caller's own `User-Agent` in its probe
(`AdminRequestFacts.UserAgent`), so a rule set that refuses the caller on a proxied admin host is caught and one that refuses only other agents isn't.

*API and dashboard.* `/api/v1/ruleSets` (list, get, create, replace, delete) has the same ETag, dry-run and 422 rules as the other objects, with
`config.rule_set.*` audit actions; `GET /ruleSets/presets` lists the presets and `GET /ruleSets/{id}` also reads one; a delete of a set a host still
resolves to is refused. The dashboard has Security > **Rules**: the sets with what their rules do and who applies them, the presets read-only under them
with "Copy to edit" (`/rules/new?copy=...`), and a drawer whose rules read "When [path] [methods] [user agent] then [action]" with add, move and remove
and the real validator's messages at the fields (a regex's parse error, a rule with no condition). The settings of a host, profile, the defaults and a
route pick a set by name in the Access section (inherit, none, a set, a preset, or a staged "New rule set..." that goes in with the host in one
revision). The review shows a set's rules as numbered sentences. **Gaps:** nothing warns about a rule no request can reach (shadowed by an earlier one);
no condition on the query string, headers or client address (geo and access lists decide by address); the `**` glob has no single-character wildcard;
a set can't be applied to the unknown-host answer or an ACME challenge.

**As built (M7): forward-auth.** An `AuthProvider` is a top-level named object like an access list or a rule set: a typed `authp_` ID, a unique name,
notes and a `forwardAuth` block (a second kind, native OIDC, is a later sibling property, and exactly one is set). The block holds the endpoint `url`,
a `preset` (`authentik`, `authelia`, `oauth2Proxy`; none is a custom endpoint), `timeoutSeconds` (1-30, built-in 5), `cacheSeconds` (0-300, built-in 10, 0 remembers
nothing), `cacheScope` (`request` or `host`), `requestHeaders` (extra client headers sent to the endpoint), `responseHeaders` (copied upstream; a trailing `*` is a prefix) and a
`signIn` (`url`, `returnParameter`). A field left unset takes the preset's value, and `ForwardAuthPresets.Resolve` (Contracts) is the one place that says which,
for the validator, the mapping and the dashboard. `HostSettings.AuthProvider` and `RouteSettings.AuthProvider` are one nested record `{ enabled, authProviderId }` merged
field by field through global defaults, profile, host and route like the access list's: a route inherits the host's provider, names another or turns sign-in off
(the route that serves the provider's own pages has to), the reference survives while it is off, and naming a provider is enough (the built-in switch is on). **Naming does not imply on**: a layer that
names a provider above a layer that switched sign-in off (the defaults or a profile says `enabled: false`, the host names one and leaves the switch alone) merges to off, like an access list's, which is also how "off, reference kept" keeps working. That would fail open without a word through the API,
so the mapping warns (`authProvider.namedButOff`) **at the layer that named it**: the host's `/settings/authProvider`, the profile's (`/profiles/{i}/settings/authProvider`, once however many hosts use the profile) or the route's, which names one under a host with sign-in off; naming below the switch, or in the same layer, is deliberate and silent.
Validation codes: `authProvider.forwardAuth.required`, `authProvider.url.invalid` (absolute http or https, no credentials, no fragment, at most 512 characters),
`authProvider.timeoutSeconds.invalid`, `authProvider.cacheSeconds.invalid`, `authProvider.headers.tooMany` (32), `authProvider.header.invalid` (an RFC 9110 token the proxy doesn't own:
`Host`, `Content-Length`, the hop-by-hop set, `Forwarded`, `X-Forwarded-*`, `X-Real-IP` and `X-Request-Id` are refused; a prefix only in the response list and at least three characters), `authProvider.header.credential` (a response name that is, or as a prefix would own, `Authorization`, `Cookie` or `Proxy-Authorization`: see *The answer*), `authProvider.signIn.invalid`
(a path on the host or an absolute http(s) URL, never a `//host` reference), `authProvider.signIn.returnParameter.invalid`, `host.authProvider.unknown` and `route.authProvider.unknown`; the compiler
checks every value again (`ForwardAuthUrls`, `ForwardAuthHeaderNames` in Core are shared). Canonical JSON orders providers by ID, trims and sorts the header lists unique ignoring case, writes a preset
of custom as none, and keeps an empty response list (it says "none" where an absent one says "the preset's"); a stored document without `authProviders` reads as none, and the golden hashes changed
only by the empty list.

*The presets.* Each is what its project documents, and nothing more. **authentik** (the proxy outpost): `/outpost.goauthentik.io/auth/traefik` appended to a bare address, `X-authentik-*`
copied upstream, a decision per application (`host` scope); the outpost's own `/outpost.goauthentik.io/` has to be routed on the protected host with sign-in off for the callback.
**Authelia**: `/api/authz/forward-auth`, `Remote-User`, `Remote-Groups`, `Remote-Name` and `Remote-Email`, a decision per path and method (`request` scope), because its access-control
rules match resources, methods and networks. **oauth2-proxy**: `/oauth2/auth` (it answers 202 or 401), `X-Auth-Request-User`, `-Email`, `-Groups` and `-Preferred-Username`, `host` scope, and its bare 401 is turned
into a redirect of a browser navigation to `/oauth2/start?rd=<the original URL>` (the nginx `error_page 401` pattern; oauth2-proxy has to be routed at `/oauth2/` on the host, sign-in off). `X-Auth-Request-Access-Token`
is not copied by default: an operator who wants the token passed upstream adds the name. **`Authorization` can't be named** (`--set-authorization-header` sends the ID token back in an `Authorization` response header, which nginx's `auth_request_set` copies over the client's): a provider's identity headers are *added to* the request and *removed from the client's* on every route of the host (see *The answer*), so listing `Authorization` would strip the client's bearer token on the routes with sign-in off, and `Cookie` would break sign-in everywhere. Replacing it only on the routes behind the provider was the alternative, and it was not built: it would make "the client's `Authorization` and cookies are forwarded as they were" untrue on guarded routes and would cache a token for the allow's lifetime. `X-Auth-Request-Access-Token` does the job for an upstream that can read it from another header, and the preset copies no credential header.

*The call* (`Paduraksa.Proxy.ForwardAuth`). A `GET` with no body, as Traefik's forward-auth and nginx's `auth_request` send, so the original method is
`X-Forwarded-Method` and a request body is never read or sent. It carries the standard set of client headers (`Cookie`, `Authorization`, `Proxy-Authorization`, `User-Agent`, `Accept`, `Accept-Language`, `X-Requested-With`)
and the provider's own additions, **and no other client header**: an allow-list rather than a deny-list, so hop-by-hop framing, a client's own `X-Forwarded-*` and whatever the client invented never reach the provider, and what is
sent is what the operator can read in the editor. The original request is told in `X-Forwarded-Method`, `-Proto` (the trusted scheme, the one the redirect uses), `-Host` (the `Host` as received), `-Uri` (the **raw** target as the client sent it, so
two targets that decode alike stay different) and `-For` (the resolved client, exactly one address). `Host` is the endpoint's. The client is built per provider configuration (`SocketsHttpHandler`: no redirects, cookies or environment proxy, at most 64
connections; connections idle 30 s, live 5 min; the response decompressed) and made on first use, so compiling opens nothing. TLS to an `https` endpoint is the platform's default validation: there is no "accept any certificate"
option, so an internal endpoint is `http` on a private network or has a certificate the host trusts. The endpoint is the operator's own address; nothing a client sends chooses it, so there is no request forgery to a client-chosen URL.

*The answer.* 2xx lets the request on. The provider's response headers (exact names, or any with a listed prefix) are collected (at most 64; a value the upstream can't carry is dropped, see below), and on the upstream request (`ForwardedHeadersTransform`) every
client header that matches one of the provider's names is **removed first** and the provider's are added, so on a protected route an identity header can only come from the provider, also for an allow with no headers.
**Stripping is a property of the host, not of the route**: `CompiledHost.IdentityProviders` is every provider the host's routes ask (the host's own, resolved, and any a route names), and the transform removes the identity headers of all of them on **every** route of the host,
a route with sign-in off (the provider's own `/outpost.goauthentik.io/` or `/oauth2/` pages) included, so a client's forged `Remote-User` can't reach an upstream through the one route that skips the check. **`_` and `-` are one character** when deciding what a provider owns, for exact names and prefixes, ignoring case:
a CGI or WSGI upstream folds `Remote_User` and `Remote-User` into the same variable, so the client's `Remote_Groups` or `X_authentik_username` is removed as the provider's `Remote-Groups` or `X-authentik-*` would be. A host with no provider asks none and strips none. The client's `Authorization` and cookies are forwarded as they were, since the
upstream may want them: no provider can own them (`authProvider.header.credential`), so stripping a provider's identity headers never touches them. A 401, 403 or 407 and a 301, 302, 303, 307 or 308 are relayed: the status, the body (at most 64 KiB, the content type, `Cache-Control: no-store`), `Location`, every `Set-Cookie`, `WWW-Authenticate` and `Proxy-Authenticate`, and nothing
else from the provider's response. A redirect without a `Location` is a failure. A 401 to a browser navigation (a `GET` or `HEAD` that accepts `text/html` and isn't a script's XHR) goes to the configured sign-in URL instead, with the original address percent-encoded in the return parameter, as the one `Location` (the provider's own is replaced, never added to). **A request for the sign-in page itself is never redirected**: when
the request path (in the canonical matching view, ignoring case and a trailing `/`) is the sign-in path (a relative URL, or an absolute one on the request's own host) the provider's 401 is relayed as it is, because a page that is behind the same provider would otherwise send the browser to itself with `rd=` nested in `rd=` each time.
The mapping warns when it could happen (`authProvider.signInUnreachable`): a provider's sign-in path on a host (a relative URL, or an absolute one whose host is one of the host's names, a wildcard name covering its subdomains) whose first route to take it, in matching order, is behind a provider too, or none takes it. A route takes a request by its match kind and path alone, there is no method or header matcher in the model, so the route the warning finds is the one a GET and a POST, with any headers, both reach.
Every relayed header (`Location`, `Set-Cookie`, `WWW-Authenticate`, `Proxy-Authenticate`) must be printable ASCII or a tab: a control character would split the message and a byte Kestrel can't write would make it throw on the way out, which would be a 500, so a provider that sends one is a **502** and an error.
**Identity values may be any text but control characters.** An authentik user's `X-authentik-name` can hold "José" or Japanese, and refusing it would answer 502 to that user on every uncached request. .NET 10's `SocketsHttpHandler` writes request header values as ASCII unless it has a `RequestHeaderEncodingSelector` (the default throws `Request headers must contain only ASCII characters`, which YARP answers as a 502) and reads response header values as Latin-1 unless it has a `ResponseHeaderEncodingSelector`, so a UTF-8 name would arrive as one character per byte. Both ends are UTF-8, as Kestrel reads a client's headers (UTF-8, and a 400 for bytes that aren't): the provider's client decodes a response as UTF-8, and the forwarder's handler (`PaduraksaForwarderHttpClientFactory.ApplyDefaults`) writes every upstream header value as UTF-8, so the name reaches the upstream as the bytes the provider sent (authentik and Traefik pass them as they are). A UTF-8 encoding of non-ASCII text never contains a CR, LF or NUL byte, so it can't split the message. A value with a C0 control character other than tab, DEL, U+FFFD (the decoder's mark for bytes that weren't UTF-8, which can't be passed on as sent) or a lone surrogate **costs that one header, not the request**: it is left off, the request is let on, the client's own copy is removed with the rest (the provider owns the name, so it is never what the upstream reads instead), and a warning names the provider and the header, never the value, at most once a minute per provider. Only a call that fails outright, or a provider that sends more than 64 owned headers, is a 502.
Any exception the call raises that nobody listed (a handler's own, a value the transport refuses) is the same: counted as an error, answered 502, never an allow (only cancellation, which is the client going away, and out-of-memory escape).
**Failure closes:** any other status, an endpoint that can't be reached or answers with something the proxy can't forward, a refusal body over the limit, all answer **502**; a timeout is **504** (a client that
goes away while the call runs is dropped without an answer and counts as neither); a request that finds 64 calls to the provider already running is **503** with `Retry-After: 1`. Each has a built-in page. A failing endpoint is logged once a minute per provider.
`robots.txt` is **not** exempt, unlike the rate limit and the proof-of-work gate: auth protects what the upstream serves, and a managed robots.txt is answered before the route chain anyway.

*The decision cache.* `ForwardAuthDecisionCache` is a fixed table of 4,096 immutable entries per provider (a probe of four slots; a writer replaces the same key, else an empty or lapsed slot, else the one that expires first), so memory is bounded and
nothing is swept. Only an **allow** is stored: a refusal is the answer to a request with no session, and remembering it would keep a person at the login page after they signed in. The key is the first 64 bits of SHA-256 over a per-process random
secret and the length-framed fields: the grain, the client address (Authelia matches networks and sessions can be bound to one), the scheme, the host, the whole `Cookie`, `Authorization` and `Proxy-Authorization` headers (a session cookie's name is the operator's
and an answer may depend on more than one) and, at request grain, the method and the raw target. Also in the key: **every other request header the call carries**: the standard hints (`User-Agent`, `Accept`, `Accept-Language`, `X-Requested-With`) and the provider's own `requestHeaders` (a custom provider that decides on `X-Api-Key`), so two requests that differ in anything the provider is sent never share an entry (v0.25), whole and length-framed by its number of lines, so an absent header and an empty one differ and a keyless client
from the same address is never served another's allow: the key covers everything the call sends (the three credential headers, the standard hints and the provider's own additions, the scheme, the host and the client). Until v0.25 the standard hints (`Accept`, `Accept-Language`, `User-Agent`, `X-Requested-With`) and the scheme were left out on the reasoning that they only change how a refusal is shown; a provider is free to decide on them (a rule that refuses scripted clients, one that wants HTTPS), so an allow remembered for one would have been served to another. The price is more entries and calls for a browser whose `Accept` differs between a page and its images.
**The grain is a property of the entry, not of the request.** `request` scope stores every allow at request grain. `host` scope stores an allow at **host grain** (no method, no target) **only when the provider vouched for an identity, that is when at least one identity header was copied from its 2xx**, and at request grain otherwise; a lookup probes the host-grain key, then the request-grain key, still 0 B. What a request carries can't decide it: any visitor can send an analytics cookie or the junk in a header the provider reads, so "carries credentials" said nothing about whether the allow was for someone, and an anonymous allow for authentik's "Unauthenticated Paths" `/public` would have been served for `/admin` too. Such an allow now stands only for the same address, host, method, target and credentials. **A provider configured with no identity headers therefore always caches at request grain, even under `host` scope**: it has no way to say an allow names someone, so each path costs a call once per time-to-live. A `host` scope **assumes the provider decides per identity, not per path** for the allows that name someone (authentik, oauth2-proxy decide per application);
it is the wrong grain for one that decides per path (Authelia, and a custom endpoint, which defaults to `request`) and the operator picks it knowing that. A provider whose anonymous allows also carry owned headers (a prefix such as `X-authentik-*` copies whatever the provider sends under it) would have them taken for an identity: the response list should name only headers that identify a user. The time-to-live is **the delay a revoked session keeps working**, and the dashboard says so. It is SHA-256 over the secret and the fields, not an HMAC and not a vault key:
the banned-symbol rule keeps keyed MACs in the vault, a key that no one ever sees needs none (as the password verification cache is named), and a restart empties the caches anyway, so the "forward-auth HMAC key" §8.2 once listed is not needed.
Per provider **and per configuration**: `ForwardAuthRuntime.Bind` returns the process's state when the provider's hash (the endpoint, timeouts, scope, both header lists and the sign-in, **not the name**) is the same, so a publish that changes something else
keeps the cache and the pooled connections, and a changed provider starts empty. **A compile changes no state in the process**, whether or not it goes live (a failed publish, a dry run, a draft and a simulation all leave the live states alone; the one trace is the counters entry `Bind` adds for a provider ID the process hasn't seen, which every later snapshot shares): the snapshot carries the states its routes run on (`RuntimeSnapshot.AuthProviderStates`) and the publisher's `Swap` calls `ForwardAuthRuntime.Publish` with them
just before the store swap, as it does the rate-limit registrations, which makes them the live ones and retires the states a snapshot replaced or dropped. A retired state stays reachable from the snapshots that still hold it, so its `HttpClient` is **disposed after the timeout plus ten seconds and only once no call is running** (a timer on the runtime's clock, which waits and tries again while a call is in flight);
a call that begins later than that fails closed (502). The state's in-flight counter is also its lifecycle gate: a call takes a place by compare-and-swap and refuses a negative value, and the disposal moves 0 to a closed marker in one step, so no call can start on a client that is being disposed and none can create the lazily made client after it; a retired state is held by the runtime until it is disposed, so its timer is never collected before it fires. **A retired state can come back.** Two commits can overlap: C1 changes a provider and is still publishing when C2 compiles, reverts the change and so binds the state the process still has; C1 then retires it and C2 publishes it. `Publish` first makes every state of the snapshot live again (the disposal cancelled, a tick already on its way ignored by a generation number), and `Bind` never hands out a state that is retired or disposed. A state that was already disposed (the publish waited longer than the grace period) gets a new client and keeps its cache, rather than being replaced by a new object, because every snapshot that holds it, this one included, runs on that object.

*The gate* (Defer). `ForwardAuthPolicy.Evaluate` hashes the key in a stack buffer (a pooled array past 2 KiB) and reads the cache: a **remembered allow is synchronous and 0 B**, as is the verdict that finds none and returns `PolicyAction.Defer`; the gate then awaits
`IDeferredPolicy.CompleteAsync`, the one call, and resumes the chain after the policy (the rate limit has already run, proof of work follows), exactly as an access list's password check does. The call allocates what an outbound HTTP request allocates, which is accepted
and the reason allows are remembered; no number is claimed for it. Proved 0 B through `GateMiddleware.Decide` for a remembered allow, the deferring verdict and a request too large for the stack buffer, in both scopes, for a hit at either grain under `host` scope and for the miss that probes both, and on Kestrel
(`AForwardAuthRouteAllocatesNothingOnKestrelOnceTheAllowIsRemembered`). A call is bounded three ways: 64 at once per provider (a lock-free counter, so no queue), the provider's timeout, and the client's own abort.

*Counters.* `ForwardAuthCounters` (allowed, denied, redirected, cache hits, calls, errors, timeouts, busy) are process-lifetime `Interlocked` longs per provider, kept by the runtime and carried by the compiled provider, so they survive a publish and a simulation counts nothing.

*Simulator.* The policy's step names the provider and its endpoint and says "would call provider '...'" (GET with the original request in `X-Forwarded-*`; a 2xx lets it on, a 401, 403 or redirect is relayed, anything else refuses) or, when the cache
holds an allow for these credentials, how many seconds are left, as a sentence and as facts (`provider`, `endpoint`, `outcome` `wouldCall` or `cached`, `cacheScope`, `cacheSeconds`). It **makes no call** and shows the request going on, because the answer isn't known; the
PLAN's "an explicit opt-in may make the real call" was not built, since a simulator that sends a probe's credentials to an authentication service is a side effect of its own. It reads the cache and nothing else: no call, no counter, no write or refresh. `ForwardAuthSimulationTests`
check that against a fake endpoint that sees nothing, and compare the simulator's outcome with the real gate's for requests the endpoint allows.

*API, overview and dashboard.* `/api/v1/authProviders` (list, get, create, replace, delete) has the same ETag, dry-run and 422 rules as the other objects, with `config.auth_provider.*` audit actions and an OpenAPI entry that says what a preset fills in; a delete of a provider a host still resolves to is refused.
The security overview adds a **Sign-in** column (the provider a host asks, what it has let through and refused, its failed calls once there are some, and the routes that ask another provider or none) and a card of the providers' summed counters. The dashboard has Security > **Sign-in**:
the providers with their endpoint and who asks them, and a drawer whose preset says what it fills in and whose empty fields say what they will use (the wait, the time an allow is remembered, the resolved endpoint, the preset's headers, which can be replaced by the person's own list, even an empty one). The settings of a host, profile, the
defaults and a route pick a provider by name in the Access section (inherit, none, a provider, or a staged "New auth provider..." that goes in with the host in one revision). `AuthProvidersEndToEndTests` drives all of it in a real browser against a real host and a fake endpoint, including a real request through the public listener, at 1280 and 390 px in dark and light.

*Deviations from the design, and gaps.* **Route chain only, no host chain**: a forward-auth check is never made before the HTTPS redirect, a 404 or a Redirect/Respond answer, so a host that answers plain HTTP without forcing HTTPS is warned (`authProvider.insecureSession`) and the admin host is
never behind a provider (`authProvider.ignored`; a trusted-header admin login behind forward-auth, §10.1, is not built). **Keyed by the credentials, the address and the request, not the "session-cookie hash"** the plan named: the whole `Cookie` header is hashed, so an unrelated cookie that changes costs a call, which is the safe side of the trade. **No single flight and no queue**: identical cold requests each make their own
call (they differ in target for `request` scope anyway), and a request that finds the 64 places taken is refused 503 rather than waiting; a dead endpoint is bounded by the 64-call cap and the timeout, not by coalescing. **Allows only are cached**, an anonymous one at request grain only, so a flood of distinct unauthenticated requests reaches the endpoint at up to the cap; a rate limit before it (the order is fixed) is the defence. A refusal's `Set-Cookie`
is relayed but an allow's is not (**no `Set-Cookie` relay on a 2xx**): a provider that refreshes its session cookie on an allow doesn't refresh it through the proxy. `OPTIONS` preflights carry no credentials and are asked like any request (a CORS API behind a provider needs the preflight's route with sign-in off). No per-provider TLS trust setting (no pinning, no "accept any"). A provider's own
logout or `/oauth2/sign_out` is just a route. Native OIDC remains later (§10.2).

**As built (M7): managed robots.txt.** `HostSettings.Robots` (`Mode`, `CustomText`; built-in passthrough) inherits
through the layers and has no route override: a host has one file, and every route shares it. `CompiledHost.RobotsTxt`
is the ready `PrecomputedResponse` (`text/plain; charset=utf-8`, `Cache-Control: max-age=3600`), or null for
passthrough; the two presets are encoded once for the process (`src/Paduraksa.Proxy/Gate/RobotsResponses.cs`) and a
custom text once per host at compile time, with `\r\n` and `\r` normalised to `\n` and a final newline ensured (the
stored document keeps what the operator typed). The gate answers `GET`/`HEAD /robots.txt` after the host policy chain
and the HTTPS redirect, before a Redirect/Respond host's fixed answer and before route matching, so an IP allow list or
a redirect still comes first, those hosts can carry a file, and the route chain (rate limit, proof of work) never sees
it; any other method falls through as an ordinary request. The path is the canonical matching view routes use, so a
redundant spelling is the same file, and a query string does not matter. The answer is 0 B per request
(`RobotsTxtGateTests` measures it). The AI crawler list lives once in `Paduraksa.Core.Model.RobotsText`, shared by the
proxy and the dashboard copy, and goes stale as crawlers appear: it is refreshed with each Paduraksa release rather
than by an operator who would have to notice. The simulator reports a `robotsTxt` step either way: the mode served, or
that the path goes on to the upstream.

**As built (M7): the built-in error pages.** `HtmlPageTemplate` (`src/Paduraksa.Proxy/Pages`) builds the shared layout
once (doctype, meta, the brand style, the logo, a centred card and the footer) and pre-splits it into byte segments
around its named slots; writing it copies the segments into the response body pipe and HTML-encodes each slot with
`HtmlEncoder`'s span overloads, at 0 B for the template's own work. The CSP is hashed once from the inline style and
script (`default-src 'none'`, the style hash, no script on a page without one, `form-action 'none'` unless the page has
a form, `frame-ancestors 'none'`), with `Referrer-Policy: no-referrer` and `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`. The
proof-of-work challenge page now uses it too, with the challenge, return target and difficulty as slots, so a challenge
request no longer builds a per-request body array. `ErrorPages` holds the built-in set: 403 (IP access, no JavaScript,
failed check), 404, 413 (also a body over a host's limit), 414, 421, 429, 431, 502, 503 (no healthy destination, and the proof-of-work key unavailable) and 504,
each with a muted status line, an h1, one sentence and the request ID in monospace, all ASCII and with no script or
external resource. A request whose `Accept` includes `text/html` gets the page; anything else gets the same plain text
as before, `HEAD` gets headers only, and `Cache-Control: no-store` and `Retry-After` are unchanged. YARP's upstream
errors (502, 503, 504) get the page from the response-start hook that already re-applies the gate's headers: it runs
after the forwarder, only does work when `IForwarderErrorFeature` is set, and adds no wrapper, continuation or
allocation to the successful path. Custom pages, maintenance mode and replacing an upstream's own error bodies are
**As built (M8)** below.

**As built (M8): custom error pages.** `HostSettings.ErrorPages` is a layered setting: built-in, global defaults, profile, host, each
**entry replaced whole** by a layer that sets it (a heading from one layer and a document from another make no page), and a status no
layer sets is served from its built-in page. The entries are the statuses `ErrorPages` has built-ins for (403, 404, 413, 414, 421, 429,
431, 502, 503, 504) and the maintenance page; the document keys them by status (`"errorPages": {"404": {...}, "maintenance": {...}}`),
the contract names them (`NotFound`, ...). An entry is a **message** (a heading of up to 120 characters and a sentence of up to 500, on
one line, rendered into the built-in layout, so it keeps the strict hashed CSP and the request-ID line) or a **document** (the operator's
whole HTML, up to 64 KiB of UTF-8, with no control characters). *Variables* are `{status}`, `{statusText}`, `{requestId}`, `{host}` and
`{cfRay}`; `ErrorPageText` (Core) is the one place the syntax lives for the validator, the compiler and the dashboard. A variable is `{name}`
with ASCII letters and digits; any other brace is literal, so CSS (`a{color:red}`) needs no escaping, and `{{` writes a literal `{`. An unknown
name is `errorPages.variable.unknown` and the message lists the allowed ones. Codes are flat (`errorPages.heading.required`, `.tooLong`,
`.invalid`, `.unexpected`; the same for `text` and `html`; `errorPages.html.required`), with the status in the path
(`/settings/errorPages/404/heading`): the repo's code convention wants lower-camel segments, so `errorPages.404.*` would have failed it.
The compiler checks the same rules as a backstop. *Compile time*: each page is split into byte segments around its variables, the literal
parts HTML-encoded (a message) or kept as written (a document), and the status and its name folded into the bytes, since a page belongs to
one status; `{requestId}`, `{host}` and `{cfRay}` are the only runtime slots. *Serving*: the answers with a built-in page (one 404, one 429...)
are shared by every host, so the host's own page is looked up **when the answer is written** (`ErrorPageSelection`), from the host the request
matched, or the global defaults' pages for an unknown host; the writer then streams the segments into the response `PipeWriter`, HTML-encoding
the three values (0 B per refusal; the host is cut at 253 characters and the ray at 64; the ray is empty unless Cloudflare's headers are trusted,
as in the access log; everything non-ASCII becomes an entity). The Accept/HEAD/plain-text behaviour, `no-store` and `Retry-After` are unchanged.
A proof-of-work step's own refusals (`BrowserRequired`, `CheckFailed`, `PayloadTooLarge`, `CheckUnavailable`) and the 401 of a basic-auth
list are not customizable: they say what the visitor must do. *The document's CSP* is `default-src 'none'; style-src 'unsafe-inline'; img-src
data:; font-src data:; base-uri 'none'; form-action 'none'; frame-ancestors 'none'` (decided: the operator's own inline style and `data:` images and
fonts work, nothing runs or loads from elsewhere; no script at all, because the page is shown to anyone who can make the host fail), with
`Referrer-Policy: no-referrer` and `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`. *Preview*: `ErrorPagePreviews` (Proxy) compiles and writes a page with the
proxy's own compiler and writer and sample values; `ErrorPagePreviewer` (Application) validates by the saving rules and wraps it; the dashboard
(directly) and `POST /api/v1/error-pages/preview` return the document and its CSP, shown in an iframe with `sandbox=""` and `srcdoc`. A test holds
a preview and a served page to the same bytes (`CustomErrorPagesGateTests.APreviewIsTheBytesAVisitorGets`). *Simulator*: the outcome says which page
a browser gets (`errorPage`: built-in or custom, its mode and the layer it came from).

**As built (M8): maintenance mode.** `HostSettings.Maintenance` merges field by field: `enabled` (built-in off), `status` (503, 200, 500, 502, 504,
403, 404 or 410; built-in 503), `retryAfterSeconds` (0 sends none; up to a week) and `bypassAccessListId`. The page is the host's `maintenance` entry,
else a built-in message ("Down for maintenance"), compiled for the status. *Where it sits*: after the host's policy chain, the request limits, the
HTTPS redirect and the managed `robots.txt`; before a Redirect/Respond host's fixed answer, the proof-of-work verify endpoint and route matching, so
it covers every host kind but admin. Decided: after the host chain because a refused client must still be refused and learn nothing about an
outage; after the redirect because plain HTTP is sent to HTTPS first; **after robots.txt** because a crawler that reads a managed file should not
be told the whole site is gone (a passed-through robots.txt reaches the upstream's place in the chain and gets the 503, which is what a crawler
should read during an outage). *The bypass* is the named list's **address side**: a request skips the page when the list's allow rules (addresses,
and countries in a Cloudflare mode) admit it and its deny rules don't refuse it; a list with only deny rules, or a request outside every allow rule,
is served the page, and a password is never asked for there (one way to express IP rules: no inline list). It is precomputed per snapshot
(`CompiledMaintenance` on the host, null when off) and costs 0 B (`MaintenanceGateTests`). An **admin host never serves it**: the mapping drops it
with `maintenance.adminIgnored` (a layer that turns it on for every host can't lock the dashboard out), and the compiler refuses to carry it. A
bypass list with no allow rule warns (`maintenance.bypass.noAllowRules`); an unknown list is `host.maintenance.bypassUnknown`. The simulator traces a
`Maintenance` step for every request of a matched host: skipped (off), passed (bypassed, naming the list) or stopped.

**As built (M8): replacing an upstream's error bodies.** `ErrorPages.ReplaceUpstream` (`off`, `html`, `any`; built-in off) is decided at response-header
time by a YARP response transform (`ReplaceUpstreamErrorTransform`, after the host's response-header transform): the status has a page (the list above),
and the content type is `text/html` (mode `html`) or anything (`any`). It answers through the same writer (the host's page or the built-in one; plain text
for a client that doesn't ask for HTML), removes the headers that describe the dropped body (`Content-Length`, `Content-Encoding`, `ETag`, `Last-Modified`,
...), keeps the status, cookies, `Retry-After` and the gate's own headers, and sets `SuppressResponseBody`, so YARP disposes the upstream response unread:
nothing is buffered or drained (`ReplaceUpstreamErrorTests.TheUpstreamsBodyIsDiscardedUnread...` has an upstream that would send 2 GiB and sees it cut off).
It never applies to an upgrade request, `text/event-stream`, `application/grpc*` or a response that has started, however the host is set. The successful
path pays one integer comparison and no wrapper, continuation or allocation (`ReplaceUpstreamErrorAllocationTests`). Decided: `any` also replaces an API's
JSON errors, which is why it is a separate, explicit choice. YARP's own 502/503/504 (no upstream response at all) were already answered by the response-start
hook and now use the host's page too. An admin host never replaces its upstream's errors (the admin app answers API clients with
JSON problems): the mapping turns it off there with `errorPages.replaceUpstream.adminIgnored`, as it does for maintenance. A disposed response
closes that upstream connection, so an error storm with replacement on costs a new upstream connection per error; draining a small declared body
to keep the connection is open.

**M8: left out and open.** Per-route pages; localisation; pages for the admin app; statuses beyond the list (500, 400, 401: a basic-auth 401 carries
`WWW-Authenticate`, and 500/400 are an upstream's more than the gate's); the security overview's per-host maintenance column (the hosts list shows a
host in maintenance and has the wrench).

**As built (M7): request limits.** `HostSettings.RequestLimits` (`maxBodySize`, `maxHeaderCount`, `maxHeaderBytes`,
`maxUrlLength`) merges field by field through defaults, profile and host, and `RouteSettings.RequestLimits` overrides the
**body size only** (a route is not known when the headers and the target are judged; the validator refuses the other three on
a route, `requestLimits.route.*Unexpected`). Sizes are bytes in the document and typed with a unit in the dashboard.
`maxBodySize` 0 is "no limit", said outright; otherwise 1 byte to 1 TiB. The other bounds, in `RequestLimitBounds` (Core), are
what the validator (`requestLimits.<field>.invalid`), the compiler's backstop, the dashboard and the listeners share:

| Field | Built-in | Bounds |
|---|---|---|
| `maxBodySize` | 100 MiB | 0 (none), or 1 B to 1 TiB |
| `maxHeaderCount` | 100 | 8 to 128 lines |
| `maxHeaderBytes` | 32 KiB | 1 KiB to 64 KiB |
| `maxUrlLength` | 8 KiB | 256 B to 16 KiB |

*The built-ins.* The header and target limits are Kestrel's old defaults, so a host that sets none behaves as before. The body
is the one change: the public listener had no limit at all (the owner's call until this setting existed); 100 MiB is three
times Kestrel's own 30 MB, which breaks photo, document and package uploads, while no limit lets one request fill a disk. A
host that wants no limit sets 0 (in the global defaults for all of them), and one that takes bigger uploads on one path sets
its route.

*Kestrel's outer bound.* Kestrel's defaults are 8 KiB for the request line, 32 KiB for the headers together, 100 header lines,
32 KiB for one field on HTTP/2 and HTTP/3 and 30 MB for a body (the listeners set that to null). They would refuse what a host
with a higher limit admits, so `RequestLimitsGate.ConfigureTransport` sets the outer bound from the same constants, each with
4 KiB of headroom so the gate answers before the transport can: a request line of 16 KiB of target plus the method and protocol
(a method token has no length of its own), headers of 64 KiB plus the longest target plus headroom (Kestrel sums names and values,
and HTTP/2 and HTTP/3 count the pseudo-headers, so the target counts twice), a single field as large as all of them (one big cookie
over HTTP/2 must not be reset by the server when HTTP/1.1 takes it), and 128 lines. The hosts' built-ins sit below it, so raising
the bound costs a default host nothing but the scan it already pays. The body limit stays null in Kestrel; the gate puts the host's
on every request.

*Where the gate checks.* Body limit: put on the request (`IHttpMaxRequestBodySizeFeature`, before anything reads the body) when
the host is found, so even an answer that leaves the body unread (a redirect, a refusal) can't make the server drain past the
limit, and again when the matched route has its own. Target and headers: after the host's policy chain, before the HTTPS
redirect. Declared body: after the route's chain (a rate limit or a challenge answers first) and before the endpoint is set. All
three answer from precomputed pages (`ErrorPages`: 413, 414, 431, HTML for a browser, text otherwise) with the host's response
headers like any gate answer. The target is the raw request target in UTF-8 wire bytes; the headers are counted as lines (a header
sent twice is two) and bytes of names plus values. A body without a declared length (chunked, or HTTP/2 and HTTP/3 without one) is
Kestrel's to cut off at the limit: it throws mid-read, which YARP reports as 413 to the client when the upstream hasn't answered yet
(what the tests see: their upstream answers after reading the body); a response that has already started can't change its status, so
the client sees a reset stream (Kestrel's behaviour, not exercised here). Checked: HTTP/1.1 and HTTP/2 in `RequestLimitsEndToEndTests` (and
through `PaduraksaHost` in `PublicRequestLimitsTests`: under the limit forwards, over it is refused, and the upstream never
receives the refused body, or more than the limit of a cut-off one), HTTP/3 in the Docker QUIC run (`PublicHttp3Tests.RequestLimitsHoldOverHttp3`).

*Kestrel's quirks, found by the tests.* On HTTP/1.1 a chunked body counts its chunk framing against the limit, so a chunked
upload of exactly the limit is refused. When the gate or Kestrel answers while the client is still sending, the server closes the
connection with data unread, and a client may see the reset before the 413: a client that sends `Expect: 100-continue` gets the
refusal first, since Kestrel sends the final status instead of `100`. A cut-off body logs a Warning from YARP's forwarder (the
whole `BadHttpRequestException`), once per refused upload.

*0 B.* The checks allocate nothing on every path, on Kestrel (`GateAllocationTests.RequestLimitsAllocateNothingOnKestrel`:
allowed, with a body, and each refusal, on HTTP/1.1 and HTTP/2) and on `DefaultHttpContext` (`RequestLimitsGateTests.TheChecksAllocateNothing`).
Enumerating Kestrel's request headers through `IHeaderDictionary` boxes the enumerator, so the scan copies them into an array with
`ICollection.CopyTo`, which can't be a `stackalloc` (it holds strings). The arrays come from a small pool of slots taken with
`Interlocked.Exchange` and returned cleared: stocked by the first requests, then allocation-free whichever thread runs the gate (a
`[ThreadStatic]` array cost 4 KB on every new thread's first request, which the allocation tests caught). Kestrel also keeps `Content-Length` as a number and writes a new string for
it on every copy (32 B on every request with a body), so the check takes the header out for the copy, counts it by hand (14 bytes
of name and its digits) and puts it back in a `finally`. No bytes are summed once the target or the line count has decided.

*Simulator.* `GateStepKind.RequestLimits` (contract `requestLimits`, "Request limits"), reported twice: the target and headers
("35 headers, limit 30: 431", with the sizes and the answer as facts) and, for a request that has a body (a declared length, or
`Transfer-Encoding`), the body ("body 12 MiB declared, limit 10 MiB: 413", with where the limit comes from, host or route; a
chunked body says the server cuts it off). A simulated request declares a body with a `Content-Length` header, which must be one
number (`simulation.request.headers`). `GateReason.RequestLimits` is the outcome's reason (a fixed answer). `SimulatorParityTests`
sweeps every limit on both sides and both routes.

*Dashboard.* A **Request limits** section in the host settings (global defaults, profiles, hosts) with four rows, and "Body
size on this route" in a route's settings. `SizeField` is the reusable size control: inherit (naming the layer below's size),
"No limit" where 0 means that, presets, or a number with a unit (KiB, MiB, GiB) and the bytes that follow; a size no unit holds
exactly (an API value of 1500 bytes) shows in KiB with decimals. `CountField` is the header lines' (empty inherits). The summary
phrase reads "Request limits: body up to 10 MiB, 30 header lines, ...".

*Not built.* The body limit isn't put on a request that is answered before a host is found (an ACME challenge, an unknown host's
default answer), where the server drains its unread body up to Kestrel's own limit, which is none. A per-route header or target limit (judged before a route is known). A counter or security-overview column for
refused requests (the overview's host table is already six columns wide; the access log shows the 413, 414 and 431). A
limit on an upgraded (WebSocket) connection, which Kestrel doesn't apply a body limit to. The proof-of-work verify endpoint
keeps its own 2 KiB.

### 7.4 Observability (no locks on the hot path)
- **Counters:** `long[]` indexed by `metricSlot * K + metric` (stable slots, §6.3; never the snapshot's host index), striped per core (cache-line padded) to avoid contention. Summed only when read.
- **Latency:** fixed log-linear histogram buckets (≈ 1 µs → 60 s, ~120 buckets), updated with `Interlocked.Increment`. Two histograms are kept: total time and upstream time, so the proxy's own overhead is visible.
- **Exposed via** `System.Diagnostics.Metrics` (`ObservableCounter`/`ObservableGauge` callbacks read the arrays), so OpenTelemetry and Prometheus `/metrics` (admin port) work out of the box.
- **Dashboard live data:** a 1 s sampler writes into in-memory ring buffers (last ~1 h). A background job persists 1-minute rollups to SQLite **keyed by typed ID** (`HostId`, `UpstreamId`, ...), with configurable retention.
- **Upstream health:** YARP active (HTTP probe, interval, path, expected status) and passive (failure-rate) health checks. State changes go to the audit log + notifications and appear as dashboard status dots.
- **Access log and error log (M6):** the hot path fills a fixed-size ring buffer of structs referencing existing strings (no copies); a background writer formats plaintext lines to `<data dir>/logs/access.log` (one per request, per-host on/off), and the process's own Warning-and-above logs go to `<data dir>/logs/error.log`. Files rotate by size and daily, rotated files are gzip-compressed in the background, and retention is by count, age and total size. A full ring drops lines and counts the drops; it never blocks a request. The same drain feeds the dashboard's **live tail** (M9, **As built (M9): the live tail** below).
- **Request ID:** `X-Request-Id` (honour incoming from trusted proxies or generate one), shown on error pages so users can report it.

**As built (M6): the log files.** `<data dir>/logs/` holds two logs, each with a live file (`access.log`, `error.log`) and any number of rotated ones (`error-20261109T120000Z.log`, gzip-compressed to `....log.gz`). The names, their parsing and the "is this one of ours?" test are `Paduraksa.Core.Logging.LogFileNames`, shared by the writer and the reader so a name that isn't one of them is never opened. `RotatingLogFile` (`Paduraksa.Server/Logging`) is the writer: every call site formats a whole line and offers it to a bounded queue (8192 lines, dropped and counted when full), so nothing on the request path waits for a disk. One background thread appends to a buffered `FileStream` (append, `FileShare.Read`, 0600; the directory is 0700, since the access log holds client IPs), flushes once a second, and rotates when the next line would pass `Logs:MaxFileSize` or the UTC date has changed: the live file is renamed aside (a `-2`, `-3`, ... suffix when two rotations land in the same second, checked and then renamed without overwriting) and a second thread gzips it (to a `.gz.tmp`, renamed into place, the `.log` deleted only afterwards) and trims the directory. Retention deletes rotated files oldest first until each log holds at most `Logs:RetainedFiles`, none is older than `Logs:MaxAge`, and the total is at most `Logs:MaxTotalSize`; the live file is never a candidate. At startup the same thread removes stale `*.log.gz.tmp`, compresses rotated files a crash left uncompressed, and trims; a process that never logs creates no directory. An I/O failure is counted (`Failures`), reported to the console at most once a minute, and retried: the file is closed at once, so the next line opens a fresh handle (a full disk, or a directory that vanished and came back), and a rotation that could not rename keeps appending until a later one can. No log call ever throws; one `BestEffort` helper holds the general suppression of "a logger's own failure must not propagate" and reports through `Failed`; the filtered catches that tell an expected failure apart (a file that can't be opened or read, the compressor's temp file) keep their own, and report the same way. `Flush()` (and disposal, after the hosts have stopped) writes out and flushes what is buffered; a `Flush` racing with the shutdown returns instead of waiting on a writer that has gone. `Flush()` wakes the idle writer at once, by offering the one item in the queue that is not a line (recognised by identity, never written): before that it set a flag and waited for the writer's own one-second poll to time out, so every flush (and therefore every host stop, two writers per host) could wait up to that long. A full queue refuses the wake-up item like any line and the flag still ends the flush at the next poll, so a request is never lost. `SharedServices` builds one `ServerLogFiles`, registers the error log's provider in the one logger factory, and exposes both writers, so the access-log branch only has to feed `WriteLine(ReadOnlySpan<char>)`. The error log's provider (`ErrorLogProvider`) keeps every entry at or above `Paduraksa:Logs:ErrorLevel` (default Warning) through a filter rule for its own provider type, so `Logging:LogLevel` still decides what the console shows and nothing else; entries render `2026-11-09T12:00:00.000Z warn: Category[12] => app=core: message`, the scopes included (which is where the role scope's `app` comes from) and an exception's text and stack indented on the lines below. Entries from `Paduraksa.Server.Logging` are skipped, so the log files' own reports can't loop back into them. Options are validated at startup (1 KiB to 16 GiB for the size, 1 to 10 000 files, 1 minute to 10 years, 1 MiB to 1 TiB), and `Logs:Directory` is empty (meaning `<data dir>/logs`) or resolves against the data directory. The CLI commands (`vault`, `setup-token`, `admin`, `doctor`) build no shared services and so create no log files; only a `serve` does, and even then only once something is logged. The setup-token banner's Warning entry keeps its parity with the console (the one documented exception, PLAN §2); nothing else new reaches either log.

**As built (after M9): a client going away is not an error.** Kestrel logs a client that vanished mid-write (a phone off the network, a laptop asleep, a NAT entry gone) at Error with a full stack, twice per connection: when the HTTP/2 GOAWAY can't be flushed and when the TLS stream's dispose flushes again. Production saw a few an hour (`SSL_ERROR_SYSCALL` over `ETIMEDOUT`), and they buried real errors in `error.log`. `PeerGoneLogFilter` (`Paduraksa.Server.Logging`), applied in `RoleLogger` before the level check, writes such an entry at Debug as one line ("... The client went away: <cause>") with no stack, and counts it (`paduraksa_connection_errors_total{kind="peer_gone"}`), even with Debug off. It is narrow on purpose: only Kestrel's categories at Warning or above, and only an exception chain of `IOException` wrappers ending in a reset, aborted, timed-out, shut-down or unreachable socket, Kestrel's `ConnectionResetException`/`ConnectionAbortedException`, or OpenSSL's `SSL_ERROR_SYSCALL` (error 5, the socket under TLS failing; the runtime's type is internal, so it is matched by name). Any other exception, from Kestrel or from Paduraksa's own code, keeps its level and its stack.

**As built (M6, `m6-access-log`: request ID, access-log pipeline, formatter, redaction, and `access.log`).** The drain writes through an `IAccessLogSink` (`void WriteLine(ReadOnlySpan<char>)`, `void Flush()`); the registered one is `AccessLogFileSink` (`Paduraksa.Server.Logging`), a thin adapter over `ServerLogFiles.Access`, so the lines land in `<data dir>/logs/access.log` and rotate with it (the writer owns the newline; the formatter produces none, and the line is passed through unchanged). The writer is built before the sink and disposed after every host has stopped, so the drain's last flush and the writer's buffer both reach the disk before the file closes; a test stops a host right after a request and reads the line from the file. A test app swaps in its own sink (or `NullAccessLogSink`) through `SharedServices.Builder.Replace`.
- **Request ID.** `Paduraksa.Proxy.Logging.RequestIds`: the header is `X-Request-Id`, a value is accepted when it is one header line of 1 to 128 visible ASCII characters (0x21-0x7E), and it is honoured only from a peer the snapshot's trusted-proxy policy trusts - the same `ClientIpResolution.PeerTrusted` decision that makes `X-Forwarded-For` or `CF-Connecting-IP` count; from anyone else, and from a trusted peer who sends nothing usable, it is replaced. A generated ID is 22 characters: an 8-character per-process prefix from `RandomNumberGenerator` and 13 base32 characters of a per-process 64-bit counter (`Interlocked`), so two processes behind one log don't collide either. The gate chooses it in `Decide` as a string reference (adopted) or a counter (generated) at **0 B**, and `GateMiddleware.InvokeAsync` materialises the text once: **one string per request is the only allocation a request accepts**, because an HTTP header value can only be a `string`. The response header, the upstream header and the access log all share that one string; `GateRequestState.RequestIdText()`, `HasClientRequestId` and the state itself are what M8's error pages will read.
- **Where the ID travels** is per host: `HostSettings.RequestIdPropagation` (`upstreamAndResponse` - the default - `upstreamOnly`, `responseOnly`, `off`), resolved through built-in defaults → global defaults → profile → host, compiled to `CompiledHost.RequestIdPropagation`, and `RuntimeSnapshot.RequestIdPropagation` for an unknown host. When it isn't sent upstream, an incoming `X-Request-Id` is **removed** rather than passed through (also from a trusted peer); when it is, ours always overwrites. On the response, ours replaces whatever the upstream set, or the upstream's is stripped when the setting says it isn't sent. The access log records the ID in every mode. The dashboard offers it in the same three layers as the TLS fields (`HostSettingsFields`).
- **The ring.** `AccessLogRing` (`Paduraksa.Proxy.Logging`) is Vyukov's bounded multi-producer/single-consumer queue over `AccessLogEntry` structs: a power-of-two slot array, one sequence number per slot, a CAS on the tail to claim a position and a release store of the slot's sequence to publish it, so a producer can never overwrite what the consumer is reading. Full → the line is dropped and `Interlocked.Increment`ed into a drop counter; no producer ever waits, and a producer stalled past a whole lap drops its line and counts it even if the ring has room again by the time it runs. A slot is padded to three 64-byte cache lines (192 bytes: the sequence number, the entry and padding), so neighbouring slots never share one and producers writing different slots don't ping-pong a line between cores; the default 16 384 slots are about 3 MiB. `Paduraksa:AccessLog:RingSize` must be a power of two from 1 024 to 1 048 576.
- **The entry** references only strings the request already holds: the resolved client address (the gate's per-connection cached text), `Host` as received, method, path, raw query, protocol, status, the response's `Content-Length`, the total and upstream durations, YARP's destination address, the request ID, `CF-Ray` (only when `CloudflareHeadersTrusted`, so a client can't forge it), referer and user agent. It never holds the `HttpContext`, a pooled object, a cookie or `Authorization`. Recording is `AccessLogMiddleware`, the public pipeline's outermost middleware: it acquires the gate's pooled state first (the gate then reuses it), stores the ring and the request's start timestamp there, registers a static completion callback on `HttpResponse.OnCompleted` whose only state is the `HttpContext`, and returns `next(context)`. Kestrel keeps the callback stack per connection (or pooled H2/H3 stream) and clears it between requests, so there is no closure and registration is 0 B in steady state; gate refusals, the HTTPS redirect, Redirect/Respond answers, YARP forwards and exceptions all get a line (in-process Kestrel tests keep the gate, the callback and the reapply hook at 0 B with logging on). The total duration is from the top of the pipeline to the end of the response. The status is what the client got, as Kestrel has settled it by the time the callback runs: an unhandled exception before anything was sent is 500, a connection that ended without a response is 499, and a request body Kestrel rejected mid-read is the 4xx Kestrel answers with (it settles that status only after the callback, so the callback reads it from `IBadRequestExceptionFeature`). The callback then clears the start timestamp, so the gate's skipped reset stays request-scoped. A request the gate dropped without a response is recorded as no status at all, written `-` - an aborted connection has no status, and the 200 a response object would still report is not invented. A request that never reaches the gate (a test app's `WithoutGate`) has no snapshot and logs under the default rule, with no invented request ID.
- **Bytes** are the response's `Content-Length` when it declares one and `-` otherwise: Kestrel doesn't expose what it wrote, and wrapping the response body to count it would cost an allocation and a copy per request. **Upstream duration** comes from YARP's own transforms (`AccessLogTransform`: a `Stopwatch` timestamp in the first request transform, the elapsed time and `IReverseProxyFeature.ProxiedDestination` in the response transform), which is the cheapest source that covers the forward and the upstream's answer. Load balancing picks the destination just before the request transforms run, so that one step sits outside the measured window; a forward that ends without a response (YARP's own 502) is measured up to the failure, in the middleware, from the same timestamp, and still names the destination YARP had chosen. `-` when nothing was forwarded at all (an answered request).
- **The line** (`AccessLogFormatter`, off the request path, into a reused 8 KiB buffer): `time client "host" "METHOD path[?query] protocol" status bytes total upstream "upstream" "request-id" "cf-ray" "referer" "user-agent"`, 13 space-separated fields. Time is ISO-8601 UTC with milliseconds; durations are milliseconds with three decimals (microsecond resolution); missing values are a bare `-`, the status included when the gate dropped the request. Inside a quoted field `"` becomes `\"`, `\` becomes `\\` and a C0 control, 0x7F or a C1 control (NEL among them) becomes `\xNN` and U+2028 and U+2029 become `\u2028` and `\u2029`, so a hostile path, user agent or referer can't forge or break a line; non-ASCII stays readable (the sink writes UTF-8). Every field has an output cap (path 1 024, query 1 024, request target 2 048, user agent and referer 512, host 255, upstream 256, request ID 128, `CF-Ray` 64), and a capped field ends with `...`. The query's sensitive parameters (case-insensitive `token`, `access_token`, `refresh_token`, `id_token`, `code`, `password`, `passwd`, `pwd`, `secret`, `client_secret`, `key`, `api_key`, `apikey`, `signature`, `sig`, `auth`, `session`, replaceable with `Paduraksa:AccessLog:SensitiveQueryKeys`) become `name=REDACTED` - including a bare `?token`; names are matched as written, not percent-decoded; `Paduraksa:AccessLog:LogQueryString=false` leaves the query out entirely. Cookies and `Authorization` are never part of an entry. The sink owns the newline: `WriteLine` receives one line without a trailing `\n`.
- **The drain.** `AccessLogDrainService`, a hosted service of the core host (the one reader), drains every 200 ms and on shutdown (then flushes the sink and so the file), formats each entry into its one buffer and hands it over. A sink that throws is reported at Warning (at most once a minute) and the line skipped, and a whole pass that throws (a formatter bug) is reported the same way so the next pass carries on: a faulted hosted service would stop the core host. Dropped lines are reported the same way with the count since the last report. `SharedServices` creates the one ring, the formatter's options and the sink (the process's `AccessLogFileSink`) for all three hosts; `SharedServices.Builder.Replace` is how a test swaps it.
- **Options** (`Paduraksa:AccessLog`): `RingSize`, `SensitiveQueryKeys` (a list, comma-separated or indexed), `LogQueryString`; validated in `PaduraksaOptionsValidator` before anything is built, documented in `docs/install.md`.
- **Not built here:** the dashboard's live tail (M9, below). The simulator reports the response `X-Request-Id` in its response-headers step; where the ID goes *upstream* is a request transform, so the trace says nothing about that leg.

**As built (M9, `m9-metrics`: counters, histograms, `/metrics`, the sampler, rollups and the live overview).** Health checks and notifications came next, and the live tail has its own section (both below); the seams are named at the end.
- **Slots.** `MetricsRegistry` (`Paduraksa.Proxy.Metrics`) owns four append-only `Guid -> slot` registries (`MetricSlotRegistry`: host, route, upstream, destination; the key is the typed ID's value) and one striped table set per subject. It copies the rate-limit slots: a *publishing* compile calls `SlotFor` (a draft, a dry run or a simulation only `FindSlot`s, so `SnapshotCompilerOptions.WillBePublished`, formerly `RateLimitsWillBePublished`, governs both), the publisher calls `Publish` just before the swap (re-asserts the snapshot's objects, retires every other live one, so a slot a failed compile took is reclaimed at the next publish too), and a retired slot is rented to another object only after the grace period (one minute, `MetricsRegistry.DefaultGracePeriod`) with its counters cleared first. Retired slots are reused *before* the table grows. **Slot versions:** every slot has a version, even while stable; a rent makes it odd, clears the counters, sets the owner and makes it even again, and a compiled `MetricSlot` carries the version it was handed out under. `RecordRequest` drops a record whose slot version is no longer current (one compare per slot, still 0 B), so a WebSocket, SSE stream or slow upload on a deleted host that finishes after its slot went to another host is never counted into the new owner; a request that is mid-record at the very instant of a rent (nanoseconds, once per rent) can still land one stale increment. A publish that cannot reclaim an object's slot (another object holds it by then) is logged at Warning, as the rate-limit case is, *and* made harmless: that object's compiled slot is stale, so it records nothing (never into the other object) and the exporters leave it out until the next compile gives it a fresh slot. Only enabled hosts, their routes, the upstreams they route to and those upstreams' destinations get slots; the admin host's implicit route has none. Host slot 0 is reserved for requests no host owns (`(unmatched)`: scanners and typos show up as traffic that belongs to nobody). `CompiledHost.MetricSlot`, `CompiledRoute.MetricSlot` and `CompiledRoute.Upstream` (`UpstreamMetrics`: the upstream's slot and its destinations', found by YARP's destination key with a short scan) carry them; `RuntimeSnapshot.MetricRegistrations` is what the publisher hands the registry. S6's metric part is `MetricsEndToEndTests`: under a publish every 100 ms that adds and drops a host and an upstream, counters never decrease, stay on the same slots, and every answered request is counted once.
- **Storage and hot path.** `MetricTable` is a `long[]` per chunk of 16 slots holding every stripe's block, indexed `(stripe * 16 + slot % 16) * stride + metric`; the stride is a multiple of 8 longs, so no two slots share a cache line and stripes are kilobytes apart. Stripes are the core count rounded up to a power of two, at most 16, and a request uses `Thread.GetCurrentProcessorId() & mask`; counters are summed only when read. Chunks are created under a lock before a slot is given to a snapshot. Layouts (`RequestMetric`, `UpstreamMetric`, `DestinationMetric`): a host and a route count requests by status class (`1xx`-`5xx` and `none`, a dropped connection) and by `GateOutcome`, the response's `Content-Length` bytes (a streamed body adds nothing), and a host also sums the total and upstream microseconds and keeps two histograms; an upstream counts forwards, YARP `ForwarderError`s by kind, and its upstream-time histogram and sum; a destination counts forwards and the errors on its side (`ForwarderErrors.IsUpstreamFault`: a client that hung up is not a destination's error, though the upstream's kind counter still records it). A histogram is 101 fixed log-linear buckets (`LatencyBuckets`: 0-3 microseconds one each, then four per octave up to 2^26 microseconds = 67 s, the last bucket is the overflow; at most 25 % wide), `Interlocked.Increment` per request, quantiles interpolated inside the bucket. **Gate outcomes** (`GateOutcome`): `Forwarded` and each refusal kind the security page tells apart (`RefusedAccessList`, `RefusedRuleSet`, `RefusedRateLimit`, `RefusedProofOfWork`, `RefusedForwardAuth`, plus `RefusedRequestLimits`, `RefusedConnection`, `NoRoute`, `Unavailable`, `Dropped`, `UnknownHost`, `RefusedOther`, and `Answered` for the host's own answers: redirect, robots.txt, Redirect/Respond hosts, ACME, the verify endpoint). The policy that refuses records its kind on the pooled request state (`IRequestPolicy.Outcome`, a default member each policy class overrides), the gate sets `Forwarded` where it picks the endpoint, and the rest is classified from the facts at the end (`GateOutcomes.Classify`: aborted, no host, host kind, 421/413/414/431/404/503). Recording is `RequestMetricsRecorder`, called from `AccessLogMiddleware`'s completion callback for every request whether or not the host logs it, from the same two clocks the access log reads (the request's start timestamp and `AccessLogTransform`, which now also keeps YARP's destination key); the process's `RequestMetrics` struct goes in by `in`. It is 0 B: `RecordRequest` has an allocation test, the in-process Kestrel test measures the recorder next to the registration, recording and reapply callbacks, and the gate test runs with metrics on. The existing process totals (`ProofOfWorkStats`, `RateLimitStats`, `AccessListStats`) are not counted a second time: `ProcessCounters` exposes them where they live.
- **Cost.** `RecordRequest` is about 14 atomic increments on lines the core owns plus two feature lookups and one more stopwatch read in the completion; measured alone 75 ns a call on one thread. Measured on the Kestrel test context at about 330 ns for the whole recorder before the trims (the forwarder's error is looked up only for a 4xx/5xx or an aborted request: the lookup alone was 93 ns; the two "timed" counters are derived from the histograms). Bench (`bench/run-baseline.sh`, Paduraksa only, h1 and h2-tls, this branch against `main`, one box shared with other work so only quiet pairs count): at a fixed concurrency of 64, h1 176.4k vs 172.3k requests/s (-2.3 %), h2-tls about -2 % in the quiet pairs, proxy CPU 18.8 vs 19.1 microseconds a request (+1.6 %); an earlier set before the trims gave h1 -2.3 % and CPU +3 % over the median of four pairs. A single 30 s fixed-rate run at 30 000 requests/s, three repeats (medians): h1 p99 2.13 ms vs main's 220 ms (main's was a machine stall, its best repeat was 203 ms in a run the box was busy for), h2 p99 2.21 ms vs 2.48 ms; those tails are noise at this length, not a result. The loss is inside the 3 % the brief allows; the next saving would be sharing the access log's `Content-Length` and elapsed-time reads with the recorder (about 70 ns).
