- **Exposure.** `PrometheusWriter` writes text format 0.0.4 straight from the registry (no exporter package): counters per host (`paduraksa_requests_total{host,host_id,status_class}`, `paduraksa_gate_outcomes_total`, `paduraksa_response_size_bytes_total`), route, upstream (`_errors_total{kind}`) and destination, real histograms (`paduraksa_request_duration_seconds`, `paduraksa_host_upstream_duration_seconds`, `paduraksa_upstream_duration_seconds`: cumulative at the octave edges, which are bucket edges, so the counts are exact: `le` is 2^e - 1 microseconds (`0.000015` to `67.108863`), since Prometheus's `le` means "at most" and the counts are of observations below 2^e; `_sum` and `_count`), the process counters, `paduraksa_config_generation` and `paduraksa_hosts`. It labels from the *live snapshot* (`MetricInventory`), so a deleted host's series end with it; label values are escaped (`\\`, `\"`, `\n`), and a test feeds a host name that tries to close its label and start a new series. `PaduraksaMeter` (meter `Paduraksa`) offers the same counters as `ObservableCounter`s (`paduraksa.requests`, `paduraksa.gate.outcomes`, `paduraksa.upstream.errors`, ...) and two gauges, with the same tags; the meter API has no observable histogram, so a latency there is a sum in seconds. Both are read-only on demand, so the request path never touches them. **`/metrics`** on the admin port is `GET /api/v1/metrics` (`metrics:read`, in OpenAPI) with `/metrics` rewritten to it by `ApiPipelineMiddleware`, so a scraper gets the API's bearer-token authentication, permission check and problem bodies instead of a copy; `Cache-Control: no-store`. `metrics:read` (a new `Permission`, scope `metrics:read`) is held by every role: the numbers say no more than the config the same roles read, and the live overview is for all of them. `GET /api/v1/metrics/overview?range=hour|day|week|month` returns the dashboard's data. Scrape config and the families are in `docs/install.md`.
- **Sampler and rings.** `MetricSampler` (`MetricSamplerService`, a hosted service of the core host) reads each host's and upstream's counters and histograms, takes the gain since the tick before, and files it in a ten-second bucket and a minute window; a finished bucket goes into the series' ring, **360 buckets (an hour)**, a finished minute into `MetricRollupBacklog`. **Decision: 10 s buckets, not 1 s samples, in the hour.** 1 s samples would cost 3 600 points of about 160 bytes (about 0.6 MB) per series; the sampler still reads every second, and the headline is the last 30 s, but a ring holds the hour at 10 s resolution (58 KB a series, hosts and upstreams only; routes and destinations keep counters but no history, since nothing draws them over time). A bucket's percentiles come from the histogram of its own requests; a longer stretch combines them weighted by requests (`MetricPoints.Combine`), exact under steady load and an approximation otherwise, which the dashboard doesn't label. Series follow the slot's *owner*: when a slot is rented to another object the old series' unfinished minute is flushed under its own typed ID, and the new owner starts at zero (a grace period of many ticks makes it unlikely that a count is lost; a tick that overlaps a rent is skipped, because the sampler reads the slot's version before and after the owner and the counters and files nothing if it changed, so a half-cleared read is never taken for a gain). The totals series is the sum of the hosts' gains, the unmatched slot included, so a deleted host's traffic stays in it.
- **Coverage, alignment and no straddling (timeline honesty).** Every window (a ring bucket, a minute, a rollup row) records how many of its seconds the sampler observed: `MetricPoint.CoveredSeconds` next to `Width` (the bucket's length), and every rate (requests a second, 5xx a second, the tiles) divides by the covered seconds and never by the width, so a half-observed minute isn't diluted. 0 means nobody was looking; a covered window that saw no request has coverage and zero counts, and the two are never the same thing. The sampler measures from its own construction (the registry has counted since then), and each tick covers the time observed since the one before it. **Coverage and splitting come from monotonic time** (`TimeProvider.GetTimestamp`, the elapsed time between ticks); the wall clock only says which bucket and minute a moment belongs to. A wall clock stepped backwards therefore never reopens a closed bucket or minute and never doubles a second: the labels run on at the monotonic rate until the wall clock catches up, each real second is counted once, and a window's coverage can't exceed its width. **Ticks are aligned to whole seconds of the clock** (`MetricSamplerService` waits `UntilNextSecond` on the injected `TimeProvider`), so an interval crosses a ten-second or minute boundary only by the few milliseconds a tick is late. A tick that does cross one **splits its gain in proportion to the time on each side** (`TickSplit`): every counter and every latency histogram count is divided by rounding the cumulative share to the nearest whole count at each piece's end (the piece's share is the difference), so the pieces are whole numbers, add up to the gain exactly, and none is more than one count off its proportion; a tick 4 ms late keeps its three requests in the window they happened in (0.012 of one belongs after the boundary), a tick 5 s late over a boundary splits 4 s to 1 s. **Decision: a gap longer than 5 seconds (`MetricSampler.MaxTickGap`) is judged by what was counted in it.** *Nothing counted* means a suspended process, a VM pause or an outage: the seconds are not observed, and the buckets in between are in the ring as buckets with no coverage (only the hour before now is filled, so a day-long stall leaves 360 of them ending at the tick, not a ring of stale ones), which the chart draws as a gap. *Requests counted* means the process was running and serving and only the sampler was late (thread-pool starvation, a long GC): the gap counts as observed and its gain is split in proportion across every bucket and minute it spans, up to the ring's hour, like any tick. Filing that gain at the tick's end instead would have shown a gap, or a spike of five times the rate, for time that was served. The accepted cost: a process suspended with requests in flight that complete on resume gets the suspension reported as covered time with those few requests spread over it. The clock stepping forwards is a gap in the labels (the second before the new time is observed, the time skipped is not).
- **Shutdown and the seed.** When the sampler's service stops it takes a final reading and calls `MetricSampler.Flush`: the open bucket and the open minute of every series are closed as partial, with the seconds they observed up to now, and the minutes queued. The rollup job is registered before the sampler's service, so it stops after it and its last write carries them; the core host stops last of the three, after the public and admin hosts have drained, so the final reading sees the last requests. A crash loses what the process held in memory: the minute in progress and any minute closed in the last write interval (15 s by default), and the time after it shows as a gap, not as zeros. **The hour is seeded from the rollups:** before the sampler's first tick the job (`MetricRollupJob.StartAsync`) reads the last hour (and the minute before, for the window's edge) of rollups, every subject, once, and hands it to `MetricSampler.Seed`. It is a snapshot held in memory (`MetricHistory`, minute points keyed by object, trimmed as they leave the hour) and never reread, which is what makes the boot minute right: its first half is the database row from before the stop, its second half is the ring, and when the job later merges the live half into the row the snapshot doesn't see it, so nothing is counted twice. The busiest-hosts and upstream tables for the hour sum the seed and the live series; the tiles ("last 30 s") read the ring alone and, right after boot, divide by the time actually observed. An unreadable database at startup logs a warning and leaves the view starting at boot (as before). A seed is at most an hour of minute rows of the series that existed: a few thousand points.
- **Rollups.** `MetricRollupJob` (core host, registered before the sampler so it starts first and stops after it) writes the backlog every `Paduraksa:Metrics:WriteInterval` (15 s) into `MetricRollups` (migrations `MetricRollups` and `MetricRollupCoverage`): an Int64Ordered `MetricRollupId` (`mrl_`), the subject (`Total`, `Host` or `Upstream`: the `MetricRollupSubject` enum in Core), the object's typed-ID string (empty for the totals and for the unmatched host), the minute as whole Unix minutes, `CoveredSeconds`, the counters and the six percentiles; a unique index on (subject, ID, minute). **The totals series has a row for every minute the process observed, idle ones included** (with its covered seconds: a partial first minute after boot, a partial last minute at shutdown), which makes it the record of when the process was up: a minute with no totals row is a minute nobody observed. Host and upstream rows stay sparse (written for a minute only when they counted something); a missing row inside an observed minute means zero for that object. One totals row a minute is 1 440 a day, 43 200 a month of running and 20 160 held at the 14-day default, against the per-host and per-upstream rows a busy install already writes (at about 150 series, up to 150 a minute): it is noise in the table, so there is no separate presence table. The migration sets `CoveredSeconds` to 60 for the rows already stored (they were minutes with traffic, which the process was there for); **idle minutes from before the upgrade were never stored, so they show as gaps** in the history they leave, and only minutes from the upgrade on tell idle from down. **Decision: a minute written twice is merged into its row, not kept as a second row.** A graceful stop writes the partial minute and the next start goes on to fill the same minute, so `MetricRollupStore.WriteAsync` loads the stored rows of the written range and combines each collision with `MetricPoints.Combine`: counts and sums add, observed seconds add (capped at 60: the two halves never overlap in time), percentiles are the mean of the two weighted by their requests (the same approximation as a longer range's). Two rows per minute would break the unique key that every read relies on (one row, one minute) and push the merge into every reader, where it would be paid on every poll instead of once per restart. A failed write puts the minutes back and logs a warning; the backlog is bounded (9 000 minutes), so a database that stays down loses the oldest, never memory. Retention (`Paduraksa:Metrics:Retention`, default 14 days, 1 hour to 5 years, validated like `Paduraksa:Logs:*`) deletes by age hourly. **Decision: no hourly downsampling in v0.1.** The longer ranges group minutes in memory (6 minutes a step for a day, 42 for a week, 3 hours for a month, 240 points each) from at most 43 200 rows of one series; at about 150 series a day is a few tens of MiB, and the retention default keeps the table in the low hundreds of MiB for a busy install. A second, coarser table would add a job and a second query path for a saving nobody has asked for yet; the unique key and the typed-ID subject leave room for it. **Known approximations:** a latency percentile of a merged or grouped minute is the requests-weighted mean of its parts, not the percentile of the union; a stall's gain is spread evenly over the stall, not placed where within it the requests were; a suspension with requests completing on resume is reported as covered (above). The longer ranges are summed in SQL (`GROUP BY` the object or the step, `SUM`s only, the observed seconds included: nothing of the range becomes an entity), the seed is one read of the `Minute` index range, and a range's rollup read is kept for 30 s, since the page polls every twenty.
- **The overview.** `MetricsService` (Application) is the one read side for the API and the dashboard: `PrometheusText()` and `ReadOverviewAsync(range)` returning `MetricsOverview` (Contracts): the pace over the last 30 s (requests, 5xx share, refused, bytes, p50/p95/p99 total and upstream), a chart series, the eight busiest hosts and the upstreams ordered by errors. **The hour** is the seeded minutes up to where this run's ring begins (a bucket a minute, the boot minute's first half cut short where the ring starts) and then the ring's ten-second buckets; a day, week or month reads the rollups (so they lag by up to a minute and the write interval). **Gaps are in the data, not in the drawing** (`MetricTimeline`): the series runs from the first step the server observed to the last, every step between them is present, and a step the server was not running for has `coveredSeconds` 0 (and every figure 0) next to `widthSeconds`, distinct from a covered step that saw no requests (a rate of 0, no latency). A step has its own width because the hour mixes minute-wide steps with ten-second ones; a longer range's step covers the sum of its minutes' coverage, and its rates are over those seconds. **Decision: the part of the range before the first observed step, and any step after the last, are not gaps** and are left out: a fresh install's 30-day view starts where its data does, and the minute the rollups haven't been written for yet isn't drawn as downtime. **Decision: a step observed in part (the server started or stopped inside it) is an ordinary point** (its rate is over what was observed, and its tooltip says "Observed 20 s of 1 min"), not a lighter segment: only a step with no coverage at all breaks the line. The home page's `TrafficOverview` shows five tiles, a requests chart (area, with 5xx) and a latency chart (p50, p95, p99 and the upstream's p95) as inline SVG (`TimeSeriesChart`: no script and no dependency, a native tooltip per point, a round-number scale), the busiest hosts (linked; the requests no host owns, unlinked) and the upstream errors (named from the config), with a 1 h / 24 h / 7 d / 30 d switch. The chart's axis is time (points sit at their start times, so a minute-wide step and ten-second steps share it), a line is **one polyline per unbroken run of values** (a lone point is a mark the width of its step), the latency lines also break where a covered step timed nothing, and a stretch with no data is a quiet shaded band whose tooltip says "No data: Paduraksa was not running" (`pdk-chart-gap`, a 6 % tint of the text colour, so it reads in both themes and at phone width). The hour polls every two seconds while the tab is visible (the `paduraksa.pageVisibility` pause, as the security page); the rollup ranges poll a tenth as often. A range that cannot be read shows an alert with "Try again". The page needs `metrics:read`. Not built: the per-host page's own traffic (the home table links to it), which would be one more call to the same service.
- **Left out, with the seams.** *Health checks and notifications:* the registry's per-upstream error counters are the input; YARP's `IUpstreamHealth` is already read by the simulator. *Route and destination history:* add a `MetricRollupSubject` value and a series kind in the sampler. *Downsampling:* above.

**As built (M9, `m9-live-tail`: the live access-log tail).** The ring keeps its one reader; the request path is untouched (no new field, no new work, still 0 B). The drain, which already formats every entry, offers it to a second place once the file's line is written.
- **The hub.** `AccessLogTail` (`Paduraksa.Proxy.Logging`) holds the newest `Paduraksa:AccessLog:TailSize` entries (default 4 096, 64 to 65 536, validated and documented in install.md) and a bounded channel per subscriber. `AccessLogDrainService` calls `Offer` after `sink.WriteLine`, in its own `try`: a failure in the tail is reported like a failing sink and never costs the file a line. **Per-host on/off needs no code of its own: a host with its access log off never reaches the ring (`AccessLogMiddleware.Record` returns first), so it is in neither the file nor the tail. Decision, recorded because privacy depends on it: "off" means not recorded anywhere, and a test proves it through a real listener.** The tail is memory only (a restart empties it) and holds what the file holds, no more.
- **Copied, shown under the file's rules.** The ring's slot is reused, so `Offer` copies the struct into the buffer (`AccessLogTailEntry.Bound`: any string longer than a character past its cap is cut, and the raw query at the file's own query cap of 1 024 characters, so the tail never holds more of a query than the file could show; nothing is allocated unless a string is cut). **Memory, stated as it is:** an entry is typically a few hundred bytes to a KiB, so 2 to 4 MiB for a full default buffer; the worst case is every field at its cap, 3 897 characters (about 7.6 KiB) plus string headers and the slot, **about 8 KiB an entry, about 32 MiB at the default 4 096 and about 512 MiB at the largest size**, reachable only by a client that sends kilobytes in every field of every request. A subscriber's channel adds at most 2 048 converted entries, which share their strings with the buffer while the entries are still in it. Cutting the query before redaction is safe: a cut only removes the end of the last parameter, so a sensitive one that straddles it is a name with a partial value (written `name=REDACTED`) or a partial name (no value at all); a test puts a sensitive key at the cut in three positions. **Decision: nothing is converted until someone looks.** A subscriber's entries and a backlog become `AccessLogTailEntry`s when they are read: capped at the file's caps, escaped by the one escaper the file's quoted fields use (`LogEscaping`: `\"` and `\\`, C0 and C1 controls including NEL as `\xNN`, U+2028 and U+2029 as `\u2028` and `\u2029`), the query redacted by the formatter's own routine (`AccessLogFormatter.AppendQuery`, which `BuildRequestTarget` now calls too, so the tail and the file cannot disagree on which parameters are sensitive, `LogQueryString=false` included). A parity test formats an entry both ways (quotes, backslashes, CR/LF, NEL, C1 controls, U+2028 and U+2029 in the path, query, host, user agent, referer, upstream, request ID and `CF-Ray`, the redaction list, a replaced list) and compares each field with the file line's quoted text as written, with no unescaping on either side. The alternative, converting every entry as the drain sees it, measured 296 B and about 300 ns an entry with nobody watching, so it was dropped for the lazy copy below.
- **Hosts.** The entry carries only the `Host` header, so the host that owned the request is resolved against the live snapshot (`HostTable.Find`) when the entry is converted: `MatchedHostNames` (all of the host's names) or none. A backlog entry of a host deleted since reads as "no host matched"; a streamed entry is resolved as the drain offers it, within 200 ms of the request. The alternative, a field on `AccessLogEntry` set by the request path, was refused: the brief keeps the request path as it is.
- **Subscribers.** `Subscribe(filter, backlog)` takes the hub's one lock to copy the buffer's structs and to register the subscriber in the same step, then converts and filters the copy outside the lock, so the backlog and the stream neither skip nor repeat (a concurrent test checks the sequence is gapless over 32 subscribers racing a producer). A subscriber's channel is bounded (2 048) and drops its oldest entry when full, counting it (`TakeSkipped`); the drain never waits for a reader's channel. The subscribers are bounded too (`MaxSubscribers` 32: dashboard tabs and API streams together); the 33rd is refused. The filter runs before the channel (host name, "no host matched", status class or status, client address prefix, method, path contains), so a narrow filter doesn't fill the channel with lines it will never show. **The lock** covers the drain's store and the hand-over to the channels (a write to a bounded channel never waits) and a reader's plain array copy of the buffer, about a millisecond at the largest size; the drain waits for a reader's call only for that copy, converts the entry before taking the lock, and the request path never takes it.
- **Cost, measured** (`bench/Paduraksa.Benchmarks/AccessLogDrainBenchmarks`, per entry through `AccessLogDrainService.Drain`, in-process, medium job, workstation GC; a noisy box, so read the differences only roughly): file only 670 ns; tail with nobody watching 825 ns and **0 B more** (a struct copy, ten length checks and an uncontended lock, off the request path); a subscriber that keeps up 1 020 ns and 296 B (one entry object shared by every subscriber); a subscriber that never reads 1 060 ns (its channel overflows on every entry). `TheGateAllocatesNothingWhileTheTailHasASubscriber` runs the real Kestrel pipeline with a running drain and an overflowing subscriber and asserts the gate at 0 B a request. It is "free" in the sense that matters (the drain is not the request path, and at the bench's 170 000 requests/s it is a fifth of a core more), not in the sense of unmeasurable: that is the number, and a tail that converted lazily only when read was the way to get it that low.
- **The API.** `GET /api/v1/logs/tail` (`logs:read`, in OpenAPI as `StreamAccessLog`, `text/event-stream`): see the dashboard section for the wire format. It lives in `LogEndpoints` beside the file endpoints, reads through `LogTailService` (Application: opens a subscription, maps `AccessLogTailEntry` to the contract's `LogTailEntry`), and is bounded like a dashboard subscriber (the same channel and the same 32). `GET /api/v1/logs/recent` (M10, for MCP, §11.2) is the same buffer as one JSON array: the newest `limit` entries (1 to 1000, default 100) the filters keep, oldest first, with no stream. The Application layer is the only place that sees the hub besides the drain; Admin and Dashboard get contract types.
- **Tests.** Unit (`AccessLogTailTests`, 40): ring semantics and wrap, drop counting, the subscriber bound, atomic subscribe, every filter, wildcard and multi-name hosts, redaction and caps parity with the file formatter, what the buffer keeps of a long field, the drain offering after a failing sink, and a tail that throws never costing the file a line (`AccessLogDrainServiceTests`). Proxy (`AccessLogTailEndToEndTests`, `AccessLogAllocationTests`): requests through a real Kestrel listener appear with the right fields and redaction, a logging-off host never does, the gate at 0 B with a subscriber. Admin (`LogTailApiTests`): scope per role, entries with sequence and redaction, filters and a backlog, heartbeat, the skipped event, the stream bound with a 503, a disconnect freeing its place, 400s, the OpenAPI entry. Dashboard: see §12. Run locally, a developer machine's port prober sends `GET /` to every new listener and it shows up in a tail like any request; the tests that count entries filter to their own client address.
- **Left out.** Persistence across restarts, regex or CIDR filters (a prefix of the address text is what the filter takes), saved filters, export (the file is the record), and other nodes (§16.1 already names the pull over the sync stream). Both a field on the entry for the matched host and a per-request sampling are seams: the first needs the request path, the second the drain.

**As built (`analytics`: traffic analytics, visitors and content, clients and bots).** Nothing is new on the request path: the ring entry already carries the host, path, method, protocol, status, declared bytes, durations, user agent, referer and client address, so analytics is a **third consumer in the drain** (`Paduraksa.Proxy.Analytics.TrafficAnalytics`), offered each entry after the file's line and the tail, in a `try` of its own. A failure in it is reported like a failing sink and never costs the file a line; a host with its access log off never reaches the ring, so **it is in neither the file nor the analytics** (a test proves it through a real listener, as for the tail). `TrafficAnalyticsDrainTests` and `AccessLogAllocationTests.TheGateAllocatesNothingWhileTheAnalyticsAreFedByTheDrain` keep the gate at 0 B with the analytics fed by a running drain. Query strings are never counted: a path is the decoded path without its query.
- **Buckets and memory.** Five minutes wide, one `TrafficBucket` per host that had traffic plus one for the requests no host owns (hosts are resolved against the live snapshot when the drain reaches the entry, like the tail; at most 1 024 per bucket, the rest counted with the unowned). A bucket closes five seconds after its end (so entries still in the ring land in it) into `AnalyticsBacklog` (bounded to 4 096 rollups: a database that stays down loses the oldest, never memory) and a shutdown closes the open one as partial. **Memory is fixed by construction**: the open buckets (one or two) times the hosts with traffic; a bucket is 4 KiB of visitor registers, four Space-Saving sketches (paths 64 keys, referers 32, user agents 32, largest responses 16), 64 latency histograms of 808 bytes (one per tracked path) and a few bounded counters (status codes 64, methods 16, browsers 64, systems 32, bot names 32), so about 100 KiB for a busy host and a few KiB for a quiet one; the user-agent cache is 4 096 strings (LRU). A late entry (its bucket closed already) is filed in the oldest open bucket, since dropping a request that was served would lose it. A bucket's stored form is 2 to 20 KiB (Brotli of varints, the visitors sketch on its own, sparse while it holds few registers).
- **What is counted, and the bounds.** Exact (up to their table sizes, the rest in "other"): requests, declared response bytes (a streamed body declares none, so bandwidth is a floor), status classes and codes, methods, HTTP versions, the device, bot-kind, browser, system and bot-name distributions. **Unique visitors** are a HyperLogLog (precision 12, 4 096 registers, standard error 1.04/sqrt(4096), about 1.6 %), merged by the register-wise maximum, so a range's visitors are the union of its buckets' (a visitor on two hosts or in two hours is one). **The top tables** (paths, referers, raw user agents, and paths by bytes) are **Space-Saving** sketches: every tracked key has a count and an error, its true weight is between `count - error` and `count`, a key that is not tracked weighs at most the sketch's floor, and a key heavier than the floor is always tracked; in a bucket the overshoot is at most (requests / capacity). Merging (Agarwal et al.) takes a key one side lacks at that side's floor, in its count and its error, so **a range's bound is the sum of its buckets' errors** and stays a true bound; a range is merged into a 16 times larger sketch so a month rarely drops a key that matters, and an hourly roll-up into one twice the size. A path's payload (bytes, 5xx, latency histogram, so the p95) covers the requests seen *since the path was tracked* (an evicted path starts over); the slowest-paths table needs five. **Decision: error rate is the share of 5xx**, as in the metrics overview; 4xx is the status breakdown's. **Decision: the largest responses are their own sketch weighted by bytes**, not a column of the paths sketch, since a large file downloaded three times is exactly what a sketch ranked by requests would evict.
- **User-agent classification.** **Decision: our own table, not a library.** `UserAgentClassifier` is one file of rules (the answer is a handful of families, a library brings thousands of regexes, which the rest of the proxy avoids, and a dependency to track), tried in order: AI crawlers (taken from `RobotsText.AiCrawlers`, so the two lists cannot drift), search engines, social previews, monitors and uptime checkers, scanners, named SEO crawlers, HTTP libraries, then anything calling itself a bot, crawler, spider, scraper or fetcher (named by its own product token); then browsers from the most specific token (Edge's `Edg/` is in a Chrome agent) to the least, an operating system and a device (desktop, mobile, tablet, bot, other: a television or console). It reads the first 1 024 characters for a bot's token (bots name themselves late) and the first 400 for the browser, system and device, case-insensitively. **A miss is cheap on purpose** (the drain is one thread, and an attacker chooses user agents): a bitmap of the first three characters of every token is tested at each position in about a nanosecond, and only a position that passes is compared with the rules that start there, the lowest rule index winning, capped at 48 matches per agent; a 1 KiB agent with no token in it takes about 3 us and one made of nothing but tokens about 5 us (first version: 34 us to 400 us). The cache (`UserAgentCache`, 4 096, LRU) is keyed on the capped copy, so a 32 KB agent pins 1 KiB. Honest limits: Brave and most forks are Chrome, an iPad on iPadOS 13+ sends a Mac's agent, a scanner that sends a browser's agent is a browser. Tested against about 90 real agent strings. A `BotKey` (`SearchEngine|Googlebot`) is the counted label, built once with the cached classification.
- **The drain never pays for analytics with a file line. Rule: when the ring is more than half full, an agent the cache does not know is not classified (it is counted as "Unclassified", device other, not a bot) and the robots check is skipped; the entry is still counted, with its path, status, bytes and visitor.** The drain passes `behind` to the analytics from `AccessLogRing.Pending` (`AccessLogDrainService.BehindDivisor`). A test streams 20 000 unique 1 KiB agents at 20 000 a second through a running drain and requires every line in the file and none dropped, and another fills a ring past half and checks the first entries are the unclassified ones.
- **Bots ignoring robots.txt.** For a host that serves a managed robots.txt (any mode but passthrough), a bot of a kind that is supposed to read it (search engine, AI crawler, social preview, scanner, other bot; **not** monitors and HTTP libraries) that fetches a path the file disallows *for that agent* is counted by name. `RobotsRules` reads the compiled body back (RFC 9309: the group with the longest agent name inside the crawler's agent, else `*`; longest rule wins, Allow wins ties; `*` and `$`; matched without backtracking, since a custom file is operator text; `/robots.txt` is always allowed), cached per host and per compiled response object, so a publish invalidates it; the group that applies to an agent is resolved once per agent (256 per host) and the rules are matched against the path cut at 256 characters, each rule's literal prefix and pinned end worked out once, so a rule that cannot match costs one comparison (1 000 wildcard rules that all share the path's prefix: about 25 us for a bot's entry, the extreme; 2 048 rules is the cap). It runs in the drain against the live snapshot, and not while the drain is behind.
- **Visitors and privacy.** **Decision: a random salt per process, in memory only, never stored.** The client address is hashed (SHA-256 of the 16-byte salt and the address: an IPv4 address or an IPv6 address's /64, with v4-mapped IPv6 counted as the IPv4 address, parsed by the gate's allocation-free `IpParser`) into the sketch, which keeps only the longest run of zeros per register; nothing stored can be turned back into an address, even by someone holding the database and the binary. A per-day salt would not change that (a sketch is already one-way) and would cut every visitor at midnight in a range that spans it; a salt that is stable across restarts would have to be stored, which is what we avoid. **The cost is accepted and stated: a visitor active across a restart is counted once on each side of it.** A referer is reduced to scheme and host (no port, path, query or credentials), `direct` when absent and `(same site)` when it is the host the request was for; user agents are kept as sent (cut at 256 characters, control characters replaced); paths are kept as the access log keeps them, so a secret that lives in a path is in both.
- **TLS version left out.** The brief allowed a field on the entry only if the gate already had the value at 0 B. It does not: the TLS version is a connection feature Kestrel exposes behind a lookup (`ITlsHandshakeFeature`), which the gate does not read, so adding it would put new work on the request path. Left out; the seam is one field on `AccessLogEntry` filled where the gate already has the connection, with an allocation test.
- **Rollups.** `AnalyticsRollups` (migration `AnalyticsRollups`; Int64Ordered `AnalyticsRollupId`, `anr_`): a **tier** (0 five minutes, 1 an hour, 2 a day), a **subject** (coverage, a host, the requests no host owns), the host's typed-ID text (never the in-memory slot), the start of the stretch (Unix seconds), `CoveredSeconds`, `Requests`, `BytesOut`, `ServerErrors`, and two blobs (`Visitors`, `Data`); a unique index on (tier, subject, host, start) and one on (tier, start). **Coverage honours the M9 timeline semantics**: every tick the drain makes credits the time it observed to the buckets it fell in (a tick later than five seconds counts only if it brought entries: a suspended process has an empty ring), and a closed bucket writes a coverage row even when nothing happened, so a stretch with no coverage row inside the observed span is a **gap, not zeros**, and a quiet bucket is a bucket with coverage and no host rows. `AnalyticsRollupJob` (registered before the drain, so it stops after it and carries the bucket the drain closes on its way out) writes every 15 s (`Paduraksa:Analytics:WriteInterval`), merges a bucket written twice into its row (a restart inside it: counts add, visitors union, coverage adds up to 300), **rolls an hour up ten minutes after it ends** into one row per host (sketches merged at twice the size, one transaction per hour, idempotent), then **a day up once all its hours are rolled** (four times the size), and prunes hourly. **Retention** is `Paduraksa:Analytics:Retention` (default 30 days, 1 hour to 5 years, validated, in install.md); five-minute rows are kept two days once their hour is rolled up, and hourly and daily rows for the whole retention. **Decision: reads take the stored rows and the backlog's unconfirmed buckets under one gate with the writer**, so a bucket is in exactly one of the two: never missing, never doubled.
- **Reads, cost and the API.** `AnalyticsService` reads **daily rows** for the whole rolled-up days inside the range (only the month, whose step is a day), hourly rows for the whole rolled-up hours of what is left (the day and the week), and five-minute rows for the edges and the hours not rolled up yet (the hour reads only these, since its step is five minutes); the steps are 5 minutes (hour), 1 hour (day), 3 hours (week) and 1 day (month). `EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN` tests pin every read to an index (all hosts: the (tier, start) walk; one host: a seek on (tier, subject, host, start); coverage the same). **Measured on a Release build** (20 busy hosts, 30 days of hourly rows: 15 120 rows of about 2.7 KB of data and 4 KB of visitors sketch): **without the daily tier a month for all hosts took 1.2 s** (0.4 s of it reading, the rest decoding and merging 14 400 blobs), so the daily tier was added: **a month for all hosts now takes 75-85 ms**, one host 5 ms, a week for all hosts 0.23 s; rolling the 30 days up once, as a catch-up, takes 1.7 s (a day a day in normal running). A result is kept 5 s (30 s beyond the hour), at most 128 of them (expired first, then the oldest: the API accepts any well-formed host ID, a deleted host's history stays readable). A step with requests and no coverage row is drawn as data (observed for the moment it is known to have had), so the chart and the totals agree; a step with neither is the gap. A dropped connection is the status "none" ("No response" on the page), the API's class name. `GET /api/v1/analytics?range=hour|day|week|month&host=host_...|unmatched` (`metrics:read`, in OpenAPI) returns `AnalyticsOverview`: totals, the series (with `coveredSeconds`), the status classes, codes, methods and protocols, the tables with each row's error bound, the browsers, systems and devices, the bot kinds, top bots and bots ignoring robots.txt. It is a **read-only** endpoint (for the MCP branch's classification at merge).
- **Bench** (`bench/Paduraksa.Benchmarks`, `AccessLogDrainBenchmarks`, per entry, one pinned core): the file's line alone 0.93-1.0 us; the analytics add about 0.8-0.9 us an entry (1.7-1.9 us in all), about 190 B of garbage an entry on the drain's thread (the referer's normalised text, mostly) for the same entry and about 700 B for 256 varied ones; the live tail's reader costs about 0.4 us for comparison. **The worst case, every entry a unique 1 KiB agent (the cache always missing): 6.6 us an entry in all (about 150 000 entries a second), 800 B an entry; before the classifier was made cheap it was about 400 us (2 500 a second), which is where the ring would have dropped lines.** End-to-end (`run-baseline.sh`, h1 and h2-tls, three alternating rounds against the merge base): h1 median 174.1k requests/s against main's 173.3k (rounds 174.1, 172.3, 174.6 against 173.2, 173.3, 174.1) and 18.9-19.3 against 19.0-19.1 us of proxy CPU a request; h2-tls 153.5k against 152.7k and 22.9-23.7 against 23.0-23.3 us; p99 0.74-0.79 ms against 0.79-0.82 ms (h1) and 1.01-1.06 against 1.02-1.07 (h2-tls). **The request path is unchanged, inside the run-to-run noise**, as it should be: the runner's hosts have the access log off by default, and the analytics live entirely behind the ring either way. The cost is the drain's, above.
- **Later (not built):** geo and a country map (after v0.1, needing a local country database), protection-over-time series (the gate's refusals by kind per step), and top client IPs (which need a place to keep addresses, against the privacy rule above, so they would be a separate, opt-in decision).

**As built (M9 part 2a, `m9-health-notify`: health state and notifications).** Active and passive checks themselves were built in M1; this adds what is done with their verdicts. The live tail (part 2b) is separate.
- **Reading YARP.** `IUpstreamHealth.ReadAll` returns, per upstream, each destination's active, passive and combined verdict (the combined one is computed: YARP's own is internal) and the last active probe in words ("HTTP 503", "timed out", "connection refused": never the exception's text). YARP does not keep a probe's result, so `ProbeRecordingPolicy` wraps its registered active policy (same name, the verdict logic stays YARP's) and notes each result in a `ProbeLog` held weakly by destination state. `Lookup` (the simulator's) is left as it was and **cannot say an upstream is down**: YARP's available-destinations list falls back to every destination when none is healthy (pinned by a test), which is why `ReadAll` reads verdicts.
- **The watcher.** `HealthMonitor` (a core-host hosted service, never the request path) reads `ReadAll` every second into the pure `HealthTracker` and publishes the result to the `UpstreamHealthBoard` the dashboard and `GET /api/v1/upstreams/health` (and `/upstreams/{id}/health`, `config:read`) read. **Debounce:** a new state must hold `Paduraksa:Notifications:HealthHold` (10 s) before it is reported. **Flap guard:** an object (a destination, or an upstream as a whole) that changes 4 times in 10 minutes is reported once as flapping and then silent until it has been still for 10 minutes, when its state is reported once more. **What is not a change (§6.3):** a destination first seen healthy or Unknown, an Unknown that follows a lapsed passive verdict, a destination removed by a publish (forgotten, so re-adding it cannot report a recovery) and an upstream with no check configured; a publish that keeps the IDs keeps YARP's state, so reports nothing; a destination first seen unhealthy is down. An upstream's change replaces its destinations' changes of the same direction in the same tick (one message names the destinations). Each reported change is a **system audit entry** (`upstream.down`, `upstream.up`, `upstream.destination.down|up`, `*.flapping`; target the upstream, details the destination, address, reason, since) and an event. Proof: `UpstreamHealthNotificationTests` runs a real probe against a switched listener on a real host with the dispatcher and a fake webhook: exactly one audit row and one message per transition, none for publishes. A restart forgets "last change" (the audit has the history).
- **Channels** are an **operational table** (`NotificationChannels`, migration `NotificationChannels`), the DNS-provider decision: they hold delivery state outside the document and secrets that must never be in one, so there are no revisions or review-before-apply; each change is audited (`notification.channel.create|update|delete|test`). Permissions: **`providers:read` and `providers:write`**, shared with ACME accounts and DNS providers (they hold credentials the same way; no new permission, role or token scope). Kinds: ntfy (JSON publish to the server root, optional bearer token), Gotify (`POST /message`, token in `X-Gotify-Key`), Discord (embed, `allowed_mentions.parse` empty, markdown in names escaped), generic webhook (JSON; with a signing key, `X-Paduraksa-Timestamp` and `X-Paduraksa-Signature: sha256=hex(HMAC-SHA-256("<timestamp>." + body))`), and email. **Email is MailKit 5**, not `System.Net.Mail.SmtpClient` (Microsoft discourages it and it has no modern TLS or SASL): MIT, maintained, STARTTLS can be *required* (a server that does not offer it is refused, never sent the password in clear) or implicit TLS, the certificate is checked against the system roots, plain text with `Auto-Submitted`, recipients and the subject sanitised. The senders live in a new project, `Paduraksa.Notifications` (Core only), so MailKit stays out of Application.
- **Secrets** (webhook URL, token, SMTP password) are vault `NotificationSecret`s (migration `NotificationSecrets`, which rebuilds `Secrets` to widen its kind check: a test pins that `TotpStates` survives and that its Down deletes the notification secrets first (the narrower check would refuse them), keeping every other row, and EF's non-transactional-PRAGMA warning is ignored for the vault only) and a webhook's signing key an `HmacSigningKey` read through `IMacKeySource` (HMAC stays banned outside the vault). **The vault grows its fourth sanctioned egress**, the narrowest: `INotificationSecretSource.UseAsync` lends a `NotificationSecret`'s value to a callback as a view of the pinned buffer, zeroed when the callback returns (a webhook URL, token or password has to be used by the sender, which no handler or MAC handle can do); every other kind answers as absent, nothing returns the value, and the arch test lists the position. A secret is in no config document, response, OpenAPI document, audit entry or log (tests walk them all); a replacement keeps, replaces in place or removes (empty) a secret; a failed change takes back what it stored. Failures read as fixed lines ("The server refused the credentials (HTTP 401).", "Could not connect to the server.") and say whether a retry can help. **No TLS with a login is refused:** a channel with `smtpSecurity: none` may carry neither a login nor a password (`notification.smtp.insecureLogin`), since both would cross the network in clear; a trusted-network relay takes neither. **SSRF, decided:** a channel's URL or SMTP host may be any address, loopback, link-local and the admin port included. That is accepted because only `providers:write` holds it (as with DNS providers), responses are never echoed (only the fixed lines above), redirects are refused, and the test endpoint grants nothing the delivery loop does not.
- **Dispatcher.** *(As first built, in memory; the queues became database rows in `notify-outbox`, "As built: durable delivery and history" below. The retry schedule, the coalescing and the digest wording are unchanged.)* `INotificationEvents.Publish` only queues (bounded 1000, oldest dropped and counted, never blocks); a loop addresses each event to every enabled channel subscribed to it; each channel has a queue (bounded 100) and a loop, so a dead channel never holds up another. A transient failure (network, 408/425/429/5xx, a 4xx mail reply) is retried after 15 s, 1 min, 4 min and 16 min, with the channel's later messages waiting behind it, then given up; a permanent one (401/403/other 4xx, a refused login, a missing secret) is recorded at once; the channel keeps **last sent** and **last error** for the dashboard. Settings are read again for every attempt. **Storm coalescing (decided):** after the first message reaches a channel's queue it waits `CoalesceWindow` (3 s) and takes everything queued; health, expiry and ACME events of one kind that are several become **one digest** (title "12 notifications: upstreams down", ten lines and "and 2 more"), sign-in events and tests always go singly. "One notification per event per channel" holds: each event is delivered once per channel, as itself or inside a digest. Messages are short and name the object, since when, the reason and what to do, with a dashboard link only when **`Paduraksa:Notifications:AdminUrl`** is set (the process cannot know which name or port its admin is reached by).
- **Events.** *Upstream down/up and destination down/up* (above; new channels start without the destination pair, which a one-destination upstream says twice). *Certificate expiry:* `CertificateExpiryMonitor` looks hourly at every active certificate whatever its source (ACME, uploaded, self-signed, local-CA leaves and the root) and sends the smallest crossed threshold of 30/14/7/1 days **once per chain and threshold**: `ExpiryNotices` (certificate, `NotAfter`, days; cascade on delete) records it, so a restart does not repeat it, a renewal (a new `NotAfter`) starts over, several crossed thresholds are one message with all marked, and **nothing is marked while no enabled channel subscribes**, so a channel added at 5 days still hears the next one. *ACME:* the first failure of a streak (not every retry; the expiry notices cover a certificate that stays unrenewed) and the recovery after it. *Sign-in anomalies, defined:* **(1)** an account locked by failed sign-ins (the lockout the identity core decides), **(2)** an Owner or Admin signing in from an address with **no completed sign-in as that user in the last 30 days** (a password-only step does not count until the second factor completes), for a user who has signed in before; the answer comes from the audit log, so a restart forgets nothing. **The lookup is cheap by construction:** two seeks on the `AuditEvents (ActorUserId, Action, Timestamp)` index (migration `AuditSignInIndex`), bounded by time ("has signed in before" looks back a year, "known address" 30 days), run before the sign-in's write transaction opens; the `Details` LIKE that tells a completed password-only sign-in from a pending one runs only over that user's rows in the window, and a test pins the query plan (no scan of the table). A user whose last sign-in was over a year ago is treated as signing in for the first time. **Not notified:** the per-address rate limit (`AdminThrottle`): it fires on any attacker's burst and the lockout already covers guessing at an account; the seam is the refusal in `AdminThrottle.TryEnter`. *Test:* the "send test" button, one message without retries.
- **Dashboard.** Upstreams page, upstream drawer, hosts list and overview: dots per destination and per upstream with the reason, a "last change" line, a "Upstream down/degraded" chip beside a host's name, "N down" on the overview's tile. **Polling:** `UpstreamHealthFeed` reads the board every 3 s and renders only when the reading differs; unlike the security page it does not pause on a hidden tab (that JS hook has one slot and the overview already uses it, and a memory read that renders nothing unless something changed costs a hidden tab nothing). **Settings, Notifications** (`/settings/notifications`, `providers:read`): a row per channel with its target, event count, last sent / last error, send test with its verdict inline, and a drawer whose fields follow the kind (**adding a channel sends it one test message at once**, so the operator learns whether it works without a second click; saving an edit does not, and a test pins both); secrets are password fields emptied as the form is sent; events are grouped checkboxes. The list re-reads every 10 s for the delivery state the dispatcher changes.
- **Cost.** Nothing on the request path changed (the allocation tests are untouched): the watcher and the probe recorder run off timers.
- **Left out, with the seams.** The per-IP throttle notice (above); a "since the process started" limit on the last-change line (a persisted history would read the audit); per-channel quiet hours or severity filters; Pushover, Telegram, Slack (a `INotificationSender` and a kind each); email as HTML; Sign-in anomaly rules beyond the two; the live tail (part 2b).

**As built (`notify-outbox`: durable delivery and history).** Before this, an event waited in memory and a stop or a crash lost whatever was queued. Now it is a row, and the channels' workers send from the rows. Nothing is on the request path.
- **Publish never waits for the database.** `INotificationEvents.Publish` is a `TryWrite` into a bounded in-memory buffer (`EventCapacity`, 1000; the oldest is dropped and counted, `paduraksa_notification_buffer_dropped_total`). The **flusher** (inside `NotificationDispatcher`, a hosted service) writes the buffer in one transaction every `FlushInterval` (250 ms, real time: a throughput knob, not a schedule, so a test that holds the clock still does not hold it up), at once when `FlushBatchSize` (100) events are waiting, and on a graceful stop. **That buffer is the only window in which a crash loses an event: at most about one flush interval of events** (plus, while the database cannot be written to, what the bounded buffer holds; a failed write keeps its batch and tries again, and what arrives meanwhile drops oldest-first and is counted). A test proves `Publish` returns at once with the database held and that the buffer then drops and counts.
- **Tables** (config database, migration `NotificationOutbox`, `Int64Ordered` IDs `nev_`, `ndl_` and `ndg_`). `NotificationEvents`: the kind, the subject (the upstream, destination, certificate or user ID it is about, as text), the title, body, severity, dashboard link path and the webhook's facts (a JSON array of pairs), when it happened and when it was published: what a sender needs to build the message again after a restart, and the history's backbone. `NotificationDeliveries`: the event, the channel (**no foreign key**, so the history outlives a deleted channel), the state (`pending`, `sent`, `failed`, `expired`, `dropped`, by name with a check), attempts, next attempt, last attempt, sent at, the last error (the senders' fixed, secret-free text) and the digest ID. Indexes: **(channel, state, next attempt)** for the worker's claim and for "is a retry waiting"; **(event, channel), unique**, so an event is addressed to a channel once and the history's deliveries, its channel and outcome filters and retention's "no pending delivery" are probes; **(channel, ID)** for a channel's latest deliveries; **(created)** on events for the history's range and retention. `EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN` tests pin the claim, the retry look, the recovery, the retention reads and deletes, the history page, a channel's recent deliveries and the seven-day figures to those indexes (the one statement that scans, a start dropping the pending deliveries of channels that no longer exist, is named in its test).
- **Routing at flush time.** One read of the enabled channels per flush (not one per event; a test counts the statements), then for each event one `pending` delivery per enabled channel subscribed to its kind, in the same transaction as the event. An event nothing subscribes to is stored with no delivery, so the history shows it ("No channel was subscribed").
- **Workers.** One per channel (a task, created by the first wake or at start for a channel with pending rows). The flusher wakes it in process; a slow poll (`PollInterval`, 30 s) is the safety net, and a retry's own time is the usual wake. **The first send goes at once** (the agreed design; an earlier build waited the window before every first send): a channel that has not sent within the last `CoalesceWindow` (3 s) claims what is due the moment it is woken, and only a channel that has (a burst) waits out the rest of the window and then **claims the due `pending` rows** (`next attempt <= now`, in due order, at most `ClaimBatchSize` 500 a pass, going on at once when a pass was full). **Coalescing:** the first event of a burst goes alone and the second and later ones, which arrive inside the window, go together: events of one digestible kind (health, expiry, ACME) that are several go out as one digest with the same text (**a storm of more than 500 is one digest per claim batch**, not one message), and the rows of a digest get its **digest ID before anything is sent and keep it through retries**, so a resend after a failure or a crash is the same message with the same ID; sign-in events and tests always go singly. **Retry is as before** (15 s, 1, 4 and 16 minutes after a transient failure; a permanent one is final at once) but kept in the row (attempts, next attempt), so it survives a restart. **While a channel has a retry waiting, its newer deliveries wait behind it** (order is kept: an "up" never overtakes its "down") and are digested with it when it comes due. One process, one worker per channel and one send at a time: a row cannot be sent twice in process, and every row update says `State = pending`, so a delivery dropped meanwhile stays dropped. A channel's settings are read again before each group (an edit, a deletion and a pause take effect). A message already in flight when its channel is paused or deleted is sent, and its row stays `dropped` (the pause or the delete marks every pending row, in flight or not, and recording a send touches only rows still pending): the history can show a dropped delivery that did reach the service, which is the safe side of at-least-once. A worker whose channel is gone marks its pending rows `dropped` ("The channel was deleted.") and leaves; a paused one marks them "The channel is paused."; `NotificationService` does the same in the transaction of the delete or the pause, so the history is right at once.
- **Restart and outages.** At start, pending rows resume, the pending rows of channels that no longer exist are dropped and **rows that have been due for longer than `Paduraksa:Notifications:MaxDeliveryAge` (1 hour; 1 minute to 7 days, validated) are marked `expired` instead of sent**, so a long outage does not end in a burst of stale "upstream down" messages. **The same rule is applied when a worker claims**, so events that waited out a database outage in the flusher's buffer (they keep their publish time) are expired when the database returns, not sent. "Due for" is the row's due time: a new row is due from the moment its event was published (the event's age), and a manual retry is due from the retry, so an old event sent again on purpose is not expired again. A row whose event is missing (retention keeps an event with a pending delivery, so it should not happen) is marked `dropped` ("The event is gone.") at claim instead of being found every pass. **Recovery heals itself:** a start whose recovery failed is retried by every upkeep tick, and each tick wakes every channel with a due `pending` row (one seek per channel on the claim index), so a channel is never left without a worker whatever happened at boot. The certificate-expiry monitor's own dedup (`ExpiryNotices`) is unchanged.
- **Guarantee: at least once.** A crash between a successful send and the row update sends again at the next start (a test simulates it: the process is stopped in the middle of a send). Writing a delivered message down is outside the send's error handling: the rows are updated first, then the channel's last-sent state, and a failure of either is logged and is never counted as a send failure (a test makes the channel-state write throw a non-database exception). A **webhook** body carries `eventId` and the request `X-Paduraksa-Event-Id` (both signed with the rest when the channel has a key): the event's ID, or the digest's when several events went out as one message, **the same on every resend**, so a receiver drops the repeat. Other kinds have no such key. The "send test" button does not go through the outbox and carries no event ID. Documented in install.md.
- **Retention.** `Paduraksa:Notifications:HistoryRetention` (14 days; 1 hour to 5 years, validated) prunes events **with** their deliveries once the event is older and **no delivery is pending** (every other state is final, `failed` included, since it was given up), hourly, `PruneBatchSize` (500) events at a time, each batch in its own transaction, with indexed reads and deletes. A pending delivery keeps its event whatever its age, until a claim or the next start expires it. **The prune is safe against a manual retry:** it chooses the events outside its transaction, so inside it deletes only deliveries that are not pending and then only events left with no delivery at all; a delivery put back to pending in between, and its event, are never touched (a test races them). The retry itself answers 422 if retention removed the row under it.
- **History API.** `GET /api/v1/notifications/history` (`providers:read`, the permission of the channels): events newest first, each with its deliveries (the channel's name and kind, absent for a deleted one; state, attempts, the next try of a pending one, the last attempt, when it was sent, the last error), filtered by `channel`, `kind`, `outcome` (an event with a delivery in that state, to that channel when one is given) and `from` and `to` (when it was published), with `limit` and `cursor` as the audit log's. **Retry:** `POST /api/v1/notifications/deliveries/{id}/retry` and `POST /api/v1/notifications/events/{id}/retry` (every failed or expired delivery of it) (`providers:write`): the row goes back to `pending`, due now, with a fresh schedule and no digest ID, and the channel's worker is woken; refused with 422 for a delivery that is not failed or expired, and for one whose channel is deleted or paused (it would only be dropped again). **Audited** (`notification.delivery.retry`, `notification.event.retry`: who, which delivery and event and channel, or how many). The channel list (`GET /notification-channels`) gains `delivery` (one channel's `GET` does not: the figures read every channel's deliveries): the deliveries queued now and the sent and failed (failed or expired) counts of the last seven days, by when each ended (a final row keeps its end time in `NextAttemptAt`, so the figures are a covering range scan of the claim index with no join). Contracts `<summary>` docs are in OpenAPI. **MCP:** the history GET is a reviewed read (`EndpointRules.Reviewed`: our own messages and the senders' fixed lines, no secret and no channel setting) and each retry a write that needs `confirm`; the OpenAPI walk test passes with them listed.
- **Metrics.** Through the existing `ProcessCounters` path (so `/metrics` and the OpenTelemetry meter both show them; `ProcessCounter` gained a `Gauge` flag): `paduraksa_notification_queue_depth` (pending deliveries, a gauge: counted in memory as rows are added and ended and counted again from the table on every poll, so a delivery dropped by a channel change cannot make it drift), and `paduraksa_notification_sent_total`, `_failed_total`, `_expired_total` and `_dropped_total`, each with a `kind` label (ntfy, gotify, discord, webhook, email: **by the kind of channel, never its name**, so a label does not grow with the config), plus `paduraksa_notification_buffer_dropped_total`.
- **Dashboard.** **Settings, Notification history** (`/settings/notifications/history`, `providers:read`; its own navigation entry after Notifications, and a History button on that page). **Decision: under Settings, not under Insight.** Insight's pages are read-only logs behind their own permissions (metrics, audit, logs); this one is read with the channels' permission, carries the retry the way the channels page carries "Send test", and is linked from the channel list and the channel drawer, so it sits with them and its address agrees with its navigation entry. **Page:** a filter card (the shared `SegmentedControl` for the time range, 1 h / 24 h / 7 d / All, and for the outcome, any / sent / failed / retrying / expired / dropped, then a channel and an event select; "Clear filters"), and a list, newest first, 50 at a time with "Load older": each event row is its time, its title with a kind badge and its subject, and a **chip per channel**: sent (a tick), failed ("Mail failed", the error and the attempts in its tooltip), queued or "retrying, next 14:03 UTC", expired, dropped (a deleted channel is named "Deleted Webhook channel"). **Retry:** a 36 px refresh button beside each failed or expired chip (`providers:write` only) and "Send all again" on an event with several; a refusal is said in a snackbar in the API's words. The first page is read again every 8 s (a retrying delivery changes by itself) and replaces the list when nothing older was opened, and otherwise offers "Show the latest". The channel links (`?channel=` and `&outcome=`) set the filters. **The channel list** adds, on each row, "N queued", "N failed" (a link to those in the history) and "98.5% delivered, 7 d" (sent out of sent and failed, rounded down, never 100 while something failed); **the channel drawer** adds "Recent deliveries" (its latest eight with a link to all of them). Phone width: the row is one column. **Tests:** bUnit (`NotificationHistoryTests`, 14: the chips and tooltips, the filters and the query, retry and "send all", a refusal, the poll and the older pages, the empty and failed states, the figures and the drawer's list, the rate and the wording) and Playwright (`NotificationHistoryEndToEndTests`: a refused delivery shows failed with its error, a click delivers it with the same event ID twice at the receiver, and the channel row and drawer show the outcome).
- **Left out.** Counts on the outcome segments (a count per outcome is a query per segment; the filter finds them); a delivery's detail drawer (the chip's tooltip carries what a person acts on); per-channel quiet hours and severity filters (as before); a cross-process claim (one process, one worker per channel is the rule, and a second process on the same data directory is already refused by the run lock); exporting the history (the API pages it).

### 7.5 HTTP/3 strategy

**Spike S1 (see `docs/spikes/S1-http3-sni.md`) showed that per-SNI selection and hot-swap work on Kestrel's QUIC listener in .NET 10**, through `ServerCertificateSelector`, `TlsHandshakeCallbackOptions` and `ServerCertificateSelectionCallback` alike. So every host that enables H3 gets it, and the single-H3-cert fallback isn't needed.

- **Listener layout:** two `Listen` calls on :443 (dual-mode `[::]`).
  - TCP: `Http1AndHttp2`, `DisableAltSvcHeader = true`, then the connection middleware (PROXY protocol, IP gate), then `TlsHandshakeCallbackOptions`.
  - UDP: `Http3` with its own `TlsHandshakeCallbackOptions`, which also performs the stage-A gate. It's added only if `QuicListener.IsSupported`.
- **Builder:** the public app runs on the generic host with `UseKestrelCore()`, which leaves out Kestrel's HTTPS configuration and the QUIC transport. `UsePublicHttpsTransports()` adds both (`UseKestrelHttpsConfiguration()` and `UseQuic()`); without them H3 listeners fail at startup. *Spike history:* S1 found this under `WebApplication.CreateSlimBuilder`, which has the same gap.
- **Still open:** a real browser upgrading to H3 (needs a trusted cert), routable IPv6, and arm64 libmsquic. Carrying the QUIC-callback ClientInfo through to the request works: a feature set on the QUIC connection context in the callback is visible to the H3 request (`PublicHttp3Tests`, Docker).
- **As built in M1:** `UsePublicHttpsTransports()` on the generic host's `UseKestrelCore()`, then `ListenPublicHttp` and `ListenPublicHttps` (§3). `run-quic-tests.sh` runs the QUIC tests of `Tests.Proxy` and an end-to-end H3 request through `PaduraksaHost` in Docker with libmsquic.
- **Either way, `Alt-Svc` is emitted by Paduraksa, not Kestrel.** Kestrel's automatic header is disabled (`DisableAltSvcHeader`). Our header is precomputed per host, only for H3-eligible hosts, using the **public** HTTPS port from settings (default 443), not the bound port. That way a port-mapped deployment can't advertise the wrong port.
- H3 is off globally if `libmsquic` isn't available (`QuicListener.IsSupported`). `paduraksa doctor` reports this. The stock `aspnet` image doesn't ship it: install `libmsquic` from packages.microsoft.com (≈ 21 MB incl. `libnuma1`; tested 2.6.3 on Ubuntu 24.04).

### 7.6 Client IP behind Cloudflare (native)

Many homelabs sit behind Cloudflare's proxy (orange cloud) or a Cloudflare Tunnel. Getting the real client IP must be a toggle, not a hand-maintained CIDR list. It's a built-in **trusted-proxy provider**, which the generic trusted-proxy model (CIDRs + header) and future providers also use.

- **Modes:** `Off` · `Proxy` (Cloudflare connects to us directly) · `Tunnel` (a local `cloudflared` connects to us). Set globally and overridable per host (§5.3); M1 has the global setting only (`DefaultsSpec.TrustedProxies`: mode, trusted CIDRs, generic header, `True-Client-IP` and X-Forwarded-For fallbacks), and the per-host override comes with M2/M7. The wizard asks "Is this server behind Cloudflare?" and offers to set it.
- **Who is trusted:**
  - `Proxy`: the TCP peer (after PROXY protocol, if any) must fall within **Cloudflare's published edge ranges**. These are shipped as a baked-in list and refreshed by a background job from `https://api.cloudflare.com/client/v4/ips`, falling back to `https://www.cloudflare.com/ips-v4` / `ips-v6`. They're stored with a fetch timestamp and compiled into the snapshot as a CIDR trie. A failed refresh keeps the last good list and raises a dashboard warning if it's stale (more than 30 days old).
  - `Tunnel`: the peer must be the configured `cloudflared` address (loopback, a Docker network CIDR or a specific IP). Edge ranges don't apply because the TCP peer is `cloudflared`.
- **Which header:** `CF-Connecting-IP` (single value, so there's no X-Forwarded-For chain parsing). Optionally `True-Client-IP` (Enterprise) or the right-most untrusted entry of `X-Forwarded-For` as a fallback. The value is parsed span-based into the same `UInt128` client-IP representation used everywhere, with IPv4-mapped addresses normalised. A missing or unparsable header from a trusted peer counts as a counter + debug log, and the peer IP is used.
- **Anti-spoofing:** when the peer is **not** trusted, **every `cf-*` header** (Cloudflare adds more over time, and Cloudflare Access's `Cf-Access-Jwt-Assertion` / `Cf-Access-Authenticated-User-Email` carry identity) and `True-Client-IP` are ignored, and they're **stripped before forwarding upstream**. Upstreams always get a clean `X-Forwarded-For` / `X-Real-IP` built from the resolved client IP (§7.2 E).
- **What the real IP feeds:** WAF IP allow/deny, geo, rate-limit keys, access lists, forward-auth headers, access logs, metrics. Connection-stage limits (§7.2 A) necessarily see the Cloudflare edge IP. They must therefore exempt trusted Cloudflare peers from per-IP connection caps, or every visitor would share a handful of edge IPs.
- **Optional extras (per host or global):**
  - **Geo from `CF-IPCountry`:** when the peer is trusted, use it instead of the local Geo DB lookup. It's free and accurate. `XX` maps to "unknown" and `T1` to a Tor pseudo-country rules can name (built in M7, the only country source of that wave: §7.3, **As built (M7): country rules**).
  - **`CF-Visitor` scheme:** the original scheme for the HTTP→HTTPS redirect decision when Cloudflare connects to the origin over plain HTTP ("Flexible" SSL, or `cloudflared` forwarding to port 80). This avoids redirect loops. Built in M1 (§7.2 C) and always on in the Cloudflare modes; Flexible is counted and logged once, and the dashboard (M5) will warn that it is insecure.
  - **Origin lockdown:** reject (or 403) requests on a Cloudflare-mode host whose peer isn't Cloudflare (or `cloudflared`), so the origin can't be reached by IP to bypass Cloudflare's WAF. It's enforced at the connection stage when *all* hosts on a listener require it.
  - **Authenticated Origin Pulls:** request a client certificate from Cloudflare and verify it against Cloudflare's origin-pull CA (shipped, replaceable) on the TCP listener (§7.2 B, per-SNI `ClientCertificateRequired`). This is the strong form of origin lockdown.
  - **`CF-Ray`** is recorded in access logs and offered as a variable on error pages, next to our request ID, so issues can be correlated with the Cloudflare dashboard.
- **Certificates:** Cloudflare Origin CA certificates can be uploaded like any other (§9), with a hint that they're only trusted by Cloudflare. DNS-01 via the Cloudflare DNS provider (§9) works whether or not the proxy mode is on.
- **Request simulator** (§7.7) takes a "via Cloudflare" toggle, so users can see which IP and country the policies would see.

**As built (M7): Cloudflare ranges, origin lockdown and Authenticated Origin Pulls.**

*The host setting.* `HostSettings.Cloudflare` (`CloudflareSettings`, last field, merged field by field like the other nested records) has `originLockdown` (`off` default, `forbid`, `drop`; the shared enum `OriginLockdownMode`) and `authenticatedOriginPulls` (bool, default off). Both only take effect when the global trusted-proxy mode is `CloudflareProxy` or `CloudflareTunnel`: the compiler resolves them to `Off` otherwise, so the gate never looks at the mode. A host that sets either under `Off` or `Generic` gets the warning `cloudflare.needsMode`; Authenticated Origin Pulls on a host with no HTTPS (`cloudflare.aop.needsHttps`) or under a Tunnel (`cloudflare.aop.tunnel`: `cloudflared` presents no client certificate) can only refuse and says so.

*Edge-range refresh.* `CloudflareRangeRefresher` (Application, a hosted service of the core host; `Paduraksa:Cloudflare:RangeRefresh:Enabled`, default true, with the URLs, start delay and interval overridable) fetches `https://api.cloudflare.com/client/v4/ips` (`result.ipv4_cidrs`/`ipv6_cidrs`), and the plain-text `ips-v4`/`ips-v6` when that fails or its list is refused: two minutes after start, then daily with up to an hour of jitter; a failure retries after 5 minutes, doubling to 6 hours. Each request has a 15 s timeout, no redirects and a 64 KiB body cap. A list is accepted only if `EdgeRangeList.TryCreate` passes it: every entry a strict CIDR with a prefix, at least three IPv4 and three IPv6 blocks, nothing wider than /8 (IPv4) or /12 (IPv6) (so no `0.0.0.0/0`), at most 512. Otherwise it is logged, counted as a failure and ignored, and the last good list stays. An accepted list is stored with its fetch time in `<data dir>/cloudflare-ranges.json` (atomic write; a read goes through the same checks, so a damaged or edited file is ignored) and is in force from the first compile after a restart. Proxy never touches the network or the disk: `CloudflareEdgeRangeSource` (a process singleton) holds the current list and the failure count, `SnapshotCompilerOptions.CloudflareRanges` points the compiler at it, and `RuntimeSnapshot.EdgeRanges` records the list a snapshot was compiled with (null unless the mode is `CloudflareProxy`, the only one that trusts them). **A changed list is not a config change**: the job calls `ConfigService.RepublishLiveAsync`, which compiles the live document again and publishes it with no new revision, exactly as a certificate change does (§9.1); it does so only while the live snapshot trusts the edge ranges, and a refetch of the same blocks only moves the list's age. The publish is **level-triggered**: `RepublishLiveAsync` reports whether it queued one, and a change (a new list, a new warning, or a live snapshot whose list differs from the one in force) stays pending until it did, tried again on the failure backoff and at every refresh, so one failed or skipped attempt can't leave a revoked range trusted. The built-in list (`CloudflareIpRanges`) is the list in use until a fetched one is stored.

*The warning.* In `CloudflareProxy` mode the compile adds `cloudflare.ranges.stale` (at `/settings/trustedProxies`, so it shows where config warnings do) when the list in use is older than 30 days or three or more refreshes in a row failed; the job compiles again when that warning appears, changes or clears. The dashboard's client-address card says "Cloudflare ranges: updated 3 days ago (22 ranges)", "built-in list from 2026-11-06 (22 ranges)" or the warning, and the overview's attention list carries the warning. The simulator's client-address step has an `edgeRanges` fact (which list, how many ranges, when it was fetched and how many days ago).

*Origin lockdown.* A request for a host with lockdown on whose peer (the PROXY-protocol source when there is one, else the socket) is not a trusted peer of the snapshot's policy is refused before anything else of the host: right after the host lookup and its `Server` header, before the TLS check, the policies and the redirect. `forbid` answers 403 with a built-in page, `drop` aborts the request; both are constants, so the decision allocates nothing. It uses `ClientIpResolution.PeerTrusted`, the decision the client-IP resolution already made, and a forged `CF-Connecting-IP` or `X-Forwarded-For` from an untrusted peer changes nothing. An unknown host is the default behaviour, not a lockdown.

*At the connection stage.* The compiler decides once per snapshot whether **every** host in it (the enabled ones; an admin host counts like any other) is locked down (`RuntimeSnapshot.LockdownAtConnection`). Then an untrusted peer is dropped before TLS: `OriginLockdownMiddleware` on the HTTPS TCP listener (after `UseClientInfo`, so the address is the PROXY-protocol source's) and the QUIC handshake gate (`OriginLockdown.CreateQuicGate`, which also publishes the `ClientInfo`). Both modes drop there, since nothing can be answered before TLS; the TCP drop allocates the abort exception per connection like the PROXY-protocol one (the connection itself allocates far more), and both are counted in `OriginLockdownCounters`. **The plain-HTTP listener has no connection-stage check on purpose.** An ACME HTTP-01 validation reaches a DNS-only record from the CA's own address, never from Cloudflare, so the gate answers a published challenge token before any host decision (as it does for every request, §7.2 C) and leaves everything else of a locked-down host refused per request; behind a proxied (orange-cloud) record the validation arrives from Cloudflare's ranges and passes either way. A locked-down admin host is a host like any other: only reachable through Cloudflare, and the dashboard row says it covers the admin host but not the admin port (9443), which is a different listener. The lock-out guard (§3.1) models a caller of a locked-down admin host as arriving through Cloudflare (otherwise its probe would never have been forwarded and the guard would let a deletion through), and a change that adds lockdown to the admin host is refused for a caller who may have come directly.

*Authenticated Origin Pulls.* Cloudflare's zone-level origin-pull CA (`https://developers.cloudflare.com/ssl/static/authenticated_origin_pull_ca.pem`, fetched 2026-11-11, self-signed, valid until 2029-11-01, SHA-256 `9A1AC2B4...25A2B9`) is an embedded resource (`cloudflare-origin-pull-ca.pem`, whose header comment records this) loaded by `OriginPullCa.Default`. A host with the setting gets its own TLS entry: `ClientCertificateRequired = true`, the chain built offline (no AIA or revocation fetch) with `X509ChainTrustMode.CustomRootTrust` against that CA alone, so validity dates are checked and a certificate from any other authority, a public one included, fails the handshake. The setting is part of the entry's deduplication key (the `TlsPolicy` record carries the `ClientCertificatePolicy`, which holds the roots by reference), so two such hosts on one certificate share options and an ordinary host on it does not. The gate's second line, before the TLS check, refuses a request on a connection that carries no verified client certificate (Kestrel sets `ITlsConnectionFeature.ClientCertificate` only when the handshake accepted one): with **421** when the connection is TLS and its SNI doesn't select the host's TLS entry (a coalesced connection, or no SNI at all: the handshake never asked, and Cloudflare's retry on a connection of its own will), like every other coalescing mismatch; with **403** for plain HTTP, which can't carry a certificate, and for a connection whose SNI does name the host and still has none. The host's entry sets `AllowTlsResume = false`: a resumed TLS 1.3 session may carry no client certificate, so those hosts do a full handshake every time (every other host keeps resumption). **Not over HTTP/3:** Cloudflare doesn't connect to origins over QUIC, so an Authenticated Origin Pulls host compiles with `Http3Enabled = false` whatever its own setting says: no `Alt-Svc` for it (the header step says why), no QUIC options (its QUIC handshake is refused and counted as `Http3Disabled`), and an HTTP/3 request for it on another name's connection is refused. The CA can be replaced without a release by `Paduraksa:Cloudflare:OriginPullCaFile` (a PEM file read once at startup, relative to the content root; one that holds no certificate stops the start). **Gap:** no admin upload of a replacement through the certificate machinery: the inventory models server certificates with keys, and a trust anchor without one is a new kind of object, so it stays a deployment setting for now. Cloudflare's own zone setting must be turned on too, or no request gets through; the dashboard row says so.

*Simulator.* `GateStepKind.OriginLockdown` ("peer 1.2.3.4 is not a Cloudflare edge address: refused (403)", or in Tunnel mode "not the configured cloudflared address") and `GateStepKind.ClientCertificate` are new steps; the listener's admission step also reports the connection-stage drop (HTTPS before TLS, after PROXY protocol; QUIC after the handshake's name selection, as the callback does), and the handshake step fails when the entry requires a client certificate the probe doesn't present. `GateProbe.ClientCertificate` defaults to "presented exactly when the arrival is `CloudflareProxy`", so the existing "via Cloudflare" arrival gives both outcomes and a direct or tunnel arrival the refusals. `GateSimulator.Run` takes the time the edge-range age is read against.
