*Tests.* `OriginLockdownTests` (compiler resolution, both modes, tunnel, PROXY protocol source, forged headers, order before the TLS check and policies, the ACME exception, the connection stage on a real TCP listener and the QUIC gate, simulator parity, 0 B on every path, also on Kestrel in `GateAllocationTests`), `AuthenticatedOriginPullsTests` (a test CA standing in for Cloudflare's, handed in through `SnapshotCompilerOptions.OriginPullCa`: valid, absent, foreign, expired and self-signed certificates, no AIA fetch, the coalesced and non-SNI refusals, dedup keys, no `Alt-Svc`, the shipped CA's identity, simulator and 0 B), `CloudflareEdgeRangesTests`, `CloudflareRangeRefresherTests` (a fake Cloudflare: JSON, text fallback, refused lists, size, timeout, backoff, republish only on change) and `CloudflareRangeRefreshHostTests` (a real host against a loopback Cloudflare, including the restart). The QUIC cases (`KestrelQuicTlsTests`, trait `Transport=Quic`) run only through `run-quic-tests.sh`.

The security overview's per-host row (`HostProtections.OriginLockdown`, `AuthenticatedOriginPulls`, read from `CompiledHost`) has a Cloudflare column: "Cloudflare only (403)" or "Cloudflare only (drop)" and "Origin pulls", or None; both are off in the compiled host unless the mode is a Cloudflare one, so the column says what the gate does.

### 7.7 Request simulator ("Why was this blocked?")

Admins test their rules before or after a change by asking what a request would get, and why. **The simulator runs the real gate code over a real compiled snapshot, never a reimplementation**, so its answer can't drift from what production does.

- **Input:** URL (scheme, host, port, path, query), method, headers (including the user agent and cookies), the peer IP plus how it arrives (direct, through a trusted generic proxy, via Cloudflare Proxy or Tunnel, with or without PROXY protocol), and the TLS context (the name sent in the handshake, ALPN/HTTP version, TLS version). The surfaces fill in sensible defaults, so a URL and an IP are enough.
- **Target config:** the live snapshot, any stored revision, or a *draft*: a document or pending command compiled through the dry-run path (§6.1 steps 1-4) and never published. So "what would this change do to this request" is answerable before committing.
- **Output:** the outcome (forward to upstream X / fixed answer / redirect / 421 / drop) plus the **trace**: client-IP resolution (which header and hop were trusted), host match (exact/wildcard/default), the TLS-policy check, the host's HSTS (sent, or why not: plain HTTP, an IP literal or loopback name, or off), each policy in order with its verdict and reason, the HTTPS redirect, route match (which route, and the matching view of the path), and the upstream (cluster and the destinations currently healthy; load balancing is random, so not which one).
- **No cost on the hot path.** Gate steps report through a tracer passed as a **generic struct type parameter**. Production uses a no-op tracer, which the JIT removes entirely; the simulator uses a recording one. The 0 B allocation tests and the gate benchmarks guard it.
- **Stateful and outbound policies (M7 on)** are simulated without side effects: a rate limit reports "would allow, N tokens left" without taking one; forward-auth reports "would call `<provider>`" (as built, never the real call: **As built (M7): forward-auth**); geo shows the country it resolved (the client step's `country` fact and the access-list step's, **As built (M7): country rules**).
- **Parity tests:** for generated configs and requests, the simulator's outcome must equal the real gate's (the M2 `CompiledBehaviourTests` harness is the seed). Each policy added later (M7) must add its trace step and parity cases.
- **Surfaces:** one Application function, `SimulateAsync(target, request)`. It is exposed as `POST /api/v1/simulate` (M4), as the MCP `simulate_request` tool (M10), and in the dashboard simulator page (M5). Access-log entries link into it with the request pre-filled (M9).

**As built (M4, simulator core).** The tracer is `IGateTracer`, passed as a struct type parameter to the one decision body, `GateMiddleware.Decide<TTracer>(context, snapshot, clientAddresses, challenges, tracer)`; production's `Decide(context)` passes `NoGateTracer` (empty methods, so the JIT drops them), and the Kestrel 0 B tests plus a direct `Decide<NoGateTracer>` allocation test guard it. The gate reports client resolution (peer, source, trusted header, scheme source), the ACME hook, the host match (`HostTable.Find(..., out HostMatchKind)`), the TLS-policy check (`ConnectionTlsCheck.Check` names the 421 reason; `Satisfies` is `Check == None`), each policy by scope and index, the HTTPS redirect, fixed hosts, the route match with the canonical path, and the upstream hand-over. `Paduraksa.Proxy.Simulation.GateSimulator.Run(snapshot, probe, challenges, listeners)` runs the real `Decide` with the recording tracer, after the stand-ins for what the server does first, in the server's order: the listener's admission (`ProxyProtocolAdmission.Check`, the connection middleware's own decision, from `ListenerFacts`: a PROXY header the listener doesn't expect, a missing one, an untrusted socket, or no HTTP/3 listener is `ConnectionRejected`); the TLS handshake (`TlsHandshakeSelector.Select` over the snapshot's certificate table, which the listeners call too, plus the entry's own TLS-version and ALPN options: unknown SNI, HTTP/3 off, a TLS 1.2 client to a TLS 1.3-only name, the h2 to http/1.1 fallback); and Kestrel's request checks (`RejectedByServer`, 400: on HTTP/1.1 anything but exactly one valid `Host`; on HTTP/2 and HTTP/3 `:authority` is the host and a `Host` header is ignored; the path is percent-decoded except `%2F` (a `%5C` becomes a backslash), escapes that aren't UTF-8 stay as written, and dot segments are removed, all checked against the real server over sockets in `SimulatorSocketParityTests`). A snapshot not bound to YARP gets placeholder endpoints. Client resolution is `TrustedProxyClientAddressSource.ResolveCore` with no counters and no log line, so a simulation changes no counter. `RequestSimulator.SimulateAsync(target, request, origin)` (Application) takes live, `RevisionSimulationTarget`, `DraftDocumentSimulationTarget` (a `ReplaceConfigCommand` on the current head, retried once on a commit in between) or `DraftCommandSimulationTarget`; drafts go through `ConfigService.PlanDraftAsync`, which is `AttemptAsync` as a dry run that hands back its compiled candidate, so validation, certificate rules and compile errors are the real command's and are returned as `Invalid` with the dry run's errors, while a stale version or missing object is `DraftRejected`; a command that changes nothing simulates the head it planned on. The same checks that reading applies (`apiVersion`, null list elements) run on any payload first. A corrupt head, a degraded store or an unreadable database is `Unavailable` with a reason, never an exception. Nothing is audited, stored or published. Request limits are the public listeners' outer bounds (20 KiB of request line, 84 KiB and 128 headers, a token method of at most 32 characters, no control characters in the query, no backslash in the authority; the host's own limits are the gate's, traced as the **Request limits** step), counted as Kestrel counts them: the request line, URL and header names and values in UTF-8 wire bytes, so a character above ASCII costs more than one (and the simulator is conservative where a header could be sent as Latin-1). A field that would change nothing (`transportIp` without PROXY protocol, `peerIp` on a direct request, `tls` on a plain-HTTP URL) is refused, not ignored. At most 4 drafts or revisions compile at once (`RequestSimulator.MaxConcurrentCompiles`); one that waits more than 2 s is `Unavailable`; the live target takes no slot. Authorization: `ISimulationAuthorizer` is asked for every target (see §10). Parity: `SimulatorParityTests` (Proxy: every outcome, 421 variants, all trusted-proxy modes and header shapes, PROXY protocol, ACME answered and fall-through), `SimulatedBehaviourTests` (Unit: 300 generated documents against the `GateHarness`), `SimulatorSocketParityTests` (real sockets) and end-to-end tests through a real host.

**Stateful policies must not mutate when simulated (M7 rule).** (The access-list policy follows it: its step is described in §7.3.) The simulator calls `Evaluate` on the live policy instances. `RequestView.IsSimulation` is true on that path and a constant `false` in production (a static abstract member of the tracer type, folded away by the JIT). A policy with state peeks: a rate limit reports "would allow, N tokens left" without taking one, a challenge issues no nonce, forward-auth starts no call unless the request opts in. Built-in policies are stateless today, so this is the seam plus the rule; each M7 policy adds its `Name`/`Explain` trace hooks and a parity case that checks it left its state alone.

**As built (M6): one response-headers step.** `IGateTracer.ResponseHeaders(context, host, operations, outcomes)` reports a stage of the host's compiled header list as `GateStepKind.ResponseHeaders` (contract `SimulationStepKind.ResponseHeaders`, "Response headers" in the trace view), and the later stage extends the step the earlier one opened: it lists each of `Alt-Svc`, `Strict-Transport-Security`, `Server` and the response `X-Request-Id` that the answer carries, with its value, and each one it doesn't, with the reason (off, no HTTP/3, plain HTTP, an IP literal or loopback name, the upstream's own, removed, or `the host's mode`). The request ID is reported as sent when the response is written, because that is when its one string is materialised. This replaced the separate HSTS and `Server` steps. `SimulatorParityTests` compares the headers the real gate put on the response with the values the trace reports, for every host, scheme and negotiated connection it sweeps, loopback and IP-literal names included; `GateSimulatorTraceTests` covers the modes and the reasons.

Decisions and gaps. (1) **No key is loaded for a draft or a revision.** Whether a certificate is usable is decided from what the vault describes (`ICertificateCatalog.DescribeAsync`: public facts, no key opened): a certificate the vault doesn't hold, the local CA root, or a chain outside its validity period is unusable with the real loader's reasons (`CertificateInventoryLoader.ForSimulation` shares the validity logic), so a draft binding to one is refused as a real command refuses it, and Auto hosts pick the same certificate. Built-in (placeholder) certificates count as served by this process. What is served is a self-signed placeholder per ID (`SimulationCertificateSource`). The handshake step names the inventory certificate (`ServerCertificate.Id`, set by `CertificateInventoryLoader`; a context not built by the loader has an empty ID and isn't named). Without a catalog (no vault registered) every certificate the table calls valid counts as usable. When the certificate table can't be read the result says so. (2) **Destination health is live YARP state** (`IUpstreamHealth`, over `IProxyStateLookup`, attached by the public app through `DeferredUpstreamHealth`): a destination of the target config reports the live proxy's answer for the destination with that ID **and address**, `Unknown` otherwise, with a note (the "can't be known for a config that isn't live" note only for non-live targets); the destination that serves a request is never named. A destination the live proxy keeps out of rotation is `Unhealthy`; YARP counts a destination with no health data yet as available. (3) **The ACME HTTP-01 answer is traced for real**: `GateSimulator.Run` takes the live `AcmeChallengeStore` and the gate's own `TryAnswerAcmeChallenge` answers or falls through; challenges are process state, not config, so every target uses the live store. (4) Trace facts use stable camelCase wire names (`TraceNames`) for the gate's own values, and header facts use the header's own name (`Server`, `Strict-Transport-Security`, ...), because that is what the person is looking at on the wire; policies name themselves explicitly (`static`, `ipAllowList`). (5) Per-host trusted-proxy overrides don't exist yet, and stateful or outbound policies (M7) aren't there to simulate. (6) The probe takes the HTTP and TLS versions as what the client negotiates; an HTTP/2 request to a name that offers only http/1.1 is reported as the HTTP/1.1 request it becomes. A request DTO can't say "no Host header" (the probe can). (7) An admin host's implicit route (PLAN §3.1) forwards to the admin app: the trace says so and `GateUpstream.Admin` is true with the `__admin` cluster, rather than naming a user upstream; the parity suite includes one. Not in M4 core: MCP and the dashboard page.

---

## 8. Vault (key-material trust boundary)

### 8.1 What it holds
TLS private keys (server certs **and** upstream mTLS client certs) · ACME account keys · EAB HMAC keys · DNS provider credentials · TOTP secrets · internal CA key · Data Protection key ring (the dashboard's antiforgery and circuit state) · the PoW HMAC key (forward-auth needed none: **As built (M7): forward-auth**) · access-list users' password verifiers (PBKDF2, one credential per user: §8.2 **As built (M7): password credentials**).

### 8.2 Public surface (capability interfaces in `Paduraksa.Core`, all implemented `internal` in Vault)

The contracts are in `src/Paduraksa.Core/Vault` (sketch, with the refinements made when they were written):

```csharp
interface IServerCertificateSource   // the ONE sanctioned egress for serving: Kestrel needs the key in-process
{   ValueTask<SslStreamCertificateContext?> LoadForServingAsync(CertificateId id, CancellationToken ct); }

interface IClientCertificateSource   // second sanctioned egress: upstream mTLS, for SslClientAuthenticationOptions.ClientCertificateContext
{   ValueTask<SslStreamCertificateContext?> LoadForUpstreamAsync(CertificateId id, CancellationToken ct); }

interface ICertificateIssuance       // keys are generated inside, only CSR / public data leave
{   ValueTask<CsrResult> CreateKeyAndCsrAsync(CertificateId id, KeySpec spec, IReadOnlyList<string> names, CancellationToken ct);   // CsrResult carries a StagedKeyId
    ValueTask<CertificateInfo> AttachChainAsync(CertificateId id, StagedKeyId stagedKey, ReadOnlyMemory<byte> pemChain, CancellationToken ct);
    ValueTask<CertificateInfo> CreateSelfSignedAsync(CertificateId id, KeySpec spec, IReadOnlyList<string> names, TimeSpan validity, CancellationToken ct);
    ValueTask<CertificateInfo> EnsureLocalCaAsync(CertificateId id, KeySpec spec, TimeSpan validity, CancellationToken ct);   // the root is a normal certificate
    ValueTask<CertificateInfo> IssueFromLocalCaAsync(CertificateId id, KeySpec spec, IReadOnlyList<string> names, TimeSpan validity, CancellationToken ct);
    ValueTask<bool> DeleteAsync(CertificateId id, CancellationToken ct); }   // a deleted certificate takes its key with it

interface ICertificateImport         // uploads stream straight in; returns public info only
{   ValueTask<CertificateInfo> ImportAsync(CertificateId id, Stream pemOrPfx, ReadOnlyMemory<char> password, CancellationToken ct); }

interface ICertificateCatalog        // the vault describes what it holds: public facts per ID, no key opened (after a DB loss, an ACME crash window)
{   ValueTask<IReadOnlyList<VaultCertificate>> ListAsync(CancellationToken ct);                  // VaultCertificate = (CertificateId Id, bool IsLocalCaRoot, CertificateInfo Info)
    ValueTask<VaultCertificate?> DescribeAsync(CertificateId id, CancellationToken ct); }

interface IJwsSigner                 // ACME: Acme builds the JWS, the vault only computes the signature over it
{   ValueTask<AcmeAccountKeyInfo> CreateAccountKeyAsync(AcmeAccountId id, JwsAlgorithm alg, CancellationToken ct);   // public JWK + RFC 7638 thumbprint
    ValueTask<AcmeAccountKeyInfo?> GetAccountKeyAsync(AcmeAccountId id, CancellationToken ct);
    ValueTask<JwsFlattened> SignAsync(AcmeAccountId id, ReadOnlyMemory<byte> protectedHeader, ReadOnlyMemory<byte> payload, CancellationToken ct);
    ValueTask<bool> DeleteAccountKeyAsync(AcmeAccountId id, CancellationToken ct); }

interface IMacKey { MacAlgorithm Algorithm { get; } int TagLength { get; } int Sign(ReadOnlySpan<byte> data, Span<byte> tag); bool Verify(ReadOnlySpan<byte> data, ReadOnlySpan<byte> tag); }
interface IMacKeySource { ValueTask<IMacKey?> OpenAsync(SecretId id, SecretKind expected, CancellationToken ct); }   // refused on a kind mismatch

interface ISecretStore               // no Get(). Secrets go in, capabilities come out.
{   ValueTask<SecretId> PutAsync(SecretKind kind, ReadOnlyMemory<byte> value, SecretOptions options, CancellationToken ct);   // label, MAC algorithm, allowed hosts
    ValueTask<SecretMetadata> ReplaceAsync(SecretId id, ReadOnlyMemory<byte> value, SecretOptions? options, CancellationToken ct);
    ValueTask<bool> DeleteAsync(SecretId id, CancellationToken ct);
    ValueTask<SecretMetadata?> DescribeAsync(SecretId id, CancellationToken ct); }

interface ISecretHttpHandlerFactory  // DNS provider creds: the vault returns the whole transport; the credential never reaches the caller
{   HttpMessageHandler CreateAuthenticatingHandler(SecretId id, SecretInjection how, SecretHttpTransportOptions transport); }

interface IDataProtectionKeyStore    // the admin app's Data Protection ring: the element XML is the third sanctioned egress
{   ValueTask<IReadOnlyList<DataProtectionKey>> ReadAllAsync(CancellationToken ct);
    ValueTask StoreAsync(string friendlyName, string keyXml, CancellationToken ct); }

interface IPasswordCredential        // a handle on one user's password: it can be verified against and never read; it outlives a password change
{   PasswordCredentialId Id { get; }   bool IsSet { get; } }
interface IPasswordCredentials       // set, import, delete: no read of any kind
{   IPasswordCredential Open(PasswordCredentialId id);                                                       // sync, from memory
    ValueTask<PasswordChange> SetAsync(PasswordCredentialId id, ReadOnlyMemory<char> password, CancellationToken ct);
    ValueTask RevertAsync(IReadOnlyList<PasswordChange> changes, CancellationToken ct);   // puts each previous sealed row back exactly (or no row), last first
    void Keep(IReadOnlyList<PasswordChange> changes);                                      // the commit succeeded: forget what it took to undo
    ValueTask<IPasswordCredential> PrepareAsync(PasswordCredentialId id, ReadOnlyMemory<char> password, CancellationToken ct);   // a handle for a draft: nothing stored or cached
    IPasswordCredential PrepareImport(PasswordCredentialId id, string passwordVerifier);
    ValueTask<bool> ImportAsync(PasswordCredentialId id, string passwordVerifier, CancellationToken ct);   // an existing PBKDF2 string, as it is
    ValueTask<bool> DeleteAsync(PasswordCredentialId id, CancellationToken ct);
    ValueTask<IReadOnlyList<PasswordCredentialId>> ListAsync(CancellationToken ct); }
interface IPasswordVerifier          // the gate's check: a hit or miss without a lock or an allocation, and the slow PBKDF2 the caller schedules
{   PasswordCacheProbe TryVerifyCached(IPasswordCredential c, ReadOnlySpan<byte> username, ReadOnlySpan<byte> password, ReadOnlySpan<byte> client);
    PasswordVerdict Verify(IPasswordCredential? c, ReadOnlySpan<byte> username, ReadOnlySpan<byte> password, ReadOnlySpan<byte> client, bool remember); }
```

Notes on the refinements:
- **Serving flow.** The snapshot compiler is synchronous, so Application preloads the serving contexts (`LoadForServingAsync`) for the certificate inventory (§9.1) before each compile and gives the compiler an immutable `IServerCertificateResolver` over them. The proxy never sees the source, and the vault returns the same context instance for an unchanged certificate (the proxy deduplicates by instance). The vault never disposes an instance it handed out; a replaced one is dropped and collected. The context's `TargetCertificate` carries the key, so the guard against exporting it is the key-material ban list (`BannedSymbols.KeyMaterial.txt`), not the type.
- **Caller-chosen IDs, atomic replacement, staged keys.** Every way of replacing a certificate (`AttachChainAsync`, import, self-signed, local-CA re-issue) keeps its `CertificateId` and swaps key and chain in one transaction, so Specific bindings survive renewals. `CreateKeyAndCsrAsync` stages a key and returns a `StagedKeyId` that `AttachChainAsync` must present; a stale token throws `StagedKeyMismatchException`.
- **Import.** PEM must carry a PKCS#8 key (plain or encrypted), otherwise PFX under the vault's own `Pkcs12LoaderLimits`; at most 1 MiB is read; every failure surfaces as `CertificateImportException` with a reason. The leaf is also refused if it is an RSA key under 2048 bits (`WeakKey`) or a CA certificate (`CaCertificate`); an expired leaf is accepted (the inventory treats it as unusable until renewed). "Usable name" is decided by `ICertificateNameRules`, implemented by the inventory's own normaliser (`CertificateNameRules`) and handed to `AddPaduraksaVault`, so the vault never accepts names the inventory drops (a vault built without one uses its strict rule). The KDF cost of an encrypted PKCS#8 is bounded before it runs (PBKDF2 or PKCS#5/12 iteration count at most 600000, else `Unreadable`) and runs once per key type the certificates could have (normally once); everything found wrong while reading the certificate's extensions after parsing is `Unreadable`, never an exception.
- **Certificate metadata.** `CertificateInfo` has the SHA-256 fingerprint, the serial number as the DER INTEGER content bytes (leading `00` kept), the Authority Key Identifier (with the serial it is the RFC 9773 ARI certificate ID) and the issuers of the chain (for `preferred_chain` selection). The subject has a CN only for a DNS name of at most 64 characters, otherwise it is empty.
- **JWS.** Acme writes the protected header and payload; the vault base64url-encodes, signs (ES256/ES384 as fixed-width `R||S`, RS256 as PKCS#1 v1.5) and returns the flattened-JWS pieces. It refuses a header whose `alg` is not the key's. EAB's inner JWS is built by Acme and signed with an `IMacKey` (HS256). Account key rollover is deferred (§9).
- **MAC keys.** The algorithm is fixed in the secret's metadata when it is stored, and `OpenAsync` checks the expected kind. A handle resolves the current version on every call; `Verify` also accepts the previous version for a grace window after a rotation, `Sign` never does. `Verify` takes only whole tags; `VerifyTruncated` takes a prefix of 16 to full-length bytes (the proof-of-work token keeps the first 16 bytes of HMAC-SHA-256 on purpose), a separate method so a protocol whose tag length an attacker controls can never be checked against one byte.
- **Secrets carry their destinations.** `SecretOptions.AllowedHosts` (required for `DnsApiToken`) is set by the admin when creating the DNS provider and stored with the secret. The authenticating handler uses its intersection with the provider code's own `SecretInjection.AllowedHosts` (normalised host names, compared with `Uri.IdnHost`).
- **TOTP.** Since there is no get, M4's TOTP needs vault-side generation: the vault creates the secret, returns only the one-time provisioning URI for the QR code, and verifies codes itself.
  **As built (M4, `ITotpAuthenticator`).** `BeginEnrollmentAsync` generates 160 random bits, seals them as a `TotpSecret`, and returns the secret's ID and the `otpauth://` URI (a `Uri`: the one deliberate egress, once); `ConfirmEnrollmentAsync` turns a pending secret into a confirmed one with a code; `VerifyAsync` accepts codes for confirmed secrets only; `DeleteAsync` removes it. RFC 6238 with SHA-1, 6 digits, 30 s steps, one step of drift either way, every step compared in constant time. **Replay protection** is a `TotpStates.LastStep` column advanced by one conditional update, so a time step (and every earlier one) is accepted once per secret, across restarts, processes and simultaneous requests (vault migration `Totp`). No call returns the secret after enrolment, and the architecture test holds the contract to plain data. Recovery codes are not the vault's: ten 16-character codes (about 79 bits), shown once, stored as SHA-256 in the config DB, single use by one atomic update.
- **Async lookups, nullable misses.** `Describe` and the public-key lookups are async (they read the vault database), and a missing item returns `null`.

**As built (M3 vault certificates).**
- **Storage.** `CertificateKeys` holds one row per `CertificateId`: the PKCS#8 key sealed by the envelope cipher (AAD purpose `certificate-key`, or `certificate-key/local-ca` for the root, so a leaf row edited into the root fails to open), the public chain PEM (leaf first, intermediates, no root unless the certificate is its own root), a version that is both in the AAD and a concurrency token, and a unique-where-true `IsLocalCaRoot`. A replacement is an update of that row in one `SaveChanges`, which is what makes key and chain swap atomically. `StagedKeys` holds at most one row per certificate (the key sealed to its own `StagedKeyId`, plus the requested names): staging again updates the row in place, so the earlier token no longer names it. Every replacement route drops the ID's staged key in the same transaction, `DeleteAsync` takes both rows, and KEK rotation re-wraps both tables (counted as `CertificateKeys` and `StagedKeys`). `CertificateInfo` is recomputed from the stored chain; no metadata is duplicated.
- **Chains.** `AttachChainAsync` takes the first certificate as the leaf, requires its public key to be the staged key's and its SANs to cover every requested name (exact, or the wildcard of the parent; a wildcard does not cover the apex or a deeper name), also requires it to be valid now (not expired, not starting later than a five-minute tolerance), orders the rest behind it, and throws `CryptographicException` otherwise (the staged key keeps waiting); no staged row for the token is `StagedKeyMismatchException`, including after a replacement or delete. A certificate counts as the issuer of the one before only if the names link up, the key identifiers agree where both carry them, the issuer is a CA and its key verifies the signature (ECDSA or RSA PKCS#1 v1.5 with SHA-2); strays, same-name impostors, intermediates expired now and the root are dropped. Imports assemble the chain the same way.
- **Vault-signed certificates.** SAN (critical when the subject is empty), serverAuth EKU, key usage (`digitalSignature`, plus `keyEncipherment` for RSA), SKI, AKI (the root's SKI for a leaf), CA:false; serials are 126 random bits (the top two bits of 128 are fixed so the integer is positive and needs no sign byte); validity starts five minutes early. The root has CA:true with path length 0, `keyCertSign`/`cRLSign`, and the name `Paduraksa Local CA (<8 hex>)`; a leaf's validity is capped at the root's remaining validity and, for local-CA leaves and self-signed certificates, silently at 825 days (what Apple's trust store accepts); the root is not capped. The root has no name constraints and path length 0; name constraints (limiting it to the user's own domains) are deferred. The root is never served (`LoadForServingAsync` of its ID is `null`), cannot be replaced or staged over, and `EnsureLocalCaAsync` returns the existing one regardless of the ID it is given.
- **Contexts.** `CertificateContexts` implements both egresses over one cache of `SslStreamCertificateContext` (offline chain) per certificate: a load on a miss takes the vault's gate, writers invalidate inside it, so a context built from a replaced row can never be cached afterwards; the vault never disposes what it handed out. The cache is per process: a second process writing the same database would not invalidate it (the proxy's vault is the only writer; the KEK-rotating CLI does not change certificates). **Seals check the key.** Every operation that seals (certificate keys, staged keys, secrets, account keys) begins a transaction and re-reads the database's KEK check value first, refusing when it is not this process's KEK: a `rotate-kek` run while the service serves cannot make it seal under the old key (a rotation committing after the check makes the write fail). **Staged keys** are sealed to `{certificateId}/{stagedKeyId}` and a digest of their names, and a staging deletes staged keys older than 30 days (abandoned orders). A start whose generated `master.key` does not match the database names `master.key.old` or `master.key.new` when that one does (an interrupted rotation). `EnsureLocalCaAsync` for an ID other than the root's throws `LocalCaExistsException` carrying the real ID and info.
- **Import.** The upload is read into one pinned buffer (at most 1 MiB, stops reading at the cap), PEM key blocks are decoded from bytes (never through a string) and PFX is read under `CertificateImport.Limits` (32 certificates, one key, 600k iteration limits). Wrong password versus unreadable is told by the outer structure (ENCRYPTED PRIVATE KEY or PFX outer sequence parses: wrong password; otherwise unreadable), because the Pkcs12 and PKCS#8 info types are not in the shared framework. Contract addition: `CertificateImportFailure.NoUsableName` (a leaf with no DNS or IP SAN is refused before anything is stored, since nothing could ever select it).

**As built (M5): the Data Protection key ring.** `IDataProtectionKeyStore` (`Paduraksa.Core/Vault`) backs the admin app's `Microsoft.AspNetCore.DataProtection.Repositories.IXmlRepository`: `ReadAllAsync` returns every element, `StoreAsync` seals one element under its friendly name, and that is the whole surface (the optional `IDeletableXmlRepository` is not implemented, so a retired key stays in the ring until it expires). Each element is a row in the vault's own `DataProtectionKeys` table, sealed by the envelope cipher under the purpose `data-protection-key\n<friendly name>` with its typed `DataProtectionKeyId` and version in the AAD, inside the KEK-check sealing transaction, so editing the name or moving the ciphertext fails to open it. `vault rotate-kek` re-wraps the table (counted as `DataProtectionKeys`), and a restore keeps the ring because it is inside `vault.db`. A row that fails to open is logged at Error and skipped, so one damaged element does not take the ring down. The element XML **is** key material, so `DataProtectionKey.KeyXml` is the **third sanctioned egress** (after Kestrel's and the upstream mTLS contexts): the admin's `VaultXmlRepository` is its only reader, and it hands each element straight back to Data Protection in-process; `DataProtectionKey`'s printing hides it. No `IXmlEncryptor` is registered - the vault already encrypts at rest, so a second layer would only wrap ciphertext in ciphertext and need a key ring of its own to protect this one - and no file-system repository is created (`KeyManagementOptions.XmlRepository` is set to the vault's); the framework's "No XML encryptor configured" warning is filtered from the log. The ring is **admin only**: the dashboard's antiforgery and circuit state need it to survive a restart, while the public proxy gets YARP's session-affinity key from an in-memory `EphemeralDataProtectionProvider` (the affinity cookie is a destination ID, not a secret) and never reads or writes the vault; its Data Protection key-ring warm-up hosted service is removed so no key is ever created. The admin session cookie itself is Paduraksa's own random value, not Data Protection. The core host resolves no protector. The adapter and the `AddPaduraksaDataProtection` extension live in `Paduraksa.Admin.DataProtection`.

**As built (M7): password credentials.** An access-list user's password is a **credential** in the vault, named by a `pwc_` ID that the config document carries (`{ username, credentialId }`): no hash, no password and nothing that verifies one is in the document, a revision, an audit entry, an API response, the simulator's trace, a log or an exception. `PasswordHash` (the PHC string `$pbkdf2-sha256$i=210000$salt$hash`, a 16-byte salt, a 32-byte hash; a cost outside 100 000 to 10 000 000, a non-canonical encoding or another shape is refused) left Core: it is `internal` to the vault, with its parsed form, the cache, the registry and the store, and an architecture test holds each of them out of every other assembly (the proxy's own `BannedSymbols.txt` also bans `Rfc2898DeriveBytes`, so the gate can't hash a password either). *Storage.* `PasswordCredentials` (vault migration `PasswordCredentials`): one row per credential, the verifier sealed by the envelope cipher (AAD purpose `password-credential`, the version in the AAD and a concurrency token, inside the KEK-check sealing transaction like every seal), re-wrapped by `vault rotate-kek` (counted as `PasswordCredentials`) and carried by a backup of `vault.db`. A row that doesn't open is logged at Error with its ID and skipped; its user can't sign in until a password is set again. *Memory.* The verifiers are loaded once, on first use (`Open` is synchronous because the snapshot compiler is, so it never starts the vault itself: called before `IVaultLifecycle.InitializeAsync` it throws an `InvalidOperationException` that names the fix, instead of blocking a thread on an asynchronous start; the host initializes the vault long before a compile), parsed into a slot per credential ID that is never removed, so a handle held by a compiled snapshot sees a later `SetAsync` or `DeleteAsync` without a recompile. `SetAsync` hashes outside the vault's gate (a fresh salt, 210 000 iterations), then seals and swaps the slot's state inside it; `ImportAsync` keeps an existing verifier as it is (no re-hash) and does nothing if the credential already has a password. *The verifier.* `TryVerifyCached` is the old cache, moved with its per-process key and its refresh on hit (a hit pushes the expiry to five minutes out once less than four are left): a keyed SHA-256 over the key, the credential's **stamp** (a hash of its salt and verifier, so a password change, a delete or a re-import makes every entry of the old verifier miss with nothing to sweep), the masked client address (16 bytes, as the caller masks it) and the user name and password as sent (the name is in it because a credential can be renamed without changing its password); it builds the key on the stack, so a hit and a miss are 0 B (`PasswordCredentialStoreTests` measures both with `AllocAssert`). It returns the probe's two facts: hit, and an **attempt key**, a 64-bit value from that hash, 0 for a credential with no password. The proxy's line uses it to run identical attempts one after the other (the coalescing as built), and it says nothing about what was sent. `Verify` is the PBKDF2 path: a null credential (a name nobody has) and a credential with no password pay the same PBKDF2 against a dummy verifier, so neither can be told from a wrong password by timing (the verdicts differ, `Rejected` and `NoPassword`, for the simulator's trace); with `remember` a success is cached, a failure never is, and a simulation passes false. *Taking a change back.* Every `SetAsync` keeps, in memory until it is kept or reverted, what it replaced: the previous sealed row as it was (bytes, version, times) or the fact that there was none, and the slot's previous verifier, and returns a `PasswordChange` that names it. `RevertAsync` writes exactly that back inside the vault's gate and the KEK-check transaction (a master key that changed since is a failure, not a silent re-seal) and puts the slot back; `Keep` forgets it. The config pipeline sets the passwords after validation and compile and before the commit, and on any failure of the commit reverts them: a command whose commit failed leaves every password as it was. If the revert itself fails, the pipeline logs at Error and writes an audit event of its own (`config.access_list.password.restore_failed`, outcome Failed) that names the lists and users whose password may be in an unknown state, never a password, because the commit's own audit event was never written. `PrepareAsync` and `PrepareImport` return a handle that exists for the caller alone (not stored, not reachable by `Open`, never seen by the cache): what a dry run or a draft compiles against, below.

*Lifecycle (decided).* A credential lives **as long as the live config names it**: after a command commits, the credentials its revision stopped naming (a removed user, a deleted list, a rollback to a document without them) are deleted, and at startup, when the live config is the database's latest revision, so are the vault's credentials nothing names (what a command that failed after setting a password left behind). Revisions are not consulted. Keeping a credential for as long as any revision names it would keep a removed user's password for as long as the history lasts, and a rollback would bring that user back with it; the user a rollback restores gets **no password** (or the one the vault holds then, if a credential with that ID still exists): the config compiles with a warning, `accessList.user.noPassword`, and that user can't sign in until one is set. A start on the last-known-good file, or with no revision, sweeps nothing: it doesn't know what is stray, and the config database may come back. *Backup and export.* The hashes are in `vault.db` now, not in the config document, so a config export carries none and a full backup (the vault's database, KEK passphrase-wrapped, §8.3) is what carries the passwords; restoring only the config gives users with no password (the warning says so). **Gaps:** the stamp and the key are per process, so a restart starts with an empty cache (one PBKDF2 per active user, as before); a slot is a few words per credential ID ever opened and is never reclaimed; the vault database is not part of the config commit, so the pipeline takes a failed commit's passwords back (above) rather than rolling them back with it, and a process killed between the set and the revert leaves them changed (the next start's sweep deletes the credentials of a user that never made it into a revision).

**Why the handler factory returns the whole transport.** A caller-supplied inner handler would see every request header, including the credential; a returned `DelegatingHandler` would let the caller swap its `InnerHandler` until the first send; and a response's `RequestMessage` would carry the injected header back. So the factory returns a sealed `internal` vault handler over a `SocketsHttpHandler` the vault builds itself (no automatic redirects, https only, timeouts, DNS refresh and proxy from `SecretHttpTransportOptions`), adds the credential to a **clone** of the request and sets `response.RequestMessage` back to the caller's original. It refuses non-https requests, hosts outside the allowlist and secrets that are not `DnsApiToken`, on every request. It never follows a redirect: providers handle 3xx themselves, and a redirect to another host fails the host check, so it never carries the credential. Tests substitute the transport through an internal seam in Vault, never through Core.

The `ISecretHttpHandlerFactory` pattern is the key trick. A DNS-provider client in `Paduraksa.Acme` builds an `HttpClient` over a vault-owned handler. The Cloudflare API token is added to the outgoing request *inside* the vault assembly and never exists as a value in Acme code. RFC2136 TSIG gets the same treatment through `IMacKey`.

### 8.3 At-rest encryption
- **Envelope encryption.** Each secret has its own random DEK (AES-256-GCM). DEKs are wrapped by the **KEK**. The AAD is `secretId || kind || version`, which stops anyone swapping ciphertexts between rows.
- **KEK source** in priority order: `PADURAKSA_MASTER_KEY_FILE` (Docker secrets / systemd `LoadCredential`), `PADURAKSA_MASTER_KEY` env, then auto-generated `data/master.key` (0600). The dashboard warns when the KEK sits in the same directory as `vault.db`. **As built (threat model G3):** the overview's "things need you" list carries it ("The master key is stored beside the vault", with why: a copy of the data directory is a copy of every key, and a link to install.md "The master key"), from `VaultStatus.KekBesideDatabase`, for a user who holds `vault:manage` (the Owner: only the person who runs the server can move the key). The shipped compose example uses the Docker secret by default, with the generation commands in its comments.
- `paduraksa vault rotate-kek --new-key-file <path|->` re-wraps the DEKs (as built: the new key is read from a file or stdin, never argv; with the generated `master.key` the command stores the new key itself (old file kept as `master.key.old`, new one written through `master.key.new`, all 0600 and flushed with the directory, the old file put back if the rotation fails; standard input that is a terminal is refused and a group-readable key file is warned about); with a key file or the environment as the source the new key must come from a file the operator keeps).
- Plaintext buffers are pinned and zeroed (`CryptographicOperations.ZeroMemory`) after use, best effort.
- **Export.** Config export excludes secrets (and so password hashes: they are in the vault). An explicit "full backup" produces an archive with `vault.db` encrypted under a user passphrase (Argon2id-derived), and it is audited. **As built (M10):** below.
- **As built (M5): the Data Protection key ring** is one more sealed table (`DataProtectionKeys`), so it follows every rule above: its DEKs are re-wrapped by `vault rotate-kek`, its rows are protected by `secure_delete` and the WAL checkpoint, and it is covered by a backup of `vault.db` without any extra step.
- **As built (M3 vault core).** Format 1 of a sealed value is one BLOB: format byte, KEK ID (8 bytes), wrap nonce, DEK wrapped with AES-256-GCM (AES-KW does not exist in .NET, and GCM lets the wrap carry the AAD), data nonce, ciphertext, tag. The AAD of both operations is `domain || format || (KEK ID) || len(id) || id || len(purpose) || purpose || version`, with ID the typed-ID string (`sec_...`, `acct_...`) and the purpose carrying what decides how the value may be used: a secret's kind, MAC algorithm and allowed hosts, an account key's JWS algorithm. Editing that metadata in `vault.db` therefore makes the value fail to open (a change of hosts is a replace, which seals again). The KEK is base64 of 32 bytes from all three sources, and never becomes a string inside the vault when written; a source that is set but unusable is an error, never a fall-through. `master.key` is created exclusively (written whole to a private 0600 temp file, then given its name with an atomic hard link that cannot replace an existing file) and never overwritten, a concurrent reader replays its open and waits for the whole key, and a new one is not generated if `vault.db` already holds data. An existing generated `master.key` readable by other users is refused (group access warns), judged on the open handle; an explicit key file is only warned about, since Docker secrets are mounted 0444. `vault.db` stores an HMAC check value of the KEK, compared at startup (`IVaultLifecycle.InitializeAsync`, which also reports whether the KEK sits beside the database), and runs with `secure_delete`. The previous value of a MAC secret verifies for 10 minutes after a replacement, then is deleted; `Sign` and `Verify` use cached key material and never wait for a rotation. **KEK rotation** (`IVaultKekRotation`) re-wraps every DEK in one transaction and checkpoints the write-ahead log, so the old wraps are overwritten; it re-wraps only, it does not re-key the data, so a copy of the old database plus the old KEK still opens that copy (to cut that off, replace the secrets). The order is the caller's: store the new KEK durably at its source, rotate, then retire the old one; the vault never stores the new KEK itself, and a crash with the new KEK stored nowhere after the commit would lose the vault. The authenticating handler allows only port 443 unless an allowed-host entry names the port (`host:8443`, in both the secret's and the caller's list), and it refuses a request whose `Host` header differs from its URL (domain fronting) and never copies it. Vault tests live in `Paduraksa.Tests.Vault`, a key-material boundary project.
- **Future hardening:** the capability interfaces are the seam for moving the vault into a separate process (gRPC over a Unix socket with peer credentials) without changing any callers.

### 8.4 As built (M10): backup and restore

**The owner's decision:** a backup contains **everything** (the config database, the vault database, the last-known-good file), encrypted under a passphrase. It restores on a fresh machine with that machine's own master key, and it is useless without the passphrase. Code: `Paduraksa.Vault/Backup` (the archive and its crypto), the contract `IVaultBackup` and `IStagedBackup` in `Paduraksa.Core/Vault`, `BackupService` (Application; the permission check, the audit), `POST /api/v1/backup`, Settings > Backup, and `paduraksa backup|restore` (`Server/Cli/BackupCommands`).

- **The archive (format 1, `*.pdkbak`).** A plain 48-byte header, then everything else sealed. The header is magic `PDKBAK 1A 0A` (the `1A 0A` catch a text-mode transfer, as PNG's do), the format version, the key-derivation and cipher ids, the Argon2 memory (KiB), passes and lanes, a random 16-byte salt and the chunk size (64 KiB). A parser refuses an unknown version or algorithm, non-zero reserved bytes and costs outside 8 KiB x lanes .. 1 GiB, 1 .. 16 passes, 1 .. 16 lanes, so a header cannot make a restore spend a gigabyte of memory twice. What follows is a **chunked AES-256-GCM stream** (the STREAM construction of age and Tink): 64 KiB plaintext chunks each sealed with nonce = four zero bytes and the chunk index (64-bit big-endian), the whole header plus a last-chunk flag as the associated data, a 16-byte tag after each chunk. Every chunk but the last is exactly full and the last is shorter (possibly empty), so a reader knows the last one from its length and authenticates it as such: a cut at a chunk boundary leaves no last chunk (refused as truncated), a cut inside a chunk fails its tag, and a moved, dropped or appended chunk fails its index or its flag. A tag that fails on the first chunk is reported as a wrong passphrase (or an altered header, which cannot be told apart); anywhere later it is a damaged archive. The sealed stream is a **tar** (ustar) of fixed entries, in this order: `manifest.json`, `paduraksa.db`, `vault.db`, `last-good.json` (when the source had one), `vault-backup.key`. No other name is ever accepted and no name is ever used as a path.
- **Why tar and not zip.** Both ends are forward-only streams: the writer produces the archive in constant memory while it encrypts, and the reader decrypts and unpacks in one pass, authenticating every chunk before any byte of it is believed. A zip needs to seek (its index is at the end), which means buffering the plaintext archive or the ciphertext in memory or in a second temporary file; it adds nothing here, since SQLite files do not compress usefully once sealed and the compression would be before encryption. `System.Formats.Tar` is in the framework.
- **The manifest** (`manifest.json`, the first entry, so it is read before any file): `format`, `paduraksa` (the build, as its informational version), `createdAt`, `databases.config` and `databases.vault` (the EF migration IDs applied to each snapshot, oldest first), `entries` (name, size, SHA-256 of each file, including `vault-backup.key`) and `kdf` (algorithm, version, memory, passes, lanes; it repeats the header, and a disagreement is a damaged archive). It is sealed with the rest, so it is readable only with the passphrase. The brief's "source instance name" is not recorded: Paduraksa has no such name, and the machine's host name would only be noise in a file that moves between machines.
- **Key derivation.** Argon2id (RFC 9106), **64 MiB, three passes, four lanes** (RFC 9106's second recommended option, sized for a homelab box: about half a second on a small CPU, and 64 MiB freed afterwards), a random salt per archive, the 32-byte result used directly as the AES key. The parameters are in the header, so a later build can raise them without breaking old archives, and a restore accepts what the header says within the ranges above. **Library:** `Konscious.Security.Cryptography.Argon2` 1.3.1 (MIT; a managed implementation, so there is no native library per RID to ship in a multi-arch image or to find on a bare-metal box, which libsodium bindings would need; widely used, and the only maintained managed Argon2id with the associated-data and secret inputs). A test (`BackupKeyDerivationTests`) pins it to RFC 9106's own Argon2id vector, and `BackupBoundaryTests` fails if any project but `Paduraksa.Vault` references it. The library's internal buffers, and the passphrase string a caller started from, are not ours to zero; the pinned copies Paduraksa makes are zeroed. The passphrase is used as its UTF-8 bytes exactly as typed (no Unicode normalization: the platform's can differ, and a rule that differs between the machine that made an archive and the one that opens it would lock people out). It must be 12 to 1024 characters (`BackupPassphrase`, one rule for the CLI, the API and the dashboard; no composition rules, as for passwords); the dashboard shows a hint by length alone.
- **Decision: the vault's keys are re-wrapped under a backup-only key (option b).** At backup time the vault takes its snapshot of `vault.db`, then re-wraps every data-encryption key in the snapshot (`KekRotation.RewrapAllAsync`, the one list of what holds a sealed value, shared with `vault rotate-kek`) under a fresh random **backup key**, and puts that key in the archive as `vault-backup.key`. So **the source's master key never enters the archive**, a restore never installs it, and an archive stays restorable after the source's master key is rotated or lost. On restore the DEKs are re-wrapped again under the target's own master key (`StagedBackup.WriteVaultDatabaseAsync`). Option (a), carrying the source's KEK wrapped by the passphrase, was rejected: it would put the one key that opens every copy of the source's `vault.db` into the archive. The cost of (b) is nothing the owner notices; the backup key is protected by the same passphrase as everything beside it, and it exists only so that the vault file inside is not wrapped under a key that exists nowhere else. The re-wrapped snapshot is compacted with `VACUUM INTO`, so the pages of the old wraps are not in it (and the vault runs with `secure_delete`).
- **Decision: the config database is encrypted too.** It holds the users' password hashes, TOTP references, the audit log, sessions and client addresses, so the whole archive is sealed, not just the vault. Nothing is readable without the passphrase, the manifest included.
- **Online and consistent.** `SqliteConnection.BackupDatabase` (SQLite's online backup API) reads each database in one step inside one read transaction, so each snapshot is the state at one instant while the server keeps writing; a file copy of a live WAL database can miss pages that are only in the log. The vault snapshot is taken under the vault's gate (no KEK rotation or seal in between, and the database's key check is compared with the key this process holds: a rotation run elsewhere is refused with a message saying to restart). The two databases are **not** one instant: the config is read first and the vault second, so every key a certificate row points at is already in the vault snapshot (issuance writes the key first). A certificate deleted in the few milliseconds between the two reads can come out as a row without its key, which the startup certificate recovery already handles (an inventory row whose key the vault lacks is retired and re-issued, §9.2). Scratch files live in `<data dir>/run/backup-<random>/` (0700, files 0600), are removed afterwards, and each holds an exclusive lock on a `.lock` marker for the backup's life, so the sweep of leftovers (any process's, at the start of a backup) removes only a directory nobody holds (or a markerless one over an hour old) and never a long-running backup's. One backup runs at a time per process (a second gets `Busy`/409).
- **API.** `POST /api/v1/backup` with `{"passphrase": "..."}` in the JSON body (over the admin's HTTPS, never a URL) answers `application/octet-stream` with `Content-Disposition: attachment; filename=paduraksa-backup-<UTC stamp>.pdkbak`, `Cache-Control: no-store` and the length. The archive is complete in a private temporary file (0600, deleted when the response ends) before a byte is sent, so a failure is an ordinary problem and never a half download. 422 `backup.passphrase` (too short or too long), 409 `backup.busy`, 500 `backup.failed` (the log says why; the answer names nothing). There is **no restore endpoint**: a restore replaces the databases and needs the server stopped. The new permission **`backup:create`** is held by the **Owner only** (`RolePermissions`: Owner has every permission; Admin, Operator, Viewer and Auditor do not) and an API token can carry it as a scope, but only if its owner holds it (a token never exceeds its owner; an Admin cannot mint one). The refusal of a caller without it is the endpoint filter's 403 (not audited, like every filter refusal); a refusal inside the service (the dashboard, the CLI's evaluator) is audited as `denied`.
- **Audit.** `backup.create`: success with `{"bytes": N}` (the actor, the channel, the client address and the time are the event's own), `denied`, `invalid` (a weak passphrase) and `failed`. Never the passphrase; a test searches the audit log, every log line, the output of the CLI and the archive for it. The CLI audits under the `cli` actor.
- **CLI.** `paduraksa backup --out <file>`: the passphrase from `--passphrase-file <path>` (`-` is standard input, refused when that is a terminal; a file other users can read is used and warned about), else `PADURAKSA_BACKUP_PASSPHRASE` (ignored, with a warning, beside a file), else a prompt without echo, **twice**; never an argument (a test parses the command line and finds no option that takes it). It works while the server runs, needs the master key the way the server does (the vault opens its database with it to re-wrap), refuses an existing `--out` and a data directory with no config database, creates the output's directory, and writes to a private `<out>.partial-<random>` that it flushes and renames, so a file at `--out` is always whole. `paduraksa restore <file>` first claims the data directory (see **One server per data directory**) and refuses while a server holds it; then reads the passphrase (once) and **verifies the whole archive before touching anything**: header, passphrase, every chunk's tag, the manifest, every hash, that both databases are sound SQLite files (`quick_check`) and that no migration in either is unknown to this build (**a newer schema is refused**, naming the migration: the vault's own list is this build's `VaultDbContext`, the config's is `ConfigDbContext`; an older one is accepted). Only then it moves the current `paduraksa.db`, `vault.db`, their `-wal`/`-shm` files and `last-good.json` to `<data dir>/pre-restore-<UTC time>/` (0700; nothing is deleted, `master.key` stays), migrates the staged vault to this build's schema, re-wraps it under this machine's master key (resolved as the server does; **a `master.key` is generated when there is none**, and the command says to keep a copy of it apart), installs the three files, and runs what a start checks: the vault opens under that key and the config database opens through `ConfigDatabase.InitializeAsync` (which applies an older schema's migrations, reported) and has its revisions (the latest revision's number and hash are printed). Any failure after the first move **undoes it all** (what was placed is removed, what was moved comes back, a `master.key` this run generated goes), and says so; a rollback that cannot move something back names where it is. Staging is `<data dir>/run/restore-<random>/` (0700) and is removed at the end.
- **One server per data directory.** The brief's "detect it via the run/lock mechanism": the existing mechanism was the internal listener's socket probe, which cannot see a server with `Listen:AdminInternal` off or on the loopback fallback, so the server now claims `<data dir>/run/server.lock` (`DataDirectoryLock`, `Paduraksa.Core/Hosting`): an exclusive file lock held for the process's life (`flock` on Unix, a share mode on Windows), released by the operating system however the process ends (no stale lock, no pid to believe), taken by `SharedServices` before anything else uses the directory and released with the last shared service. A second server on the directory now fails to start (exit 2, Critical log) instead of "isn't supported", and `restore` holds the same claim for its whole run, so a server started meanwhile finds it taken. On a file system that cannot lock (some network mounts, where .NET ignores a failing `flock`) the claim is not enforced, as before: the lock detects that by finding a second open of the file succeeds (`Enforced`), and the server logs a Warning at startup (and `restore` prints one) so the operator knows.
- **Container.** `docker exec paduraksa paduraksa backup --out /data/backups/x.pdkbak` (add `-it` for the prompt, or give the passphrase as `PADURAKSA_BACKUP_PASSPHRASE` or a mounted file) and, with the service stopped, `docker compose run --rm paduraksa restore /data/backups/x.pdkbak`; `docs/install.md` ("Backups") has both and the homelab routine.
- **Dashboard.** Settings > Backup (`/settings/backup`, visible with `backup:create`, so the Owner's): a warning that only the passphrase protects the file and nothing recovers a lost one, the passphrase twice with a length hint (`PassphraseStrength`), and **Download backup**, which hands the archive to the browser over the circuit (`DashboardBrowser.DownloadAsync`, as the audit export does; the file is deleted when the page is done with it and the fields are emptied as the request goes out). A second card names the restore command and says it needs the service stopped (restore stays CLI-only). `IDashboardBackup` is the seam bUnit fakes; the Audit page words the action as "Made a full backup".
- **Boundary.** The vault does all the cryptography: `AesGcm`, Argon2 and the backup key stay in `Paduraksa.Vault` (the banned-symbol rules and `BackupBoundaryTests` hold). `IVaultBackup` crosses the contract test's deny-by-default rule at exactly two allowed positions, `CreateAsync(destination)` (ciphertext going out) and `OpenAsync(archive)` (sealed bytes coming in), each with its reason in `VaultContractTests`; the plain config database and `last-good.json` pass through the vault only as files in a staging directory. The vault's architecture tests and banned-symbol rules stay green.
- **Left out.** The wizard's backup reminder (§12.1): it needs a record of whether a backup was ever made and a place for it in the checklist, which is not small; the audit log already says (`backup.create`). Scheduled backups and retention (the install guide shows a cron line; a scheduler inside the process would have to hold the passphrase), incremental backups, a restore endpoint or button, and including the log files (they are not state). The download goes through the circuit and the browser holds it in memory, which is fine for a homelab's databases and not for a multi-gigabyte one; the CLI and the API stream.
- **Tests.** Vault: RFC 9106 vector, header parsing, the chunked stream (every size around a chunk, a wrong key, a changed byte per position, cuts at and inside chunks, swapped, dropped and appended chunks, a changed header), the archive round trip with every kind of sealed value (secrets with a previous value, account keys, certificate keys and the local CA, the Data Protection ring, password credentials, TOTP) opened under another master key, the source key absent from the archive, a generated key when the target has none, a wrong passphrase, tampered bytes at sampled positions, truncations, a non-archive, a newer schema, private modes, one backup at a time, a consistent snapshot while a writer replaces secrets. Data: the service's permissions per role, the token scope, the audit of every outcome, the download file's lifetime. Integration: a real host backed up while it serves and restored on a data directory with another master key (the revision hash, the certificate's key, an access-list password, an authenticator-protected sign-in), the CLI's passphrase sources, no overwrite, refusal while a server runs, a second server refused, the old databases kept aside, a wrong passphrase, a tampered and a truncated file, a newer schema, and a failure after the move undone; the API's roles, scope, validation, 409, audit and the passphrase's absence from logs. Dashboard: bUnit for the page and a Playwright download.

---

## 9. Certificates & ACME

- **Own ACME client (RFC 8555)** in `Paduraksa.Acme`, about 1.5k LOC. We write our own instead of using Certes/ACMESharp because the account key must stay in the vault (`IJwsSigner`), and we want first-class **ARI (RFC 9773)** and **ACME profiles** (e.g. Let's Encrypt `shortlived`).
- **Challenges:** HTTP-01 (served by the public app's gate from an in-memory store). DNS-01 (provider plugins + propagation check by querying authoritative NS directly). TLS-ALPN-01 later.
- **DNS providers (v1):** Cloudflare, deSEC, Hetzner, DigitalOcean, Porkbun, DuckDNS, Route53, RFC2136, acme-dns, plus "manual" (show the TXT record and wait for confirmation) and a webhook provider. Interface: `CreateTxtAsync / DeleteTxtAsync / ValidateCredentialsAsync`. Every provider needs a **"Test credentials"** button in the UI.
- **CAs:** Let's Encrypt (prod/staging), ZeroSSL (EAB), Google Trust Services (EAB), Buypass, custom directory URL.
- **Account key rollover (RFC 8555 §7.3.5) is deferred past v1.** Recovery from a lost or compromised account key is a new account. The shape when it comes is additive on `IJwsSigner`: `StageAccountKeyAsync` (generate the new key, return its public JWK), `SignWithStagedAsync` (sign the inner `keyChange` JWS with it) and `CommitStagedAccountKeyAsync` (swap it in once the CA accepted the change). Nothing existing changes.
- **Renewal:** a background scheduler uses the ARI window if offered, otherwise renews at ⅓ of lifetime remaining, with jitter, exponential backoff and rate-limit awareness. On success the snapshot is recompiled and published (zero-downtime cert swap). On failure: notification + dashboard banner.
- **Auto-matching:** hosts with `CertificateBinding = Auto` get the most specific valid cert covering their names (exact beats wildcard). The dashboard flags hosts without coverage and offers "Issue cert for these names".
- **Other sources:** uploaded PEM/PFX (straight to the vault through the API's streaming endpoint, so the dashboard upload goes to that endpoint rather than through the Blazor circuit). Self-signed. **Local CA**: an internal root generated in the vault, issuing leafs for LAN-only hosts, with the root cert downloadable for installing as trusted.
- **Serving:** `SslStreamCertificateContext.Create(cert, chain, offline: true)` is prebuilt per snapshot, so no chain building happens during a handshake. **OCSP stapling is out of scope for v1.** Let's Encrypt dropped OCSP in 2025, and we don't want to promise cross-platform server-side stapling behaviour.
- **Cloudflare's origin-pull CA** (Authenticated Origin Pulls, §7.6) is public data shipped as an embedded resource and replaceable by a PEM file setting; it is a trust anchor, not an inventory certificate, and no client certificate is ever stored.
- **Expiry monitoring** covers every certificate, not just ACME ones. Uploaded and self-signed certs have no renewal path, so they get the same 30/14/7/1-day notifications and a dashboard badge. Local-CA leaves can be re-issued in one click (or automatically).

### 9.1 As planned for M3

Storage and compile-time decisions for certificates (decided before M3 work started):

- **Certificates, ACME accounts and DNS providers are not part of the config document.** They are operational resources in the config database (`Certificates`, `AcmeAccounts`, `AcmeOrders`, `DnsProviders`): metadata, the public chain and renewal state. Secrets appear only as `SecretId` references into the vault (§5.1, §8). Application services change them and audit every change; they do not create config revisions. Config export (§11.5) carries certificate metadata only.
- **The document references certificates only by binding:** Auto, Specific (`CertificateId`) or None. The compiler resolves bindings against the **certificate inventory** (the valid certificates and the names they cover), passed in alongside the document. Auto picks the most specific valid certificate covering the host's names: an exact name beats a wildcard, then the latest `NotAfter` wins.
- **A certificate change (issued, renewed, uploaded or deleted) recompiles the latest revision with the new inventory and publishes it**, without creating a revision. Deleting a certificate that a Specific binding uses is refused.
- **Replacing a certificate keeps its ID and is atomic.** Renewal, re-issue, a new upload and a new self-signed certificate on an existing `CertificateId` swap key and chain together in one vault transaction, so Specific bindings survive. ACME and manual CSR flows stage the new key first: `CreateKeyAndCsrAsync` returns a `StagedKeyId` that `AttachChainAsync` must present, so an overlapping second staging cannot silently take the first one's place (it fails with a documented exception instead).
- **The local CA root is a normal certificate.** `EnsureLocalCaAsync(CertificateId, ...)` creates it under a caller-chosen ID, so it has a Certificates row, expiry monitoring and a downloadable public chain like any other. Rotating or deleting the CA root is later work (with a plan for leaves issued under the old root); **nothing renews it yet** either, so its last 90 days shorten every leaf it issues (the admin listener's own included, §3), and that is the open item §3 refers to.
- **DNS credentials carry their own destinations.** The allowed hosts of a DNS token are metadata the admin sets when creating the DNS provider, stored with the secret (`SecretOptions.AllowedHosts`); the vault's authenticating handler uses their intersection with the provider code's own list, speaks only https, never follows redirects, and is the whole transport (§8.2).
- **Shared EF/SQLite infrastructure lives in `Paduraksa.Storage`** (§1): typed-ID conventions and converters, SQLite pragmas and the connection-string builder, the UTC timestamp converter. Vault may reference only Core and Storage, so it gets these without seeing any config entity.

### 9.2 As built: the certificate inventory (M3)

- **Storage.** `Certificates` (one row per `CertificateId`: source `acme`/`uploaded`/`self_signed`/`local_ca`/`local_ca_root`, status `pending`/`active`/`failed` with a message, subject, issuer, serial, AKI, SHA-256 fingerprint, key algorithm label, `NotBefore`/`NotAfter`, chain issuers, the **public** chain PEM, label, notes, created/updated) and `CertificateNames` (one normalised row per SAN, `NOCASE`, indexed). Expiry is not a status: it follows from `NotAfter` and the clock. Added as a new migration (`Certificates`), not a regenerated `Initial`, so a development database upgrades and parallel branches don't collide. No private key column exists; host bindings reference a certificate by ID with no foreign key (the document that names it is a revision, not a row).
- **`CertificateService`** (Application, DTOs in `Contracts.Certificates`): `RegisterAsync` (add, or replace the metadata under the same ID: a renewal), `RegisterPendingAsync`, `SetStatusAsync`, `UpdateDetailsAsync`, `DeleteAsync`, `ListAsync` (by source, status, or "covers this name"), `GetAsync` (with the chain). It never calls the vault: the vault flows call it after they created the key and chain. Every change is audited in its own transaction (`certificate.add`, `certificate.update`, `certificate.delete`; refusals too), creates **no revision**, and, when it affects serving (validity, names, chain, status), recompiles the latest revision with the new inventory and publishes it through the config writer (`ConfigService.ApplyCertificateChangeAsync`), so publishes stay ordered. Deleting a certificate that a Specific binding or the default certificate uses is refused with the paths of the referencing hosts. Names that are no DNS name, wildcard or IP address are dropped; none left is invalid.
- **Preload and the inventory.** Before each compile `CertificateInventoryLoader` reads the metadata (plus `IBuiltInCertificates`), judges each certificate against `TimeProvider` (active and inside its validity period), works out which valid certificates the document can choose (the named default, Specific bindings, the best match per name of every enabled Auto host), loads exactly those through `IServerCertificateSource`, and hands the compiler an immutable `CertificateInventory` whose resolver covers the loaded ones. A certificate the source can't load (null or an exception) is left out and the selection runs again, so Auto falls to the next best certificate. Contexts are used as the source returns them: an unchanged certificate keeps its instance, so the proxy's certificate table keeps one options object per certificate, and a renewal (a new instance) takes effect with the next publish. The inventory is a compile input, never mutated.
- **Selection is per name.** The proxy's certificate table maps names to certificates, so each DNS name of a host gets its own best certificate (`HostSpec.NameCertificates`), not one certificate for the whole host. A certificate covers a name when it lists it, or lists the wildcard of its parent (`*.example.com` covers `www.example.com`, not `example.com` or `a.b.example.com`); a wildcard host name is covered by the same wildcard. The best match is exact over wildcard, then the latest `NotAfter`, then the latest `NotBefore`, then the smallest ID. IP names never reach the table and ride the default certificate. Specific gives every name of the host that certificate (a certificate that doesn't cover them is a warning).
- **Degraded, never failed.** A name no valid certificate covers gets no certificate of its own: it is served with the default certificate, or rejected when there is none, and the compile reports `certificate.auto.noCoverage` (a `ConfigWarning` on the compilation, `ConfigService.Warnings` and the command result). A Specific binding, or a named default, whose certificate is missing, expired, pending, failed or without a key degrades the same way (`certificate.specific.unusable`, `certificate.default.unusable`), at startup, for the last-known-good fallback and for republishes: one bad certificate never fails the whole config, and an unreadable certificate table gives an inventory of the built-in certificates only. A **command** may not introduce such a binding (a new or changed Specific binding, or a changed default, naming an unusable certificate is `host.certificate.unusable` / `settings.defaultCertificate.unusable`, also in dry runs), but it is not blocked by a binding that went bad since. A rollback and the system's restore after a lost database are exempt: they put back a config that was accepted once, and degrade with warnings like startup does (the inventory may be gone with the database).
- **The placeholder.** (A year of validity: it is dev-only and new in every process, so only a process that runs for a year outlives it, and a restart replaces it.) `PlaceholderCertificates` is a source and a built-in inventory entry for the in-memory certificate under a well-known stable ID, and the inventory's fallback default. As built (vault certificates), it is composed **after** the vault (`CompositeServerCertificateSource`: a certificate the vault holds is never shadowed) and wired in at all only when `Paduraksa:Development:PlaceholderCertificate` is on (default: Debug builds yes, release builds no, §3); off, the source falls back to nothing, there is no built-in entry, and a document that still names the ID degrades like any missing certificate.
- **Validity follows the clock.** A certificate counts as valid from five minutes before its `NotBefore` (one named tolerance, for clock skew) until a second after its `NotAfter`. The inventory reports the earliest such boundary still ahead (`NextBoundary`, carried on the compilation as `ValidityChangesAt`); when a snapshot goes live the config service arms one `TimeProvider` timer for it (replaced by every newer live snapshot, cancelled on dispose), and when it fires it republishes the live config under the writer lock, coalesced with other publishes. So expiry and activation need no other trigger. A renewal under the same ID whose `NotBefore` is in the future leaves the names uncovered (with a warning) until that boundary, because the one row now describes the new certificate.
- **Republishing never publishes the empty config.** A certificate change or a validity republish compiles the database's latest revision, or, when the start is degraded on the last-known-good file or the database has no revision (it was lost), the last-known-good document. Without either it publishes nothing and logs why. A last-known-good republish doesn't end a degraded start. The compile happens inside the change's transaction, before the commit; after the commit nothing can fail the call.
- **After a database loss** the certificate rows are gone while the vault still holds the keys. The vault re-describes what it holds (`ICertificateCatalog`: `ListAsync`/`DescribeAsync` return the ID, whether it is the local CA root and the `CertificateInfo` of the stored chain; public data, read from the chain stored beside the sealed key, so no key is opened and no gate is taken; a row whose chain can't be read is left out and logged, and the query never names the sealed column, which a test pins). `CertificateRecovery` (Application) registers every certificate the vault holds that the inventory has no row for, through `CertificateService.RegisterRecoveredAsync`, as the system (audited; registrations are quiet, and the startup step queues one publish afterwards, so restored Specific bindings serve again). **Register-if-absent under the writer's lock:** per certificate, right before registering, the row's absence and the audit trail are checked inside the config writer's lock, so a registration racing with this (a vault-first upload) is never overwritten and an existing row, its label included, is never changed. **The source is not known**, so it is inferred from public facts and never guessed towards more capability: the root is `local_ca_root`; a certificate whose **signature verifies under that root's public key** (a custom-root `X509Chain`, time and revocation not judged; a matching issuer name alone is not enough) is `local_ca`; everything else is `uploaded` (a lost self-signed one too, since it cannot be told from an uploaded self-signed one, and an upload is never replaced by a re-issue; a lost ACME one too: its order, account and schedule were in the lost database, so it is not renewed until requested again). Each recovered row has the label `Recovered from the vault`. **A deleted certificate is not resurrected:** deleting removes the row (audited as `certificate.delete`) and then the key; if the key's deletion failed the vault still holds it while the database is intact, so a successful `certificate.delete` on record for the ID means "finish the deletion" (`ICertificateIssuance.DeleteAsync`, retried at the next start if it fails) instead of registering; the same trail covers a creation that was rolled back (its pending row was deleted and audited). A key with no such trail (a registration that was refused, an upload interrupted between the vault and the row) is registered, visible and deletable, and a lost database loses the trail with it, so nothing is treated as deleted then. One window remains: an upload that reached the vault but has not registered when this runs is registered as recovered and its own registration then replaces the metadata (an upload without a label keeps the recovered one). It runs once per start, in the core host (`CertificateRecoveryStartup`), as an **awaited start step after the config store and before the web apps**: registrations are quiet (no republish per certificate), then one publish is queued, so the first snapshot the listeners serve already includes the recovered certificates. The step is bounded by `Paduraksa:Certificates:RecoveryStartupTimeout` (10 s; 1 s to 5 min); past it the run continues in the background and the start goes on. A stop is checked between certificates, and registrations and key deletions run with `CancellationToken.None`, so an abort never cancels a transaction partway through (and EF never logs it at Error). `SharedServices.RecoveryCompletion` completes when the run has finished, which tests await instead of sleeping. It skips a store that does not accept changes, never stops the process, logs one summary line and leaves certificates that have a row alone whatever the row says.
- **The vault flows** (`CertificateOperations`, DTOs in `Contracts.Certificates`; Application): upload (optionally over an existing uploaded certificate), self-signed, local CA root, local-CA leaf, re-issue (self-signed and local-CA only, same ID and names) and delete. Each is one authorizer call (`ICertificateOperationAuthorizer`, allow-all until M4), the vault call and the `CertificateService` registration (so audit and republish are the service's). **Ordering** decides what a failure leaves: a new certificate whose names are known (self-signed, leaf, root) is registered *pending* first (validates names, visible, never served), then the vault creates key and chain, then the same row is registered active; a failed vault call or a refused registration deletes the pending row and the key again (not cancellable), and a crash between leaves a visible pending row, never a hidden key. A new upload learns its names from the vault, so it goes vault first and is rolled back (key deleted) if the registration is refused; a key with no row is never loaded. Replacing an existing ID swaps in the vault first (it cannot be undone) and then updates the row; if that fails the result says so, it is logged, and repeating the operation repairs it. Delete asks the inventory first, which refuses (nothing touched in the vault) while a Specific binding or the default certificate uses it, then deletes the key; a failed key deletion is a warning in the result (the certificate is already gone from the inventory, the key unreachable). Deleting the local CA root is refused while local-CA leaves exist; a delete with no row still removes an orphaned key. The root has no names, so `RegisterAsync`/`RegisterPendingAsync` accept an empty name list for `CertificateSource.LocalCaRoot`. The label (and what the inventory would refuse about the request) is checked before the vault is called, so a replacement is never reported invalid after the vault took it.
