  percent-encoding, no trailing slash: the router decodes after a check could have judged) and match an operation of the generated description (case
  insensitively, as the router does); anything else is refused with a pointer to `search`. Five methods: GET, POST, PUT, PATCH, DELETE.
- **The refused list** (`EndpointRules.Refused`, one explicit list with a reason each): backups and restore, API token management, users, roles, sessions and
  the account's own password and second factor, sign-in and setup, every request that would carry a secret into a model (a DNS provider's credential, an
  ACME account's EAB key, a notification channel's secret, a certificate upload with its private key), the live tail stream (use `GET /api/v1/logs/recent`),
  `GET /api/v1/metrics` and `/metrics` (use `/api/v1/metrics/overview`), and the API description itself. Refused whatever the token, scope or `confirm`. Rules
  for endpoints that do not exist yet (roles, restore) exist before the endpoints do (`InSpec: false`), so they are refused the day they appear.
- **The guard** (`McpRequestPolicyApiTests`): the test walks the generated OpenAPI document and fails for an operation whose path matches `SensitivePath`
  (backup, restore, acme, token, user, role, session, secret, credential, vault, eab, password, totp, recovery, auth, login, setup, key, metrics, dns-provider,
  acme-account, notification, upload, import, export, certificate, and the like) and is on neither the refused list nor `EndpointRules.Reviewed`, the list of
  such endpoints that were looked at and allowed, each with its reason. A second test shows the guard catches a new `/api/v1/vault/keys`; a third fails for a
  rule of an existing endpoint that covers no operation, and for an operation on both lists.
- **Two-step writes.** A POST, PUT, PATCH or DELETE without `confirm: true` is not sent. If the operation has a dry run the request is sent with `?dryRun=true`
  and the plan comes back as `{ status, body, dryRun: true, note }`; otherwise the answer is `{ status: 428, body: { requiresConfirm: true, method, path, note } }`
  and nothing is sent. Only the boolean `true` confirms (`"yes"` and `1` do not), and a script cannot set `dryRun` itself. A POST that only reads
  (`simulate`, an error-page preview: `EndpointRules.ReadOnlyPosts`) needs no confirm. Which operations have a dry run is **read from the API description**:
  every change that goes through the config pipeline reads `?dryRun=` (`Preconditions.TryGetDryRun`), and the description now declares the parameter on exactly
  those operations (an operation transformer keyed on `config:write` plus the config pipeline's publish-failure metadata, with a test that the two sets are
  equal), so the client has no list of its own.
- **Read-only by default.** A token without write scopes can only GET, because the API's permission filters say so; a viewer's `confirm: true` write is a 403
  and leaves no revision.

**Prompt injection.** The proxy's data holds attacker-controlled text (user agents and paths in the tail, request hostnames). In this design it can only be
data: a script can `eval` it (the child is a stock engine), but what runs there has no token, no secret, no files and no network, and its only way to act is
`paduraksa.request`, which is read-only unless the token can write, refused for anything secret-bearing or destructive, and two-step for the rest, in the parent. The tool descriptions tell the model so. Tests: hostile text in the tail (a "run this script"
instruction, quotes, `</script>`, `__proto__`) comes back as a string and nothing it says is executed or sent; a response with `__proto__` keys pollutes no
prototype. The safe default is a token with read scopes only (the dashboard's token drawer offers read scopes first).

**The logs read.** `GET /api/v1/logs/recent` (`logs:read`, `ListRecentAccessLog`) is the live tail's buffer as one JSON array, oldest first, with the tail's
filters and `limit` (1 to 1000, default 100): what a client that cannot hold a stream open reads. The stream endpoint itself is refused in the sandbox.

**The dashboard.** The API tokens page (`/settings/tokens`) has a closed-by-default card with the endpoint (from the page's own address), a Claude Code command
and a Claude Desktop config (through `mcp-remote`, with the token in an environment variable) carrying a `<token>` placeholder, never a secret, a line that a
read-only token is the safe default and where a change shows (History and Audit), and that the admin certificate comes from the local CA, so its root has to be
trusted where the client runs (Node clients read `NODE_EXTRA_CA_CERTS`). Client setup is in `docs/install.md`.

**Tests.** The proof is that a misbehaving script costs only its child. Unit (`Paduraksa.Tests.Unit/Mcp`): the frame codec (round trips, every length outside the
limit, a stream that ends inside a frame, not-UTF-8 and not-a-message frames), the request bridge (dry run, confirm, 428, the read-only POST, refusals including
`/mcp` itself, every option error, the query, the body and answer limits), the policy and the tool descriptions. Integration (`Paduraksa.Tests.Integration/Mcp`,
real children): values, errors with their positions, the spec, and the request round trip (ordering, unawaited requests, answers, a failing dispatcher);
twenty scripts that used to be dangerous (`$'` and `` $` `` replacements, `repeat('1e9')`, `concat` bombs, deep `flat`, `String` and `JSON.stringify`,
bind chains, BigInt shifts and powers, a catastrophic regex, loops, allocation, recursion, promise floods) run with the engine's constraints on and with them
off, each ending as a result, a clean error or `The script was stopped: ...`, with the parent unharmed and the next call served; the runaway ones stopped by
the parent with no constraints; a real stack overflow, a memory breach, a wall-time breach, a CPU breach, the request cap, a total-time breach and a caller who
goes away; ten broken fake children (garbage, an oversized, zero or cut frame, not JSON, no type, an unknown type, request ids out of order, an exit, a closed
pipe) and three that never say they are ready; the pool (4 at once, the fifth told to retry, replacement after N runs, idle despawn and lazy respawn on a manual
`TimeProvider`, a warm child kept warm, shutdown killing every child with no orphan, no child before the first call or while MCP is off); the child's environment
(no master key), what it applied, and that it leaves when its stdin closes; frames bound to their run (a fabricated result, a result with no or a wrong run id, a request queued
before the code, a frame of an earlier run, a frame sent while idle), a stderr flood, a caller who gives up while the child starts, an idle child that spins, the descriptors
a real child inherits (pipes only, none of the server's listeners or files) and Landlock denying a read to the thread that applied it. Admin (`Admin/Mcp*`): the official client over streamable HTTP lists the two tools,
searches, executes as the viewer, operator and owner (the actor is the token); no token, a bad one, a Basic one, a cookie alone and a cookie with a token are 401;
a revoked token stops at the next call; an oversized message is 413; limits end as tool errors; dry run versus confirm on a real host, the etag flow, 428, a
viewer's write refused with nothing left, the revision and audit events on the `mcp` channel with the token as actor, the OpenAPI walk (above), the reviewed
reads on a host that holds secrets, hostile tail text, off by default, and stopping the server killing its children. Dashboard: bUnit tests of the card.
Fuzz: `mcp-sandbox` (section 15).

**Not built.** The current config as a YAML resource (the tools do not depend on it, and a resource can be added beside them), the OAuth authorization of the MCP
specification (tokens only, as in section 10.1), a per-token switch for MCP, auditing of reads, a rate limit on `/mcp` beyond the 4 children and the admin
pipeline's own, a full seccomp profile of the runtime's system calls (considered and not taken: it would have to follow every .NET release; only the socket door is closed, above), and Windows or macOS
hardening (the child runs there with the parent's limits and kill but without the system-call hardening, and says so in its `ready` frame).

### 11.3 Health endpoints
`GET /healthz` (process alive, snapshot loaded) and `GET /readyz` (listeners bound, a snapshot live, not draining; vault unlocked from M3) on the admin port, plus the loopback plain-HTTP fallback listener when enabled. Unauthenticated, no details beyond status. Readiness means "serving traffic", not "the config database is reachable": a degraded start (§6.4) serves the last-known-good config and is still ready, so `/readyz` answers 200 with the body `degraded` instead of `ready`. These are for **external** monitors and orchestrators (Uptime Kuma, Kubernetes probes, Gatus), which can probe them over HTTP without spawning anything. The image's own Docker `HEALTHCHECK` is the exception (§16).

### 11.4 CLI (same binary, `System.CommandLine`)
`paduraksa serve` (default) · `setup-token` · `admin reset-password <user>` · `admin disable-2fa <user>` · `config export|import [--dry-run] [--mode]` (**as built (M10)**, below) · `vault rotate-kek` · `backup --out <file>` and `restore <file>` (**as built (M10):** §8.4; the passphrase from a file, the environment or a prompt, never argv) · `doctor` (ports, permissions, H3/libmsquic, DNS, clock skew; **as built (M5):** `doctor --liveness` only - the admin `/healthz` over loopback, for the container `HEALTHCHECK`) · `migrate`.

**As built (M10): `config export` and `config import`.** `paduraksa config export [--revision ID] [--output FILE] [--url] [--token-file]` and `paduraksa config import FILE|- [--mode merge|replace] [--dry-run] [--message] [--base-revision ID] [--force] [--url] [--token-file] [--allow-insecure-http]`, in `Paduraksa.Server/Cli/ConfigCommands.cs`. *Decision: the CLI is a client of the running instance's admin API, never of the database.* "One writer, one process" (§6.4) means the CLI cannot write config, and a client keeps everything an import does (validation, the lock-out guard, the audit event with the token as the actor, the one revision) the pipeline's, with no second implementation. The token comes from `--token-file` (first line) or `PADURAKSA_TOKEN`, never from an argument; it needs `config:read` to export and `config:write` to import. The address is `--url`, else `PADURAKSA_URL`, else this installation's own admin listener (the candidates `doctor --liveness` probes: the loopback plain-HTTP fallback when it is on, else HTTPS to the admin port). A loopback HTTPS address is not certificate-validated (the admin certificate is the instance's own CA's, which a short-lived process does not load; the same call `doctor --liveness` makes, and the request follows no redirect and uses no proxy), any other must present a certificate the system trusts, and a token is never sent over plain `http://` to an address that isn't loopback unless `--allow-insecure-http` says so (the CLI refuses). A token file that group or others can read is warned about, as `backup --passphrase-file` does. *Decision: no offline export from the data directory.* An instance that cannot start has `last-good.json` and its backups (M10 backup/restore) for its document, and a second reader of a database a server owns would need its own rules for a half-written WAL. A dry run prints the changes (`+`, `-`, `~` and the path) and warnings; a refusal prints each error with its YAML path and line and exits 1; exit 0 is success and "nothing to change". Tested against a running test host (`ConfigCommandsTests`).

### 11.5 YAML import/export
- YamlDotNet with a static (source-generated) context. Format:
  ```yaml
  apiVersion: paduraksa.dev/v1
  kind: Config
  settings: { defaults: {...} }
  profiles: [...]
  upstreams: [...]
  hosts:
    - id: host_0J9zb3xQk1Lr8m2V7aYc4D   # optional on import; matched by id, else by first hostname
      names: [jellyfin.example.com]
      profile: media
      routes: [{ path: /, upstream: jellyfin }]
  certificates: [...]   # metadata + binding only; never key material
  ```
- Import goes through the normal command pipeline (validate → compile → diff → one revision). Modes: `merge` and `replace`.
- The same canonical document is what revisions store, so export = "current revision as YAML".
- The import format above is a friendlier DTO than the document (M10): optional IDs, references by name (`profile: media`, `upstream: jellyfin`), `kind:`. Import maps it onto the strict canonical `ConfigDocument` (§6.4), assigning IDs and resolving names; only that document is stored, and export writes it back in a form that imports to the same document.
- Later: **declarative mode** (§11.6), where a watched file is authoritative and the UI becomes read-only for the objects it manages.

**As built (M10): YAML import and export** (`Paduraksa.Application/Yaml`, the API in `Paduraksa.Admin/Api/ConfigTransferEndpoints.cs`, the page in `Paduraksa.Dashboard/Pages/ConfigTransfer.razor`).
- **YamlDotNet is a parser and an emitter, nothing else.** *Decision (the PLAN said "static (source-generated) context"):* the YAML is read into a plain node tree (`YamlTreeReader`) and written from one (`YamlTreeWriter`), and the typed side is the document's own source-generated `System.Text.Json` contract (`ConfigJsonContext`), so there is no YAML type model to keep in step with ~40 records, no reflection and no type that input can name; a YamlDotNet static context would have been a second description of the document and a deserialiser that instantiates what the tags say. `ConfigYamlSchema` reads the shape from the JSON metadata the generator already holds (property names, nested types, which are required), so a field added to the document is in the YAML with no change here.
- **The format is the canonical document, friendlier in three ways** (all in `ConfigYamlSchema`): `version` is not a key (the pipeline assigns versions on every change); a reference to another object is its **name** under the key without `Id` (`profile: media`, `upstream: jellyfin`, `rateLimit: {policy: strict}`, `accessList: {accessList: office}`, `ruleSet: {ruleSet: Block AI crawlers}`, `maintenance: {bypassAccessList: office}`), looked up ignoring case among the YAML's objects and, when merging, the stored ones that are left alone, with the built-in rule-set presets nameable too (a name two objects share is `yaml.reference.ambiguous`, not a guess), and an ID is accepted in its place, taken as it is even when no object has it (a valid document can name an object that is gone in a layer nothing uses, so its export has to read back; the validator refuses one that matters); and `id` is optional everywhere (hosts, routes, destinations and every named object). `kind: Config` and `apiVersion: paduraksa.dev/v1` head the file. A certificate is only its binding (`certificate: {mode, certificateId}`, an inventory ID: the document has no certificate objects, §9.2), so no key material can be in an export or an import. Keys are camelCase like the JSON API; unknown keys are errors with a "did you mean" and, for `version`, `password` and a reference written by ID, a sentence of their own.
- **Identity, so that importing twice changes nothing.** An entry with an `id` is that object. Without one it is the stored object of the same name (a host: any stored host that serves the first name it lists; a route: the same match and path on the matched host; a destination: the same address on the matched upstream), unless another entry claims it by `id` or took it first; anything else is new, with a new ID. This is the same in both modes (a replace that regenerated every ID would break every reference to them); a rename needs the `id`. New IDs come from `ConfigYamlImporter.RandomIds`, injectable so the fuzz target and the tests get the same ones every run.
- **Merge and replace** (`ConfigImportMerge`). *Replace* is the document the YAML describes: a list it leaves out is empty and `settings` it leaves out are the defaults, so what it doesn't name is deleted. *Merge* is the stored config with each of the YAML's objects replacing the stored object with its ID **as written** (not field by field: a key left out is gone from that object, which is the only meaning of "update" that can also unset something) and the new ones added after; nothing is deleted, and `settings` are replaced only if the YAML has them. Both are one `ReplaceConfigCommand` built on the revision the import read (the caller's `If-Match` can name another and then conflicts when it is stale), so validation, compilation, the lock-out guard, the diff, the audit event (`config.replace`, channel of the caller, the note "YAML import (merge)" unless given) and the single revision are the pipeline's. A dry run returns the changes and warnings and commits nothing.
- **Export is the revision, as YAML** (`ConfigYamlExporter`): the canonical document's JSON walked through the schema, in the order revisions store it, without versions, with a reference written as the name when exactly one object has it (else the ID), and without the empty lists and mappings that read back from absent as the same (`passiveHealth: {}` stays: absent means something else; so does a required `names: []`). A password has no key in either direction, so an access list's user is `{username, credentialId}` (§8). **The round trip is a property**: any valid generated document is exported, read back and has the same canonical bytes (versions aside, which the YAML doesn't carry), and `ConfigImportServiceTests` imports an export in replace mode on a fresh store and compares the SHA-256 of the revisions, which are equal (one commit on each side: the pipeline gives every object the revision's `Seq` as its version, so documents built by several commits have other versions than the same documents imported at once, and the hash covers them).
- **Safety** (`YamlLimits`, `YamlTreeReader`; the input can come from the API). The bytes must be valid UTF-8 (a BOM is fine), at most 4 MiB (the API answers 413), with exactly one document; nesting is at most 24 levels, scalars 512 KiB, keys 256 characters, anchors 64, alias uses 1 000, and the tree 200 000 nodes **counting every alias expansion** (an alias is expanded by copying inside that budget, so a billion-laughs document stops at it in tens of milliseconds and a few tens of megabytes). Every tag is refused (`yaml.tag`), mapping keys must be unique scalars, a merge key `<<` is just an unknown key. The scanner throws `InvalidOperationException` for some malformed input (an unterminated flow collection followed by more lines), which is reported as a syntax error like the rest. Errors carry stable codes (`yaml.*`, `YamlErrorCodes`), a JSON pointer into the YAML and its line and column, and never name a CLR or parser type.
- **Credentials** (decision). A user's `credentialId` in an import must be one the vault holds a password under (`IPasswordCredentials.Open(id).IsSet`) or one the live config already names (the vault lost it, which the pipeline reports as a warning; an export of such a config must stay importable). Otherwise it is `yaml.credential.unknown` at the key, and a **dry run reports it as an error too**, with the diff, so a dry run says what applying would do. A user written without a `credentialId` keeps the credential the stored list has for that name (`AccessListPasswords`), and a user nobody has a password for fails the validator as it always did. The check runs only for a caller who may write config, so it can't be used to find out which credential IDs exist.
- **Errors point into the YAML.** The pipeline's validation errors index the document it was sent and the compiler's backstop its canonical form; both are mapped back to the entry that wrote the object and to the key that wrote the field (`/hosts/0/profileId` is `/hosts/0/profile`), with the line. In a merge an error in a stored object the YAML left alone is a related error, still pointing into the document.
- **API** (behind `config:read` and `config:write`, in the OpenAPI document). `GET /api/v1/config/export[?revision=ID]` answers `application/yaml` with the revision as `ETag`, `no-store`, 404 for an unknown revision, 400 for a malformed one. `POST /api/v1/config/import?mode=merge|replace&dryRun=true|false[&force][&message]` takes `application/yaml` (or `text/yaml`, `text/plain`; 415 otherwise), merge by default, an optional `If-Match: "rev_..."` naming the revision the YAML was prepared against (412 when it is stale; without it the import uses the latest it read), and answers like every whole-config command: `ConfigCommandResult`, 422 problem details whose `errors` point into the YAML, 403 without the permission. A session cookie may send the YAML types (`AcceptsAdminContentTypes`) but not `text/plain`, the one a cross-site form can send, which only a token caller (curl) may use; a cross-site or form post is refused as for every unsafe route.
- **Dashboard** (Settings, "Export and import"). Download writes the live config through the browser's download (`paduraksa-config-r42.yaml`). Import takes pasted text (capped at 10 000 characters, at most 30 000 bytes of UTF-8, under the circuit's 32 KiB message) or a chosen file (streamed, up to 4 MiB), merge or replace, maps it on "Review import" (its errors listed with path and line, nothing else happens), and hands the command to the existing review sheet: its dry run is the diff, its warnings are shown, Apply (red for replace) makes the one revision and offers Undo. A conflict reloads the review. The page follows the no-filler rule: the two help lines say what each mode deletes, the one line under Export says what is never in it.
- **Tests.** Unit: the reader's caps, tags, aliases and bombs, the writer's quoting (any text reads back as the same text, a country code `NO` stays text), the schema mapping and its errors, merge, and the export/import round trip as a property over `ConfigGen`. Data: `ConfigImportServiceTests` on a real database (modes, dry run, errors, credentials, permission, conflict, the revision hash). Integration: the API (`ConfigTransferApiTests`) and the CLI against a running host. Dashboard: bUnit for the page and one Playwright test (paste, review, apply, download, read back as a no-op). Fuzz: `yaml-import` (§15).
- **Left out.** `certificates:` as a section (the document has none), offline `config export`, YAML comments (the exporter writes none and the reader drops them), and the declarative mode of §11.6, which builds on this.

### 11.6 Declarative mode (after v0.1)

For declarative deployments (Kubernetes manifests, Helm, Compose with a mounted file, NixOS, a git repo synced by Flux/Argo or `git-sync`): the desired config lives in files the deployment owns, and Paduraksa converges to them. Not a blocker for v0.1; it builds on the M10 YAML format and import pipeline.

- **Source.** `Paduraksa:Declarative:Path`, a file or a directory of `*.yaml` documents merged in name order (a ConfigMap mount is a directory). Watched with a debounce; Kubernetes updates a mounted ConfigMap by swapping the `..data` symlink, so the watcher follows the directory, not the inode. Also reconciled at startup, before the listeners open, so a pod never serves stale config after a restart.
- **Same pipeline.** A change in the files is one import (§11.5): validate → compile → diff → one revision with channel `declarative`, actor System, and the source file's SHA-256 in the message. An invalid file changes nothing: the last good revision keeps serving, the error is logged and audited, and it shows in the dashboard, in `/readyz` details and in a metric (`paduraksa_declarative_reconcile_ok`). Readiness stays green; a bad commit to the repo must not take the proxy out of rotation.
- **Ownership.** `Mode: Exclusive`: the files are the whole config (import mode `replace`), and the API, MCP and dashboard are read-only for config. `Mode: Managed`: the files manage the objects they name. Those objects are marked `managedBy: declarative` and refused by the other channels with a clear error, while objects the files don't name stay editable. Removing an object from the files deletes it, but only if it is marked managed. The dashboard shows a lock and the source path on managed objects.
- **Secrets stay out of the files.** Credentials are referenced, never inlined: `secretFile: /run/secrets/cloudflare-token` (a mounted Kubernetes Secret or Docker secret). The reconciler streams the file into the vault (`ISecretStore.ReplaceAsync`, keyed by a stable secret ID declared next to it) and re-imports it when it changes. The value never enters the revision document or the audit log. Certificates managed elsewhere (cert-manager, Vault PKI) are referenced the same way (`certFile`/`keyFile` on a `kubernetes.io/tls` Secret mount) and imported through `ICertificateImport`, so the trust boundary (§8) holds. The KEK comes from `PADURAKSA_MASTER_KEY_FILE` on a Secret mount.
- **Identity and setup.** An Owner can be declared, with a password hash or a `passwordFile`, plus roles for other users and API tokens whose secrets come from files. When an Owner is declared, the setup-token gate (§12.1) is skipped. Alternatively `Admin: Disabled` runs the proxy with no admin listener at all, only health and metrics, for fully hands-off clusters.
- **CI support.** `paduraksa config validate <path>` (offline: schema, references, compile; exit code and RFC 9457-style messages) and `paduraksa config diff <path> --against <url|data-dir>` show what a merge would change before it is applied.
- **Kubernetes shape.** Single replica, because SQLite and the vault are single-node: a StatefulSet (or a Deployment with `Recreate`) with a PVC for `/data`, the config as a ConfigMap and the credentials as Secrets. With declarative mode the database becomes mostly a cache of the files plus state (ACME accounts and orders, the vault, audit), so losing the PVC costs reissued certificates, not config. A Helm chart and an example manifest ship with the feature. Multiple replicas are fleet mode (§16.1), where the declarative files feed the control plane.
- **Possible later step.** A Kubernetes **Gateway API / Ingress controller** mode that derives hosts and routes from cluster resources instead of files. It is a different product surface (watching the API server, RBAC, status writes), so it isn't designed here.

---

## 12. Dashboard (Blazor Server)

**Stack:** .NET 10 Blazor Web App, interactive server render mode, served on the admin app only. Component library: **MudBlazor** (most complete, good data grids/dialogs). Alternative: Fluent UI Blazor (see open questions). Charts: lightweight SVG sparklines rendered by our own components, with ApexCharts for the detail views.

**UX principles**
- Live by default: status dots, request rates and cert expiry update over the existing circuit. The sampler pushes deltas, with no polling and no per-user timers on the hot path.
- **Every change goes through review**: an edit form leads to a "Review changes" drawer (diff + validation + warnings like "this host will lose its certificate"), then Apply. Undo in a toast rolls back to the previous revision.
- Inheritance is visible: every setting shows a badge for *Inherited · Profile · Overridden*.
- Command palette (Ctrl/⌘K): jump to a host, "issue cert", "rollback", "tail logs for ...".
- Keyboard-first forms, dark/light, mobile-friendly host list.

**Pages**
1. **Overview:** traffic, error rate, p50/p95 latency, top hosts, blocked requests, cert expiries, unhealthy upstreams, recent audit events.
2. **Hosts:** a searchable/taggable list with live sparkline, health and cert status. Host detail tabs: General · Routes · Upstream · TLS · Security (WAF) · Auth · Pages · Advanced · Metrics · History.
3. **Request simulator / "Why was this blocked?"** (§7.7) Enter a URL, IP, UA and headers and see the exact decision chain (host match → each policy → route → upstream), against the live config, an old revision or the change being reviewed. The same function is exposed to the API/MCP, and access-log entries link straight into it.
4. **Certificates:** list, issue (wizard), upload, local CA, renewal timeline, ACME order logs.
5. **Upstreams & health:** destinations, probe results, history.
6. **Security:** global rule sets, rate-limit policies, geo policy, presets, blocked-request explorer. As built in M7: an overview, rate limits, access lists, rules and sign-in (auth providers) pages.
7. **Live logs:** streaming tail with filters (**Live tail**, As built (M9) below).
8. **History:** revision timeline, diff viewer, rollback.
9. **Audit:** filterable, exportable, with hash-chain verification status.
10. **Settings:** global defaults, profiles, DNS providers, ACME accounts, notifications, users & roles, API tokens, sessions, backup/export.

**As built (M5): the dashboard foundation.**
- **Stack and wiring.** A Blazor Web App in `Paduraksa.Dashboard` (Razor class library, Application and Contracts only), interactive server render mode, **MudBlazor 9** (the plan's choice held: it has what the later pages need, works within the CSP below and ships its assets in the package). `AdminApp` adds it after the API: `UsePaduraksaAdminApi` → `UsePaduraksaAdminAuth` → `UsePaduraksaAdminWeb` (authorization, antiforgery and the dashboard's pipeline hooks) → health, identity and resource API → the sign-in form endpoints (`MapPaduraksaAdminWeb`) → `MapStaticAssets` → `MapPaduraksaDashboard` at `/`. The dashboard owns its paths (`DashboardRoutes`); the server hands them to `Paduraksa.Admin.Web` as `AdminWebOptions`, so neither project references the other. Static files (Blazor's `blazor.web.js`, MudBlazor's CSS and JS, the dashboard's own `app.css`, `paduraksa.js`, `favicon.svg`) come from the app with fingerprinted URLs; no CDN, no web font. The server sets `RequiresAspNetWebAssets` (its own project has no `.razor` file, so the SDK would otherwise leave `blazor.web.js` out) and maps a build output's static web assets itself (the host does that only in Development).
- **Authentication.** One session for the API and the dashboard: the same `__Host-paduraksa-session` cookie, set only on ordinary HTTP responses. The sign-in, second-factor, sign-out and setup pages are plain HTML forms (static rendering, `[ExcludeFromInteractiveRouting]`) that post to form endpoints in `Paduraksa.Admin.Web` (`/auth/signin`, `/auth/second-factor`, `/auth/signout`, `/auth/setup`). These run the API's own steps (`SignInFlow`: throttle, bounded password derivation, lockout, audit, cookie; channel `dashboard`), need the antiforgery token the page rendered (`__Host-paduraksa-antiforgery`, `SameSite=Strict`) and pass the CSRF middleware's same-origin check (`RequireSameOrigin`), and answer with a 303 carrying only an error code (the API's problem codes, plus `setup.passwordMismatch`) and a local `returnUrl` (anything else becomes `/`). Outside `/api` the authentication middleware reads the session cookie leniently (valid: caller and `HttpContext.User` = a `SessionIdentity`; partial: the partial session; anything else: anonymous; a bearer token never counts); pages carry `[Authorize]` and the `Paduraksa.Session` scheme's challenge redirects to `/signin?returnUrl=...`. A TOTP code is accepted with the space authenticators show.
- **Circuits and revocation.** `DashboardAuthenticationStateProvider` takes the request's `SessionIdentity` (the circuit never holds the cookie), adds the user's permissions (`DashboardPrincipal.Has(Permission)`; `IfPermitted` and the navigation hide what the user can't use, the services still decide), and re-checks the session by ID (`SessionService.CheckAsync`): in the background every `Paduraksa:Admin:SessionRecheckInterval` (30 s; does not count as use, so an open page still idles out), on circuit activity at most every 10 s, and on every service call (`DashboardCaller`, counts as use). A revoked or expired session, a disabled or deleted user, or a password change elsewhere makes the state anonymous; the router then reloads the page to `/signin?notice=sessionEnded`, and a circuit still talking 10 s later is torn down (`SessionCircuitHandler`). Changed roles change the permissions on the next check.
- **Setup.** While setup is incomplete the gate redirects a browser opening any page (a `GET` accepting `text/html`) to `/setup` and opens only `/setup`, `/auth/setup`, `/_framework` and `/_content`; scripts still get the 503 problem, the circuit hub stays shut. The page is one form in two numbered steps (claim the instance with the setup token, then the Owner); it calls `SetupService` through the same flow as `POST /api/v1/setup` and signs the Owner in. Like the API's setup endpoint it is refused through the proxy (`setup.viaProxy`).
- **Hub, proxy, CSRF.** `/_blazor` needs a signed-in caller (else a bare 401) and passes the same-origin check on every method, the WebSocket handshake included. Through the proxy (§3.1) the circuit's WebSocket is an ordinary upgrade on the internal listener, hop secret checked there (tested end to end: negotiate, 101, and 401 without the cookie).
- **CSP and headers.** HTML documents outside `/api` get `default-src 'none'; script-src 'self'; style-src 'self' 'unsafe-inline'; img-src 'self' data:; font-src 'self'; connect-src 'self'; manifest-src 'self'; base-uri 'self'; form-action 'self'; frame-ancestors 'none'` (`DashboardSecurity`; the hook `AdminPipelineHooks.HtmlContentSecurityPolicy`); no inline script, no `eval`. `'unsafe-inline'` for styles is MudBlazor's need (style attributes and the theme provider's `<style>` of CSS variables) and applies only to the dashboard's pages; the API, the static files and the Scalar docs keep their own policies. Blazor's own `frame-ancestors` header is turned off (the full policy covers it). Pages and the hub are `Cache-Control: no-store`; fingerprinted files keep their long caching. HSTS, `nosniff`, `no-referrer` and `X-Frame-Options: DENY` as for the rest of the admin. The browser test fails on any CSP report in the console.
- **Look.** A custom MudBlazor theme (`PaduraksaTheme`): slate neutrals and one jade-teal accent (`#3FBFAE` dark, `#0E7D6F` light), the platform's UI font on a small regular scale, 10 px corners, outlined cards. Dark by default; with no choice of their own a person gets their system's setting (a script records it in a cookie so the next page renders right from the first byte), and the top bar's toggle stores their choice (`paduraksa-theme`). The shell: a top bar (mark, product name; theme toggle; user menu with name, username, roles, "My account" and sign-out), a responsive side navigation that folds away below the `md` breakpoint, and pages under a `PageHeader`. Conventions: `EmptyState` (icon, one line of what is missing, one of what to do, one action), MudBlazor skeletons while loading, snackbars bottom-right, newest on top, no duplicates. The sign-in pages mirror the palette in plain CSS.
- **Pages.** Overview (`/`): config source and degraded state, the latest revision, host and upstream counts, certificate count and those in use that end within 30 days, each shown only with the permission to read it; its data is carried from the server's render into the circuit (`[PersistentState]`), so it does not flash. Placeholders with a "soon" badge: Audit (the config pages and the certificate and settings pages below replaced the others). A degraded start shows a banner on every page.
- **Data Protection.** The admin's key ring lives in the vault (§8, the Data Protection key ring): antiforgery tokens and circuits survive a restart. The foundation's in-memory seam is gone.
- **Tests.** `Paduraksa.Tests.Dashboard`: bUnit (the sign-in pages' forms, messages and return addresses; the auth state provider: permissions, revocation, role changes, the background re-check, the circuit's grace period; permission-based visibility; the theme and its toggle) and a Playwright smoke test against a real `PaduraksaHost` (setup through the page, TOTP enrolment, sign-out, sign-in with password and TOTP, the overview, the light theme and a phone-sized window, a revoked session ending the open page, sign-in with a recovery code). It skips when no Chromium is installed: `pwsh tests/Paduraksa.Tests.Dashboard/bin/Release/net10.0/playwright.ps1 install chromium` (or the `Microsoft.Playwright.CLI` dotnet tool), or `PADURAKSA_E2E_CHROMIUM=/path/to/chromium`; `PADURAKSA_E2E_SCREENSHOTS=<dir>` keeps screenshots. Integration tests cover the pipeline (gate, forms, antiforgery and same-origin, hub, CSP) and the dashboard through the proxy.
- **Not yet.** Nothing of this list: the audit page and "My account" came with the settings pages (below).

**As built (M5): the config pages.**
- **Seam.** `IDashboardConfig` (`Paduraksa.Dashboard/Config`) is everything the config pages ask of the Application layer: the current config and its commands (dry runs too), the history timeline (`RevisionQueryService.ListWithChangesAsync`, the one Application addition: revision pages with each revision's change list, read from the stored summary without decompressing a document), a revision with its document, the certificate listing, the user list (to name who made a revision; empty without `users:read`), the simulator, and `Watch` over `ConfigService.Changes`. `DashboardConfig` is the real one, per circuit; the bUnit tests swap in an in-memory one. Reads are not authorized by the services (as on the API): each page checks `config:read` (`certificates:read`, `simulate`) first; commands and simulations carry the user's `CommandOrigin` and the services decide.
- **Live.** Every page loads once its circuit is live (the prerender shows the skeleton) and re-reads on each commit through `Watch`; no timer polls. An open editor says when its object changed elsewhere (with Reload); the Apply would be a conflict anyway.
- **Typed IDs at the edge.** `/hosts/{Key}` and `/upstreams/{Key}` (since the drawers: the list with that object's drawer open, below) take a string segment and parse it into a `HostId`/`UpstreamId` (anything else, a wrong-type ID included, is "not found"); `?revision=` parses into a `ConfigRevisionId`. Addresses are built from typed IDs (`DashboardRoutes.Host(id)`), and new objects get fresh typed IDs in the form models.
- **Review before apply.** Every edit opens "Review changes" (`ReviewPanel`, since the drawers a level of the drawer stack, below): the command runs as a dry run on the current head and shows the diff (`ChangeView`: grouped by top-level object, routes and destinations nested, also those an added or removed object carries; each field old and new, read from the two documents, references by name), the warnings (the compiler's, placed by object name, plus "will lose its certificate", "will stop answering", names given up, an upstream left without destinations) and the errors by place; an object command's errors also go back to the form's fields. Apply runs the same command with the version the form opened with. A stale version or a deleted object is a conflict with Reload; a commit that couldn't go live stays on screen as "saved as #N, but it isn't live" with the publisher's errors. A commit leaves a toast with **Undo** (`ConfigUndo`): a rollback to the parent revision (a replace with the empty config for #1), based on the revision it undoes, so it is refused when someone changed the config in between; a newer toast replaces the last, since only the latest change can be undone. The drawer sits above the toasts, closes with Escape and focuses its heading. "Simulate this change" runs a request against the draft in place, and the draft stays offered to the simulator page (`ReviewedDraft`) until it is applied.
- **Forms.** Hosts: names, kind (proxy routes with match, path and upstream; redirect target, status and path; fixed status, content type and body; admin), the certificate binding (auto, specific from the usable certificates, none, which lifts an inherited HTTPS redirect), the HTTPS redirect showing what it inherits, the profile, and under Advanced the TLS minimum, TCP ALPN, HTTP/3, an IP allow list, tags and notes. The form keeps what it doesn't show (a route's own settings). Upstreams: destinations (IDs kept, so YARP health survives), load balancing, active and passive health, a connection limit and notes, with the hosts and routes that use it under the form; deleting one in use is refused up front with the routes named. Inline validation is the real `ConfigValidator` over the current config with the edited object put last (as the command pipeline validates it): errors show once a field is touched, all of them (with a summary) after a review attempt. Enter submits to review.
- **Lists.** Hosts: searchable, with a status dot (off, a live warning or a certificate problem, serving), where requests go, HTTPS behaviour and the certificate state of the names (`HostOverview`: auto against the usable inventory, exact or parent wildcard; specific by ID; expiring within 14 days). Upstreams: destinations, policy, health and usage. Both reworked with the drawers (below).
- **History.** The timeline (number, who: "You", a user's name with `users:read`, a token, the CLI, Paduraksa; channel, time, message, what changed in counts) with "load older", the selected revision's diff against its parent or any loaded revision, Roll back (through the drawer, based on the latest) and Simulate.
- **Simulator.** URL, client IP, method and headers, with arrival, proxy address, TLS version, ALPN and SNI under Advanced; the target is live, a revision (`?revision=`), or the change under review. The outcome comes first in its tone, then the destinations with live health, the notes, and the trace as a vertical list of steps with verdict icons, summaries and facts. A refused request marks its fields.
- **Permissions.** Add, edit, delete, roll back and Apply only show with `config:write` (a read-only form otherwise), the simulator needs `simulate`, the certificate picker and states `certificates:read`; the services still refuse on their own.
- **Errors and 404.** An `ErrorBoundary` around the page body turns an exception while prerendering or live into an error card in the shell (logged by the framework, cleared by the next navigation) instead of the API's JSON 500; a broken circuit shows Blazor's error bar, styled. An address nothing answers, asked for by a browser (GET or HEAD accepting HTML, outside `/api`, the hub and the static files: a route constraint, so the API keeps its 405 and 415), renders the app with a 404 and the router's not-found page for a signed-in user and challenges anyone else; everything else keeps routing's answer.
- **Secrets.** None reach a component: the pages hold Contracts DTOs (config documents, certificate public facts, revision metadata) only.
- **Tests.** bUnit: the host form (kind switching, inline errors, what it sends, read-only), the review drawer (diff and warnings, Apply with the opened version, errors by place, conflict at review and at apply, publish failed), the diff, the simulation trace, and the pages per role (adding, editing and rolling back hidden without `config:write`, the simulator refused without `simulate`, a non-host ID not found, an upstream in use refused, the list following a commit). Playwright (`ConfigPagesEndToEndTests`): create an upstream and a proxy host through review and apply, see it listed, simulate a request that forwards to it, edit and undo from the toast, the history with all four revisions, the styled 404 and a phone width, with no CSP report or console error; screenshots of each page with `PADURAKSA_E2E_SCREENSHOTS`.
- **Not yet.** Route reordering; host tags as list filters. (A route's own access list is picked in its settings, M7.)

**As built (M5): certificates, ACME accounts and DNS providers.**
- **Certificates (`/certificates`).** One list of the inventory plus the process's built-in certificates (the development placeholder, now shared with the admin container when enabled): name and names, source (ACME, uploaded, self-signed, local CA, built-in), status (an issuance in progress shows as "Issuing"), a colour-coded countdown (green, amber inside 30 days, red inside 7 or after) with the date, issuer and the hosts that use it, under counters (active, expiring, failed or expired, pending); skeletons, an empty state, and cards with labelled cells below 900 px. Which hosts use a certificate is `CertificateUsage` (Application): the compiler's selection (Specific, the best match per name of every enabled Auto host, the default) applied to the metadata, shown only with `config:read`. The admin certificate is a read-only card (what it is, its names and countdown, "add names with `Paduraksa:Admin:CertificateNames`") and the local CA root has a "Trust the local CA" card: name, validity, SHA-256 fingerprint, the `/api/v1/ca/root.crt` and `root.pem` downloads (`download` links, which Blazor's router leaves alone) and how to install it on Windows, macOS, Linux, Firefox and phones. A row opens a detail drawer: status, validity bar, names, users, issuer, subject, key, serial, fingerprint, the chain's issuers, the public chain PEM, and for ACME the account, challenge, order state, next renewal, the ARI window, the last attempt, and a failure with the CA's message and the next try.
- **Actions** (with `certificates:write`; the services still decide). Upload a PEM (chain and PKCS#8 key), a PEM plus a separate key file (read as one stream through `PemConcatStream`, nothing buffered) or a PFX with an optional password. **This goes through the circuit, not the API's multipart endpoint (a change from §9's note):** `InputFile` streams the browser's file into `CertificateOperations.UploadAsync` during the call (capped at `CertificateImportLimits.MaxBytes`), the password leaves the component's state before anything is awaited and is handed over as a `char[]` cleared in `finally`, and the form forgets the file reference afterwards. Self-signed and local-CA leaves (names, label, validity, key; issuing from the local CA creates the root first when there is none), "Create the local CA", re-issue (self-signed and local-CA leaves, confirmed), delete (confirmed; refused for the admin certificate and built-ins), ACME request (names, account, HTTP-01 or DNS-01 with a provider, label, key; a wildcard needs DNS-01, an IP HTTP-01, checked in the form and again by the service) and renew now.
- **Live ACME progress.** `AcmeIssuanceService` takes an optional `IProgress<AcmeOrderProgress>` on request and renew and keeps the latest step of every running issuance for `GetProgress` (Contracts: `AcmeProgressStage` Starting, OrderCreated, Authorizing, ChallengePublished, PropagationCheck, Validating, Validated, Finalizing, Issued, Failed with the sanitised message and `RetryAt`; public facts only, a throwing reporter is logged and ignored). The dialog pushes each report to the page (no polling) and shows the steps order → authorizations → challenge published → DNS propagation (DNS-01 only) → validated → finalized → issued, skipped steps of a resumed order, the elapsed time, and a failure with the CA's message, the retry time and "Retry now". The run uses the application's stopping token, like the API: closing the dialog ("Continue in the background") doesn't stop it. The detail drawer is the one place that polls: every 2 s, while it is open, it reads `GetProgress` (in memory) for an ACME certificate and the stored order while a run is going, so a run started by the scheduler or another tab shows too.
- **Live lists.** `ConfigService.OperationalChanges` (`OperationalChangeFeed`, Application) announces every audited change made through `ApplyOperationalChangeAsync` or `ApplyCertificateChangeAsync` after its commit (the audit action and target only); `LiveUpdates` (dashboard) listens to it and, where host usage matters, to the config change feed, and refreshes the page once per burst (300 ms). No list polls.
- **Settings (`/settings`)** is a hub of tiles; ACME accounts and DNS providers are its first two pages (`/settings/acme-accounts`, `/settings/dns-providers`, `providers:read` to see, `providers:write` to change), the settings pages below fill the rest. **ACME accounts:** CA, contacts, key algorithm, staging and EAB markers, how many certificates use each; add with presets (Let's Encrypt, its staging, ZeroSSL and Google Trust Services, which need EAB) or any https directory URL, an optional contact email, the CA's terms (required, linking the CA's page), and optionally the EAB key ID and HMAC key; deactivate (final, confirmed). **DNS providers:** Cloudflare or deSEC, a label and the API token; the form names the one host the token is sent to (`DnsProviderService.ApiHostOf`); Test shows the provider's verdict inline (accepted, or its error without the token) and runs after adding or replacing a token; replace the token; delete (confirmed). **Secrets are write-only:** password fields, emptied before the call, handed over as `char[]` and turned into bytes only in `ProviderActions` (which zeroes them when the service returns); nothing returns a secret, the submission records print `***`, and the vault keeps the value.
- **Shared pieces** (new files): `ConfirmActionDialog`, `LiveUpdates`, `QrCode`/`QrCodeImage`. Toasts follow the config pages: success bottom-right, a refusal in the service's words.
- **Not yet.** Changing a certificate's label or notes; replacing an uploaded certificate under its ID from the page (the API has it); moving certificates between ACME accounts or providers; a CNAME-delegation hint for DNS-01.

**As built (M5): settings, My account and the audit log.**
- **Settings hub.** Tiles (each shown only with the permission its page needs) for **Global defaults** (`/settings/defaults`, `config:read`), **Profiles** (`/settings/profiles`, `/settings/profiles/new`, `/settings/profiles/{prof_...}`), ACME accounts, DNS providers, **Users and roles** (`/settings/users`, `users:read`), **API tokens** (`/settings/tokens`, `tokens:own`) and **My account** (`/account`, also from the user menu). Addresses in `SettingsRoutes`; every page has the "Settings" way back.
- **Global defaults.** One form over `GlobalSettings`, applied through the review drawer as `UpdateSettingsCommand` with the version it opened with: the bottom layer of host settings (HTTPS redirect, minimum TLS, protocols over TCP, HTTP/3, HSTS, an IP allow list; each "Built-in default (...)" or a value, `HostSettingsFields`), what a request for an unknown name gets (404, 421, close), the default certificate (usable ones, with `certificates:read`), and the client address: direct, behind a proxy (proxies, header, `X-Forwarded-Proto`), Cloudflare proxy or Cloudflare Tunnel (`True-Client-IP`, the `X-Forwarded-For` fallback), as cards; switching modes drops the options the validator would refuse. A line of help per setting. Inline validation is the real validator with the settings put in (`InlineValidation.ForSettings`); a change made elsewhere reloads an untouched form and puts a banner over an edited one. HSTS is in the config model since M6, so it has its group (below).
- **Profiles.** What each sets and the hosts that use it (a table since the drawers, below); the form (name, notes, the same fields, each "Inherit (the global value)") goes through review (`UpsertProfileCommand`/`DeleteProfileCommand`, IDs typed at the edge); deleting one in use is refused up front with the hosts named.
- **Users and roles.** The users with their roles, enabled, locked, two-step on or off and, with `sessions:all`, when last seen (their newest session); a card says what each role may do in one line. With `users:write`: add (username, display name, initial password, roles; checked as the service checks), edit (display name, roles, enabled, sent as one `PATCH`-like update of only what changed) and delete (confirmed). The last-Owner guard's refusal is shown in the dialog that asked (`identity.lastOwner`'s words: make someone else an Owner first).
- **API tokens.** One's own, and "Everyone's" with `tokens:all`; revoked and expired ones on request. Create: name, expiry (7, 30, 90 days, a year, never, or a date), scopes by area with read-only and "all you can" presets; only what the user holds is offered ("a token never exceeds its owner"), and never `tokens:*` or `sessions:all` (tokens can't manage tokens or sessions). The secret shows once in the same dialog, with Copy and a "you won't see it again" notice; a click beside the dialog doesn't close it, and closing it drops the secret from the page's state. Revoke is confirmed.
- **My account.** Profile (username read-only; the display name editable with `users:write`, as the service allows, read-only otherwise), password (current, new with a strength meter, again; on success "your other sessions were signed out"; the fields are emptied as the change is sent), two-step verification (set up with the wizard's `TwoStepEnrollment`; new recovery codes and turning it off, each behind the password; recovery codes shown once with Copy and "Download .txt", made in the page), and sessions (this one marked, browser and system from the user agent, address, last seen; end one, or "sign out everywhere else", confirmed).
- **Audit (`/audit`, `audit:read`).** Filters: time range (1 hour to all, or from and until in UTC), who (you, users with `users:read`, tokens one may list, Paduraksa, the CLI), action (exact or `prefix*`, with suggestions), target and outcome (`AuditFilter.Outcome`, new in Application and on the API as `?outcome=`). Rows: when, the action in words with its dotted name as a chip coloured by area, who (a name where the page may read it, else the kind and a short ID), the outcome chip; a row opens to the time, channel, client, target (linked when it has a page), revision, event ID and the details pretty-printed (never a secret, PLAN §5.2). Pages through the cursor ("Load older events"); new events reload the first page, or offer "Show them" once older pages are loaded. The hash-chain card verifies on load and on "Verify now": intact with the count and the head hash's start, or broken with the first bad event and why. "Export JSON Lines" writes every event the filter matches (up to 100 000), field names as the API's, made in the page and saved through the browser.
- **Seams.** `IDashboardIdentity` (users, password, TOTP, sessions, tokens; `DashboardIdentity` over the Application services) and `IDashboardAudit` (`AuditQueryService`). Password checks take the admin app's derivation slots (`IPasswordCheckSlots`, the server hands over `AdminThrottle`'s; busy is "try again in a moment"). Successful identity changes are announced on `IdentityChangeFeed`, so lists of users, tokens and sessions follow other circuits without a timer (changes through the API or CLI show on the next load); the defaults and profiles follow the config change feed.
- **Secrets.** Passwords, the TOTP URI and secret, recovery codes and a new token's secret live in component state only while their step shows and are dropped on close, on the next step and on disposal; no DTO the pages hold carries one.
- **Shared pieces.** `SecretValue` (shown once, Copy), `RecoveryCodesView`, `DashboardBrowser` (clipboard and saving a file from a .NET stream, in `paduraksa.js`), `settings.css`. Dialogs are MudBlazor dialogs (inline for the forms, so their state is the page's), confirmations `ConfirmActionDialog`, the QR code `QrCodeImage`. The certificate, ACME account and DNS provider forms moved from dialogs to drawers (below).
- **Fixes from the config pages.** The review drawer's footer wraps its actions and its columns can shrink, so Apply never leaves the window (the cut-off Apply in the 1366 px screenshot was the sheet caught 32 px into its slide-in; browser screenshots now take finite animations at their end). Toasts are at most 420 px wide with 20 px margins (full width less 8 px on a phone), and pages leave room below their last card to scroll clear of one; the drawer and dialogs sit above them.
- **Tests.** bUnit (`SettingsPagesTests`, `AccountAndAuditTests`): a token's secret shown once and gone from markup and state after closing, only held scopes offered; the last-Owner refusal and what an edit sends; the add-user form's checks; the users, defaults and audit pages per role; the defaults through the drawer with mode options dropped; a profile in use refused; the TOTP enrolment's states and what each drops; recovery codes once; sessions and ending one; the password change; the audit filters, a row's details and the chain card intact and broken; the export line. Playwright (`SettingsEndToEndTests`): a Viewer added and, in a second browser, offered no change; a token made on the page answers `GET /api/v1/auth/me` as a token; a global default through review and apply, with Apply inside the window at 1366 and 390 px; the audit page with those events and an intact chain; My account: TOTP enrolment, two sessions, ending the other browser's, which lands on the sign-in page; no sideways scroll at 390 px on the new pages.
- **Not yet.** Changing one's own display name without `users:write` (the service doesn't allow it; an Owner can); identity changes made through the API or CLI reaching open pages live; a CSV export. Built since: notifications (M9), the YAML export and import page (§11.5) and the backup page (§8.4).

**As built (M5): drawers and the design language (UX pass 1).** Creating and editing moved from separate pages and dialogs into drawers that stack, so making a host with a new upstream and a certificate happens in one place, as in nginx-proxy-manager.
- **The stack.** `DrawerStack` (per circuit, `Paduraksa.Dashboard/Drawers`) holds the open levels; `DrawerHost`, once in the layout after `MudLayout`, renders them: each a sheet from the right (520, 640 or 780 px, the whole width of a phone), the top one live, the ones under it shifted 28 px aside per level, dimmed by the top level's backdrop and `inert` (the page behind is inert too while any drawer is open). A component opens a level with `Push<TComponent>(parameters, width)` (or `OpenAsync`, which waits) and the level's component gets the `DrawerLevel` as a cascading value: `Close(value)` hands a result back to the level below and closes it, `Cancel()` closes without one (a level closing takes the levels above it along, cancelled), `IsDirty` says it holds unsaved changes, `Busy` keeps it open while it applies or an issuance shows its progress, `ResultOnClose` is what it hands back however it is closed (a commit that couldn't go live). Escape (`paduraksa.js` hands it over only while focus is in the top drawer and no list, menu or dialog is open), the backdrop and the close or back button act on the top level only and, when it is dirty, ask "Discard your changes?" in the drawer itself (Escape again keeps editing); leaving the page with a dirty drawer asks the same (`NavigationLock`). Focus moves into a level as it opens (its first field when the form focuses one, else its title, which names the dialog) and back to what opened it as it closes. Dialogs (a confirmation asked from a drawer) sit above the stack: the theme raises MudBlazor's dialog z-index to 1700 (tooltips 1800); the stack is at 1600 and up, above the toasts; MudBlazor places a select's list above its field, so pickers work in any level.
- **Review is a level.** `ReviewPanel` is "Review changes" as a level (`DrawerStack.ReviewAsync(ReviewRequest)` returns a `ReviewOutcome`: applied, or reload asked); Save in a form drawer stacks it on the form, and Apply closes both. Every change still goes through it. `ReviewDrawer` keeps its old parameters for the pages whose form isn't a drawer (global defaults, a rollback, the wizard's first host) and pushes the panel for them.
- **Frame.** `DrawerShell` is every drawer's frame: the way back to the level below ("‹ New host", named "Back to New host"), an overline, the title with an icon, a line under it, actions beside the close button, banners that don't scroll away (changed elsewhere, read-only, in use), a body that scrolls, and a footer for the actions (Delete on the left, Cancel, then the primary). With `OnSubmit` the drawer is one form, so Enter submits. A form that brings its own buttons (the ACME account, DNS provider and certificate forms, shared with the wizard) keeps them in the same bar at the drawer's foot (CSS).
- **Hosts, upstreams, profiles.** `HostDrawer`, `UpstreamDrawer` and `ProfileDrawer` (`Paduraksa.Dashboard/Editors`) replace the editor pages. They share `DraftDrawer<TModel, TConfig>` for the life every draft drawer has - the caller and its permissions, the document, inline validation, the dirty guard, review-before-apply, "changed elsewhere" and reload - and each keeps its markup and its real differences (host staging, upstream probes, the profile's usage and settings count). Every reference in the host form is a picker of what exists with "New ..." last (`RefPicker`): a route's upstream (each with its address, and the chosen one's address under the field), the certificate (Automatic, the usable ones, None) and the profile. "New upstream..." and "New profile..." stack the other form in stage mode: it is checked against the host's draft and handed back without being applied, and the host form picks it, marks it "New" and lists what goes with the host. **Staging:** a host with staged objects is reviewed and applied as one `ReplaceConfigCommand` revision (`StagedChange`: the latest document with the host put in its place and the staged objects it still uses added; a staged upstream it no longer routes to is dropped), after the same version check an object command makes (a host changed elsewhere since the form opened is not overwritten), with "New host app.example.com with upstream app" as the revision's message; cancelling the host drops what it staged, so nothing is left behind. A host without staged objects still goes as `UpsertHostCommand`. A conflict at apply rebuilds the replace on the latest config, keeping what was staged. (The audit action of such a revision is `config.replace`.) "New certificate..." stacks the certificate flow (`CertificateCreateDrawer`) with the host's names: a certificate is a vault object, so it is issued or uploaded at once and comes back selected (a pending ACME certificate leaves the binding on Automatic, which picks it up once issued). A form is dirty when what it would send differs from what it opened with (the canonical JSON of the objects, `StagedChange.Fingerprint`); a staged object makes it dirty. The upstream form checks a destination on request (`UpstreamProbe`, as the wizard: a TCP connection, reachable or not) and lists the hosts and routes that use it.
- **Certificates, ACME accounts, DNS providers.** A certificate opens in `CertificateDrawer` (it reads the inventory itself and follows its changes, so its address opens it from anywhere); Renew now stacks the progress on it, re-issue and delete ask in a dialog over it. "Add certificate" opens `CertificateCreateDrawer` with a choice of where it comes from (an ACME CA, the local CA, self-signed, upload; the "More" menu goes straight to one, and creates the local CA); while an issuance shows its progress the drawer ends only with Done or Continue in the background. In the ACME form the account and the DNS provider are pickers with "New ACME account..." and "New DNS provider..." (or a button when there is none), which stack `AcmeAccountDrawer` and `DnsProviderDrawer` (also what the settings pages open) and come back selected. The dialogs they replace are gone.
- **Addresses.** `RoutedDrawer` ties a list page's route key to its root drawer: `/hosts/{host_...}`, `/hosts/new`, `/upstreams/{ups_...}`, `/upstreams/new`, `/settings/profiles/{prof_...}`, `/settings/profiles/new` and the new `/certificates/{crt_...}` are the list with that drawer open; a row opens its address, closing the drawer goes back to the list's address, the browser's back closes it, and leaving the page closes it with every level stacked on it. The key is parsed at the edge as before; one that isn't an ID of the right type opens a "not found" drawer (`NotFoundDrawer`), and an object deleted under an open drawer says so in it.
- **Lists.** Hosts read like nginx-proxy-manager's: the names (more as chips, and tags), where requests go line by line ("/api → app  http://10.0.0.5:8080", a redirect's target, a fixed status), the certificate state with the HTTPS behaviour, and the status (Online, Attention with the reason as a tooltip, Off) with an on/off switch for writers (not on the admin host) that goes through review like any change. Upstreams: name and policy, the destinations' addresses, and the hosts that use each (links). Profiles: what each sets, the hosts that use it, delete. The certificates list uses the same table.
- **Design language** (shared pieces for pass 2): `PageHeader` (the title, an optional lead, and the actions with the primary last; no overline, since the navigation and the data already say where a page is), `ListToolbar` (search, how many match, the list's own controls; hidden on an empty list), the data table (`pdk-dt`: rows as grids with columns per list, the main cell's link the keyboard's way in, cards with labelled cells below 900 px), `EmptyState`, `StatusChip` (a state in a word, tone and dot or icon), `FormSection` (a heading, an optional line, an optional control, the fields; sections divided by a rule instead of cards), `RefPicker`, `DrawerShell`. A line of copy stays only if it says what the title, the navigation or the data does not and helps the person decide or act. `app.css` gained the Drawers and Lists sections and lost the editor pages' and card lists' rules; `certificates.css` lost its own table.
- **Tests.** bUnit (`DrawerStackTests`): push, pop and the result handed back, a level closing the ones above it, Escape and the backdrop on the top level only with the levels below inert, the discard question (Escape, keep editing, discard), a busy level staying open, an upstream made from the host drawer applied with the host as one `ReplaceConfigCommand` (one dry run, one commit, the message), and cancelling the host leaving nothing behind; the host drawer at its address (read-only without `config:write`), a wrong or wrong-type ID, closing a drawer going back to the list and leaving the page closing it, the upstream drawer refusing a delete in use, the upstreams list naming the hosts; the review tests run through the drawer host. Playwright (`ConfigPagesEndToEndTests`): a host made in its drawer with a new upstream stacked on it (Escape closing only the upstream's), both applied as revision #1, listed with the upstream's address, the upstream's drawer with its usage, the simulator, an edit undone from the toast, the list's switch reviewed and cancelled, "New certificate..." from the host's drawer issuing from the local CA and coming back selected, the history, the not-found drawer, and at 390 px the lists as cards, a stacked drawer with its actions inside the window, the discard question, and no sideways scroll; screenshots of each. The wizard's test opens a certificate's drawer by its row and uploads through the new drawer.
- **Not yet** after pass 1: the other pages (pass 2, below).

**As built (M5): the rest of the dashboard in the design language (UX pass 2).**
- **Where things live.** The side navigation lists areas, and an area's pages share a row of tabs under the page header (`SectionTabs`, through `PageHeader.Area`): **Hosts · Upstreams · Profiles**, **Certificates · ACME accounts · DNS providers**, and **All settings · Global defaults · Users and roles · API tokens · My account**. Profiles, ACME accounts and DNS providers are found where they are used (their addresses under `/settings` are unchanged); the navigation marks a page's area by the longest address it starts with (`DashboardNavigation.ActiveFor`: a profile marks Hosts, an ACME account Certificates, My account Settings), drawn as plain links now rather than MudBlazor's nav links. The settings hub groups its links by what they are about (Hosts, Certificates, People and access), shown only with the permission each page needs; the "Settings" way back on each page gave way to the tabs. The user menu gained API tokens.
- **Overview.** First what needs the person, most serious first, each with the drawer that fixes it (`HomeSnapshot.AttentionOf`: a certificate that failed, a certificate in use that ends within 30 days, red inside 7; a host the live config warns about or whose certificate is a problem; an upstream a host uses with no destination switched on; a master key beside the vault, for a user who holds `vault:manage` (threat model G3); Owners and Admins with no second factor, for a user who reads users (G4: one line for the viewer's own account, linking to My account, one for the others together, linking to Users; nothing forces a second factor and a disabled user is not counted); and a degraded config store), or "Nothing needs you". A link that leaves the dashboard (the install guide) opens in a tab of its own. Then the counts (hosts online and off, upstreams, certificates, the live revision; two by two on a phone), the first six hosts with where their requests go and their status (those that need attention first), and the latest five revisions, each linked. "Add host" sits in the header. It reads through `IDashboardConfig` now (testable), and the whole snapshot still travels from the first render into the circuit.
- **Drawers instead of dialogs.** Adding and changing a user (`UserDrawer`), creating an API token (`TokenDrawer`: its secret shows once in the drawer, which then has no close button and ignores Escape and the backdrop until "I've copied it", and drops the secret as it goes), two-step verification (`TwoFactorDrawer`: the enrolment, new recovery codes behind the password, shown once the same way, and turning it off) and an audit event's details (`AuditEventDrawer`: outcome and action, who, when, channel, client, target linked, revision, event, the details). Confirmations (delete a user, revoke a token, end a session, re-issue or delete a certificate) stay dialogs, which sit above the drawers. The users and tokens lists use the shared data table (cards with labelled cells on a phone).
- **Drawer stack.** A drawer opened from a page without an address of its own (a user, a token, an audit event) closes when the person goes to another page, the browser's back included (`DrawerLevel.Routed`/`OwnerPath`); one with an address closes as its address goes, as before. Leaving with unsaved changes asks first, now verified for the browser's back within the app (a back to a page loaded outside the app, a full document load, isn't something the page can hold). A stacked level's header shows the trail of every level under it ("app.home.test / New certificate / Add an ACME account"), with the way back to the one just under it, so the stack reads on a phone where the levels under it are hidden. `DrawerShell.Closable` drops the close button for a drawer that must be left through its own action.
- **Gaps from pass 1, closed.** A staged upstream or profile opens again from the host drawer (its line under the form has Edit) as it was given (`UpstreamEditModel.FromDraft`, `ProfileEditModel.FromDraft`: still new, same ID) and comes back in its place. A review that refuses the one revision of a host and what was staged hands its errors back (`ReviewRequest.OnInvalidDocument`): the host's go to its fields, a staged object's are kept for it, marked "Needs a fix", and shown at its fields when it opens again (the review itself names the object, "app-backend > Address 1"). A "New ..." picker keeps showing what it had while the stacked drawer is open.
- **Tests.** bUnit: a staged upstream opened again and changed, a review's refusal of it marked and shown at its field, the trail of three levels; the overview's attention list (order, links, the live warning) and its "Nothing needs you"; the navigation's area for a profile, an ACME account, the users and My account; the tabs per role with the open one marked; the token, user, two-step and audit flows through their drawers (the token's and the codes' drawers not closing on Escape). Playwright: a staged upstream opened again, the host's certificate picker unchanged under the certificate drawer, the browser's back with unsaved changes asking and "Keep editing" keeping them, the users, token and two-step flows and an audit event in drawers; screenshots of each at 1366 and 390 px.
- **Not yet.** History and the simulator keep their two-column pages (no create or edit flow of their own). Changing one's own display name without `users:write` (unchanged).

**As built (M6): the logs page.** **Logs** sits under Insight beside Audit (route `/logs`, permission `logs:read`: Owner, Admin and Auditor by default, and available to an API token as a scope). The page reads `ILogFileStore` (`Paduraksa.Application.Logging`), the read-only side the dashboard and the API share: `List()` (the writer's names only, never a symlink or anything else in the directory, the live file first and rotated ones newest first by the timestamp in their name), `ReadTail(name, lines, filter)` (at most 1000 lines, 200 unless asked otherwise; a plain file is read from its last 1 MiB, a compressed one is decompressed from its start for at most 8 Mi characters, and every line is cut at 4096 characters while it is being read, so no line - however long, corrupt or compressed - is ever decoded whole; the last matching lines after an optional case-insensitive filter, `Truncated` when there was more) and `OpenRead(name)` (a read-shared stream for the download). A name is resolved inside the logs directory, checked to be a log name and not a link before and after opening, so `../`, an absolute path or a symlink is a 404, not a read. The page groups the files by log in a card per log (name, live/rotated/compressed, when it was last written, size), opens a file in `LogFileDrawer` (a wide drawer with the lines in monospace, a filter field, a 200/500/1000-line choice, a note when only the end was read, and the download), and says "No log files yet" with the directory's path on a fresh install. Downloading goes through `GET /api/v1/logs/{name}` (streamed, `Content-Disposition: attachment`, `text/plain` or `application/gzip`), so the page has no file access of its own; `GET /api/v1/logs` lists and `GET /api/v1/logs/{name}/tail?lines=&filter=` gives the same tail as JSON, both in the OpenAPI document with `logs:read`. Reading the logs is a read like the audit log's, so it is not audited (§9.4). bUnit covers the grouping, the empty state, the drawer and its filter, the truncation note and the refusal for a role without the permission; the API tests cover the list, the tail bounds, the download's headers and bytes, roles, path traversal and symlinks; the Playwright settings test opens the page, reads a line in the drawer and downloads the file, at 1366 and 390 px.

**As built (M9): the live tail.** **Live tail** is the next entry under Insight after Logs (route `/logs/live`, permission `logs:read`, so Owner, Admin and Auditor, and an API token with the scope). **Decision: a page of its own, not a tab on Logs.** Logs is a browser of files (a card per log, a drawer per file); a stream is not a file, it has controls a file doesn't (follow, pause, filters that reshape what arrives), and the navigation's longest-address rule keeps both entries marked right (`/logs` is Logs, `/logs/live` is Live tail). The page reads `LogTailService` (Application): the hub of §7.4, a subscription per page.
- **Table.** Newest first (**decision**: the view stays put at the top while following, with no scroll handling, and pausing is a plain freeze), at most 500 rows, each a grid row: time (UTC, to the millisecond), status (a badge coloured by class: 2xx green, 3xx blue, 4xx amber, 5xx red, none grey), method and path with its redacted query (the row's link is the keyboard's way in), host as received, client, time taken, upstream and request ID. Below 900 px a row is a card with labelled cells, the request first. Rows are components that never re-render (`ShouldRender` false after the first), keyed by sequence, so a batch costs the new rows and not 500 diffs.
- **Controls.** *Pause/Follow* freezes the view while the channel keeps filling to its bound (what overflows is counted: "N lines skipped"; "N waiting" says what a Follow will show); *Clear* empties the table; the *filters* are host (a picker of the configured hosts' first names, from the config when the role may read it, "Any host" and "No host matched"), status (one box taking `404` or a class `5xx`), client IP (an address or its leading part), method and path contains. A text filter applies after 400 ms; any change opens a new subscription (the backlog of 200 newest matches, then the stream) so the filter runs in the hub, not in the page. "Clear filters" shows only while one is set. **One filter contract:** `LogTailFilter` (Contracts) holds the status grammar (`TryParseStatus`, shared by the API's two parameters and the page's box) and the 256-character cap on every text, and `LogTailService.Open` and `Recent` refuse a filter that fails its `Problem()`, so the dashboard's circuit is held to the API's rules (the page stops its fields at the cap and opens no tail for a longer text a client sends anyway).
- **Pushing.** The page reads its channel every 300 ms (`PeriodicTimer` on `TimeProvider`; a `BatchInterval` parameter for tests) and renders once if anything changed: a batch of any size is one render, an idle page renders nothing. A batch larger than the table keeps its newest 500 and counts the rest as skipped. The tab's visibility is the existing `paduraksa.pageVisibility` helper (as the home page and the security page): hidden, the page stops reading, so nothing is rendered or sent to a tab nobody sees, and a show reads at once. Disposal unsubscribes. **Load**, proven in `ThousandsOfRequestsASecondStayBoundedAndRenderInBatches`: 20 000 entries a second through the hub for two seconds against a page at a 40 ms batch: renders stay within one per batch, the table holds 500 rows, the newest on top and in order, and the skipped count is positive; `ABurstIsRenderedOnceAndTheTableKeepsOnlyTheNewestRows` is the small version. (The renderer's own `RenderCount` counts every child, about 35 MudBlazor components a render, so the tests count the page's own.)
- **A row** opens `LogTailEntryDrawer`: status and matched host as chips, the request ID in the copy control `SecretValue` gives (clipboard through `DashboardBrowser`), the request line, host, client, time taken, upstream with its time, response size, `CF-Ray`, referer and user agent, and a **Simulate this request** button (for a role that may simulate) linking to `/simulator?url=https://<host><path><query>&clientIp=<client>&method=<method>`. **The simulator learned two query parameters for this** (`clientIp`, and `method` when it is one the form offers, next to the `url` it already took); the link's scheme is always `https`, since the entry doesn't say, and the query is the redacted one, so a simulation of a redacted token runs with the literal `REDACTED`.
- **Copy.** Two lines say something the data doesn't: the empty state ("Requests show up as they end. A host with its access log off never does."), and the skipped badge's tooltip. No lead, no eyebrow.
- **The stream** (`GET /api/v1/logs/tail`, `logs:read`): `text/event-stream`, `Cache-Control: no-store`, `X-Accel-Buffering: no`.
