## 15. Testing & quality

- **Unit:** policies (table-driven), compiler (config → snapshot golden files), typed IDs (fuzz parse/format round-trip), YAML round-trip, diff.
- **Settings and response headers (table-driven, since the settings refactor):** `SettingsInheritanceTests` holds one row per `HostSettings` field (nested records included) and checks it at every layer, overridden at each level and at its built-in default, through the real merge, validator and compiler; its completeness test walks the contract, so a new field fails until it has its row. `ResponseHeaderMatrixTests` is one table of answer kind x scheme x connection x name x each header's mode, asserting the exact header set a response carries, plus the compiled list, YARP's transform after a copy, the refusals the compiler makes and the 0 B assertions. One end-to-end test per header stays in `Tests.Integration` (`PublicHstsTests`, `PublicServerHeaderTests`, `PublicHttp3Tests`) with the special paths (502, WebSocket) beside them.
- **Integration:** real Kestrel on ephemeral ports, real SQLite in a temp dir, a validating **Pebble** container for ACME (HTTP-01 through the gate, §9.3; + **pebble-challtestsrv** with the DNS providers), a fake DNS provider. `HttpClient` with H2/H3 to verify SNI/ALPN.
- **As built (v0.20 refactor): the integration tests' shape.** A host is expensive to build and to stop (vault, config store, three listeners, the local CA and the admin leaf), so a test class whose tests only read, or whose tests make their own uniquely named resources, starts **one host for the whole class** through an xUnit class fixture (`tests/Paduraksa.Tests.Integration/Hosting/SharedFixtures.cs`: `SharedHost`, `SharedOwnerSession`, `SharedAdminProxy`, `SharedAdminApi`, `SharedSmallBodyHost`, `SharedLoopbackHost`). A test keeps a host of its own when it changes global settings, users or the vault, restarts, fails or stops the host, or counts something global; a test that only needs an account of its own (a wrong password that counts toward a lockout, a session it ends, a second factor it enrols) creates a user instead of a host. IDs, names and paths are per test, so a shared host leaves no order dependence. Two test-only seams keep start and stop cheap without weakening a claim: a test host hashes passwords at the validator's floor (100 000 iterations), and `SqlitePragmas.BusyTimeout` is settable once, before the first connection opens (the Data suite runs the two busy-timeout tests at one second, with Microsoft.Data.Sqlite's own retry window pinned at its 30-second default so only the busy handler can be what fails the write). Production never sets either. A host's stop no longer waits for the log writer's tick either: `RotatingLogFile.Flush` wakes the writer at once (§7.4). The browser tests share one console collector (`PlaywrightBrowser.CollectProblems`): a Content Security Policy violation or a console error fails the test, except a failure the test asks for and Blazor's own "Connection disconnected" report, which is a circuit reconnect under load and not an app error; pages are waited on by signal (the shell's `data-circuit=live`, a finite animation finishing) rather than by delay. The two 0 B assertions (`MacKeyTests`, `AllocationTests`) measure after warm-up on a thread of their own and take the smallest of three rounds, so a one-off on that thread cannot fail them while a real allocation still does.
- **Architecture** (`tests/Paduraksa.Tests.Architecture`), built on **ArchUnitNET** (maintained; NetArchTest is effectively unmaintained) plus plain reflection tests for the ID/DTO rules: dependency graph (§1), no `Data` types in `Contracts`/`Admin`/`Dashboard`, no untyped `*Id` properties, Vault types internal, hot-path namespace bans (`System.Linq`, `Regex` with backtracking, `DbContext`).
- **Performance:** BenchmarkDotNet for each policy + host lookup with `[MemoryDiagnoser]` asserting 0 B. A nightly `oha` load test comparing p50/p99 and RPS against the previous run and nginx; the S7 harness now covers nginx, Caddy, HAProxy, Traefik, Envoy and bare YARP with a recorded history (`bench/RESULTS.md`).
- **Security:** threat model doc, dependency scanning, CodeQL, fuzzing of ACME/JSON/YAML/multipart parsers, an audit-chain verifier.
  - **As built (threat model G9): CodeQL and the NuGet audit.** `.github/workflows/codeql.yml` analyses the C# product code (manual build of `Paduraksa.Server`, which builds every product project and no test, benchmark or fuzz project; the `security-extended` queries) on pushes to main, pull requests and weekly. `Directory.Build.props` sets `NuGetAudit` on with `NuGetAuditMode=all` (transitive packages too) and `NuGetAuditLevel=moderate`. *Decision:* the repository builds with warnings as errors, which would make every advisory fail restore, so the choice is explicit: high and critical (NU1903, NU1904) are errors, and a **moderate** one (NU1902) stays a warning in the restore log, because a moderate advisory that has no fixed release yet would otherwise stop every build, the Dependabot pull request that updates the package included. A low one (NU1901) is not reported. Checked: a restore of the whole solution reports no advisory today, and a probe project with a package that has high advisories fails with NU1903 while one with a moderate advisory only warns.
- **As built (v0.25): property tests and coverage-guided fuzzing.** Both kinds exist, built after M9 part 1 (planned for right after M7): the per-commit part is cheap, the deep part is manual or nightly.
  - **Property tests** use **CsCheck 5.0.0** and run in `dotnet test`. `tests/Shared/Property.cs` is the one entry point: CsCheck seeds only a run's first case and draws the rest at random, so each case's seed is derived from a fixed base, the property's name and the case's number (a run is the same every time, and adding a test moves no other test's cases), and a failing case is run on from its seed with 5,000 more iterations to shrink (CsCheck shrinks by keeping the smallest failure it meets). A failure prints the shrunk input and the seed that reproduces it, and becomes a plain regression test. Case counts are set per test; `PADURAKSA_PROPERTY_SCALE` multiplies all of them and `PADURAKSA_PROPERTY_SEED` moves the base (`PADURAKSA_PROPERTY_SCALE=50 dotnet test --project tests/Paduraksa.Tests.Proxy --filter-class "*PropertyTests"`). The defaults add about 40 s of CPU to the suite (about 25 s of wall time on four cores, the two projects overlapping); the nightly workflow runs them 50 times deeper with a seed of the day. What they check, each against a slow, obvious model:
    - `Paduraksa.Tests.Proxy`: `RoutePathPropertyTests` (the canonical matching path against a plain split-and-resolve model, its own idempotence, the stack and the pooled buffer alike, and 150 raw request targets over a socket to a bare Kestrel, whose `Request.Path` is canonicalised like the model and decoded like the simulator's model, and sent through the gate to a guarded `/admin` route); `GlobPatternPropertyTests` (the automaton against a backtracking regex of the same rules, on any path and on paths built from the pattern, `/**/**/x` runs, and which patterns compile); `CompiledRuleSetPropertyTests` (indexed exact and prefix paths, scanned globs and regexes against "the first rule whose conditions all hold", with non-ASCII case folding in the user agent); `AccessListPropertyTests` (allow, deny, countries, `satisfy`, drop and passwords through the real gate in Cloudflare mode against the table written out); `SimulatorParityTests` (the fixed tables, widened: 2,100 random requests across the unknown-host behaviours and trusted-proxy modes); `ForwardAuthCachePropertyTests` (the decision cache against a model that remembers an allow under everything the provider is sent, at the grain the answer says, over sequences of one-field mutations); `ForwardAuthHeaderPropertyTests` (the owned names and the stripping against a model with `_` and `-` as one character, and a name `ForwardAuthHeaderNames.Problem` accepts never owning a header the proxy owns); `AccessLogFormatterPropertyTests` (every line read back by a plain reader of the grammar finds exactly 13 fields, the quoted ones decode to what was sent, nothing a reader may take for a line end survives, a sensitive query value never appears).
    - `Paduraksa.Tests.Unit`: `ConfigGen` generates whole config documents (every list, every settings layer, references that mostly resolve, values that are mostly valid; since the M8/M9 fuzz round the layers also carry error pages at every layer in both modes with valid and refused templates, maintenance with a bypass access list and `replaceUpstream`, and the pipeline property fails if too few valid documents reach them); `ConfigPipelinePropertyTests` (the validator never throws, a document that validates never fails to compile, its canonical JSON is idempotent, round-trips and still validates and compiles, and the same config written differently has the same canonical bytes); `SimulatedBehaviourTests` (any generated valid document and request: the simulator says what the real gate answers; and the page: `errorPage` is the operator's page exactly when the gate serves bytes equal to the preview compiler's rendering of the entry a plain walk over the document finds, host then profile then global defaults, for refusals and for maintenance).
  - **Fuzzing** uses **SharpFuzz 2.3.0** (the pinned `sharpfuzz` tool instruments the proxy's assemblies in a published copy) with a pinned release of **libfuzzer-dotnet** (v2025.05.02.0904, checked against its digest). `fuzz/Paduraksa.Fuzz.Targets` holds the targets, `fuzz/Paduraksa.Fuzz` the libFuzzer driver and the `list`, `replay` and `seed` modes, both in the solution, so CI builds them. Targets: `proxy-protocol` (v1 and v2, in one segment and two, a header that parses is its `consumed` bytes and no shorter prefix of it is anything but incomplete), `pow-cookie` (the cookie scan and the token check; a token that verifies is the one canonical encoding of what it says), `pow-form` (the posted form, the challenge, the solution against an independent SHA-256 count, and the return target, which can never leave the site), `basic-auth` (against an independent base64 decode), `client-address` (`CF-Connecting-IP`, `X-Forwarded-For`, `True-Client-IP`, `CF-IPCountry` through the resolver in every trusted-proxy mode: an untrusted peer is never moved by a header and gets no country; an address taken from a header is the one `IPAddress` reads; the parser's text and UTF-8 forms agree) and `forward-auth-answer` (the provider is a handler that answers with the input, so the whole gate runs with no socket; the answer is compared with a plain reading of the rules, and a log line that names an exception is the crash, since the policy turns one into a 502). Three more cover the M8 and M9 surfaces (**As built (fuzz round for M8 and M9)** below): `error-page`, `upstream-error-replacement` and `access-log-tail`. Every target also checks the same verdict for the same bytes and 0 B (an allocation counts when two runs of the same input both make it). Instrumented code may only run once SharpFuzz has set up its shared memory, so the driver finds the target inside the first run.
  - **Corpora and crashes.** `fuzz/corpus/<target>/` is committed (the `seed-*` files are well-formed starting points written by `Paduraksa.Fuzz seed`; the rest is what runs found, minimised by `scripts/fuzz.sh --merge`) and `fuzz/regressions/<target>/` holds inputs that once crashed a target; `.gitattributes` marks both binary (a PROXY header ends in CR LF). `tests/Paduraksa.Tests.Fuzz` replays every committed input through its target on every commit, with no libFuzzer and no instrumented assembly, so a corpus regression fails CI. A crash is copied into `regressions/`, fixed, and gets a unit test beside the code. `scripts/fuzz.sh [--time S] [--target T] [--merge] [--replay] [--list]` publishes the harness, instruments, runs libFuzzer per target for the time box and fails on a crash or an error; `.github/workflows/fuzz.yml` (`workflow_dispatch` and a nightly schedule, never push or pull request) runs it (15 minutes a target by default) and the property tests 50 times deeper, and uploads crash inputs, logs and the grown corpus. `docs/development.md` has the commands.
  - **What they found.** Property tests: (1) the simulator decoded `%5C` in a path to `%5c` but Kestrel hands over a backslash (found by the differential against real Kestrel with `/%20adm%61in;x%zz%252F%5c.%C3%A9`; the trace's decoded path was wrong, matching was not); (2) the forward-auth decision cache key left out the scheme and the standard hint headers the provider is sent (`User-Agent`, `Accept`, `Accept-Language`, `X-Requested-With`), so an allow was served to a request that differs in them (found by the cache model); (3) the access-log formatter left NEL, the other C1 controls and U+2028 and U+2029 unescaped, which a reader that splits on a Unicode line end takes for a line end; (4) a custom `Server` header that the global defaults leave without a value validated but could not compile (a document with no host, found by validate-implies-compile). One property was wrong, not the code: `RoutePath.IsCanonical` answers false for any path with a `%`, canonical or not, because it is only a fast path. The fuzzers found no product bug: in the sessions below two harness bugs came up in the first forward-auth runs (a header name that is not a token, which a transport never delivers, within 243 runs; repeated `Location` lines compared as one joined string), both fixed in the harness. Each property and harness was shown to fail by breaking the code it checks (the `/**/**/x` fix undone, a prefix candidate dropped, a satisfy rule changed, a redirect status, the owned-name folding, the cache grain, a canonical ordering, an expiry check, a return-target rule, an empty user name accepted, an untrusted peer believed, an identity-value check dropped, an allocation added, a verdict made random); libFuzzer itself found the empty-name break from one valid seed in under a minute.
  - **Sessions run (v0.25).** On a four-core box, one target per process: `proxy-protocol` 5 min (5.3 M runs), `pow-cookie` 5 min (3.8 M), `pow-form` 5 min (4.7 M), `basic-auth` 5 min (4.0 M), `client-address` 10 min (5.2 M), `forward-auth-answer` 10 min (3.2 M); none of the final runs crashed. A run of every target for the release checklist (M11) is `scripts/fuzz.sh --time 3600 --merge`.
  - **As built (M10, `m10-mcp`): the `mcp-sandbox` target.** The parent of the MCP sandbox (section 11.2) against a child that is not to be trusted. *Decision:* the
    harness no longer fuzzes the scripts in process (a stock engine in the same process as the fuzzer crashes and hangs on the very scripts the boundary exists to
    absorb, which is noise, not a finding), and a child per input is too slow (a start is about 180 ms), so the target has two modes picked by the first byte. **Frames**
    (three inputs in four, in process, instrumented, fast): the rest of the input is what a hostile child writes to its stdout, fed to the parent's run protocol
    (`RunProtocol.ExchangeAsync`) over a stub dispatcher and the real request policy, with a known run id that the seeds carry (so the fuzzer can mutate it into a wrong, a missing or an old one: no frame that is not an answer to the run in progress may be served). It may return an outcome or end as a breach or a protocol violation, and nothing else may
    happen: no other exception; a result within the output cap, an error of at most 700 characters without control characters, a bounded note; at most the request limit
    dispatched; every dispatched request one the policy allows (a plain path under `/api/v1`, a bounded query, body and entity tag, a write only with the bytes saying
    `confirm` or as a dry run); and everything the parent wrote back a `response` frame. **Scripts** (one in four, when the harness can start a child: `scripts/fuzz.sh`
    publishes an uninstrumented copy and sets `PADURAKSA_FUZZ_CHILD`, and a replay from the driver finds the driver's own assembly): the rest is a script, run on a real child
    with 1 s of wall and CPU time. The properties are the parent's: it never throws, the call ends within its wall limit plus a margin, the answer is bounded, and the pool
    holds at most one child. The script seeds (23) are the scripts that were once dangerous in process plus ordinary ones; the frame seeds (21) cover every shape of violation. Without
    a child a script input is read as frames, so the corpus replays in CI (`Paduraksa.Tests.Fuzz`) without one. *Found:* the first minute found a real bug in the parent, a
    frame with invalid UTF-8 inside a string reached `JsonElement.GetString`, which throws `InvalidOperationException` (the production loop would have treated it as a
    broken child, but by accident); frames are checked for UTF-8 first, text with a lone surrogate is a violation, and the input is in `fuzz/regressions/mcp-sandbox/`. *Session:* 25 minutes (`--out /tmp/fz-mcp-r3`), 70 562 executions (47 a second: a quarter of the inputs run a script on a real child, and the breaching ones wait out a limit), no crash, no abandoned or orphaned child, and the corpus merged on edges (`-use_counters=0`) to 397 inputs. After the review that bound every frame to its run, a fresh corpus from the new seeds and 18 minutes (`--out /tmp/fz-mcp-r4`): 163 833 executions (151 a second), no crash, no orphaned child, merged on edges to 475 inputs.

  - **Gaps.** The ACME response parsing gets a harness when it lands (M10's YAML import has one, below). The fuzzers are not shown a real network: HTTP framing is Kestrel's and the response body's streaming is not driven. The simulator parity property leaves out hosts Kestrel would answer 400 itself (the socket parity tests pin that rule) and feeds the real gate already-decoded paths (the Kestrel differential covers the decoding). The rate-limit table, the proof-of-work page and the gate's allocation have no property test. Coverage is the proxy's and Core's assemblies only; YARP and the framework are not instrumented. The harnesses build and run on Linux x64. The generators reach M8's settings since the fuzz round for M8 and M9 (M9 added nothing to the config document: notification channels are an operational table, not config).
  - **As built (fuzz round for M8 and M9).** *Targets.* `error-page` compiles a host whose pages use every variable, as a message and as a document (the global defaults' page for an unknown host, a wildcard host's own for a path no route takes), and sends the real gate a `Host`, an `X-Request-Id` from a trusted or an untrusted peer, a `CF-Ray` with Cloudflare's headers trusted or not and an `Accept`, decoded as UTF-8, Latin-1 or UTF-16 (which can hold a lone surrogate). The page must be the page's template with each variable replaced by a plain HTML encoder's reading of the value as the rules cut it (the host without its port at 253 characters, the ray at 64 and empty unless trusted, the request ID the client's only from a trusted peer and only if it is 1 to 128 visible ASCII characters); no variable adds a raw `<`, `>`, `"` or `'`, an `&` that starts no entity, or a non-ASCII byte; `HEAD` has no body; HTML is chosen exactly when an independent reading of `Accept` says so; the same input gives the same bytes; the writer and the `Accept` decision allocate nothing. `upstream-error-replacement` asks `ReplaceUpstreamErrorTransform.Replaces(mode, status, mediaType, upgrade, started)`, the pure decision the transform and the target now both call (the transform's success path is unchanged, one integer comparison, its allocation tests green), for each mode against a plain model of the PLAN's rules (never for an upgrade, an event stream, `application/grpc*` or a started response; `html` only for `text/html` in any case and with parameters; only for a status that has a page), with the media type read from the `Content-Type` lines by `HttpResponseMessage` as the transport would, and also fed the raw text of each line. `access-log-tail` sends one request's hostile host, method, path, query, protocol, upstream, request ID, `CF-Ray`, referer and user agent through `AccessLogFormatter` and through the tail entry built as the drain builds it: a plain reader of the line's grammar finds thirteen fields and no line end; every tail field equals the file line's field (numbers by value, quoted text exactly unless a cap cut it, and then a prefix of the full escape closed by the marker); the line's request target is what a plain model builds from the method, path, redacted query and protocol; a sentinel in a sensitive parameter at the front of the query appears in neither output, and with the query string off neither does a sentinel in a plain parameter; the line costs 0 B.
  - *Generators and the simulator.* `ConfigGen` sets the M8 settings at every layer (above), `Respelled` adds empty error-page and maintenance layers that canonical JSON must drop, and a property holds the simulator's page selection (`ErrorPageSelection`, which the writer and the simulator both call) to what the gate serves for refusals and maintenance, by comparing the gate's bytes with the preview compiler's rendering of the entry a plain walk over the document finds.
  - *Found.* The fuzz round found one product bug: `AccessLogFormatOptions.IsSensitive` enumerated its key list through `IReadOnlyList<string>`, which boxes an enumerator, so every query parameter of every access-log line allocated 32 B on the drain thread (the formatter documents that a line costs nothing; nothing measured it). It indexes the list now, with a test beside it (`AccessLogFormatterTests.ALineWithAQueryAllocatesNothing`) and the input kept in `fuzz/regressions/access-log-tail/`. Harness bugs, fixed before the final runs: `HttpClient` forgives blanks around the `/` of a media type (`applicatio /grpc` reads `applicatio/grpc`) and reads only the first of two `Content-Type` lines, and the tail's query can be cut twice (the redaction's marker, then the budget's), which leaves up to five dots. Mutation checks: each target failed when the code it checks was broken (a slot written unencoded, a referer left unescaped, redaction switched off, the maintenance page's selection taken out of the writer).
  - *Sessions run (this round).* On a 20-core box, one target per process, 25 min each, run in parallel: `error-page` 30.6 M runs (20 k/s), `upstream-error-replacement` 95.7 M (64 k/s), `access-log-tail` 15.9 M (11 k/s); none of the final runs crashed, and each corpus was merged (`--merge`) into `fuzz/corpus/<target>/` (144, 101 and 62 files). The runs before them stopped on the harness bugs above and restarted from the fixed harness.
  - *Left out.* `Paduraksa.Fuzz` still has no target for the dashboard's own parsing, the live tail's SSE framing or the notification senders' payloads (a sender builds its request from the operator's own text, not from a client's). The tail's query keeps the file's 1 KiB cap, so a query cut twice can end in `.....`: cosmetic, left as it is.
  - **As built (M10): the `yaml-import` target** (`fuzz/Paduraksa.Fuzz.Targets/YamlImportTarget.cs`). The input is YAML bytes, read through the guarded reader, mapped onto the document and merged, with no database, twice: replacing the empty config and merging into a small stored one (so identity matching and names that resolve against stored objects are reached). Properties: no exception, only a result with errors (each a JSON-pointer path, a code and a message that names no CLR or parser type, under 800 characters); the same bytes give the same result, the new IDs included (a counter supplies them); **the caps hold**: the reader under a tight set of limits (6 levels, 400 nodes, 64-character scalars, 16-character keys, 4 anchors, 8 aliases) returns a tree that, counted again, is no deeper or larger and has no longer scalar or repeated key, whatever aliases and anchors the input uses, and under the real limits an import of up to 4 KiB allocates under 192 MiB and runs under 8 s; and an accepted document that validates is exported, read back and is the same canonical bytes (versions aside), with no `password` key in the export (a document the validator refuses can still be written, but the import refuses its repeated IDs, so only that it can be written is checked). `scripts/fuzz.sh` instruments `Paduraksa.Application`, `Paduraksa.Contracts` and `YamlDotNet` as well as Proxy and Core. 24 seeds (well-formed configs, a flow-style one, an export, a billion-laughs document, deep nesting, tags, two documents, a merge key, a BOM, invalid UTF-8).
  - *Sessions run (M10).* On a 20-core box, one target per process: two 25-minute runs (7.9 M and 6.3 M runs, about 5 k/s each), then two 22-minute runs of the final code seeded from their corpora (6.4 M runs each), about 27 M runs in all. The first pair stopped one run early on a real finding, the second pair did not crash. The corpora were merged into `fuzz/corpus/yaml-import/` (3 052 files, 1.3 MB: every input that reached a feature the others did not).
  - *Found.* The reader's error for an unknown alias quoted the alias's name, which the input makes as long as it likes (a 3 000-character message): the name is cut at 40 characters now, the input is `fuzz/regressions/yaml-import/alias-unknown-long-name`, with a unit test beside the reader. Harness bug, fixed before the final runs: the round trip was first asserted for every accepted document, but a document with a route ID used by two hosts (a merge of an export whose host ID was changed) maps and cannot be read back, because the import refuses repeated IDs. The unit tests found the others: the emitter's own analysis misjudges some mixes of YAML indicators and quotes (`0 *'` followed by a backtick as a key), so the writer asks for a plain scalar only for text of letters, digits and a few joiners and quotes everything else itself; and the scanner throws `InvalidOperationException` for an unterminated flow collection followed by more lines. The property over `ConfigGen` found that an empty `passiveHealth` is not the same as none and that a valid document can name an object that is gone in a layer nothing uses; both are in §11.5.

---

## 16. Deployment

- **Container:** `ghcr.io/<org>/paduraksa:<ver>`, multi-arch (amd64/arm64), Ubuntu Noble base + `libmsquic` (pinned), `ASPNETCORE_HTTP_PORTS` cleared, non-root. Listens on **80/443 (tcp+udp)/9443 inside the container**, mapped 1:1 in compose, so `Alt-Svc` and redirects never see a translated port. Non-root binding below 1024 works because Docker (20.10+) sets `net.ipv4.ip_unprivileged_port_start=0` in the container's network namespace. For `network_mode: host` or other runtimes, the image also gives the apphost `cap_net_bind_service` (`setcap`). If someone still remaps ports, `Paduraksa:Listen:PublicHttpsPort` drives `Alt-Svc` and redirects. One volume: `/data`. Health: `/healthz` and `/readyz` (§11.3). The image's own Docker `HEALTHCHECK` uses `paduraksa doctor --liveness` (which calls `/healthz` over loopback) at a 30 s interval, because the image ships no curl/wget. That costs a short-lived process per probe, which is acceptable at that interval. External monitors should hit `/healthz` directly.
- **Bare metal:** self-contained single-file publish + systemd unit (`AmbientCapabilities=CAP_NET_BIND_SERVICE`, `LoadCredential=master.key`).
- **SQLite:** `Microsoft.Data.Sqlite` ships its own bundled SQLite (SQLitePCLRaw `e_sqlite3`), so the host's SQLite version doesn't matter. Required floor: **3.35** (`RETURNING`, used by EF Core 10; WAL long predates it). `doctor` prints the runtime version.
- **Runtime tuning:** Server GC with DATAS (adapts heap to load, good for small homelab boxes), `TieredPGO`, ReadyToRun.

**As built (M5): the container and the install path.** The `Dockerfile` is at the repository root, with `.dockerignore`, so `docker build .` is the whole recipe.
- **Build.** `dotnet/sdk:10.0-noble` publishes the server **framework-dependent but RID-specific** (`-r linux-$TARGETARCH`, `--self-contained false`, `PublishReadyToRun=true`, apphost kept) into `/app`: the app is one web-server binary with no native dependencies of its own, so a self-contained publish would only duplicate the ~80 MB runtime the runtime image already carries at the exact patch level. ReadyToRun crossgens the app's own assemblies (not the framework's); `--build-arg PADURAKSA_READYTORUN=false` turns it off. `TARGETARCH` (amd64/arm64) is mapped to the .NET RID explicitly, so a cross build fails loudly instead of producing the wrong architecture, and restore runs as its own step with a BuildKit cache mount before `publish --no-restore`.
- **Runtime.** `mcr.microsoft.com/dotnet/aspnet:10.0-noble` (Ubuntu Noble) with **libmsquic 2.5.10** pinned from `packages.microsoft.com/ubuntu/24.04/prod`; the feed's signing key is checked against Microsoft's fingerprint (`BC528686B50D79E339D3721CEB3E94ADBE1229CF`) after dearmoring, `ldconfig` is checked during the build, and curl/gnupg are purged in the same layer that installed them. The server logs **`HTTP/3 is on: QUIC on UDP 443 ...`** when libmsquic loaded (and the existing line when it did not), which is what the smoke test asserts. Userspace is uid/gid **10001** with `/data` owned by it; the apphost gets `cap_net_bind_service` via `setcap` before `libcap2-bin` is purged in that same layer, for runtimes without Docker's `ip_unprivileged_port_start=0`. A runtime that drops capabilities (`cap_drop: [ALL]`, Kubernetes "restricted") must add `NET_BIND_SERVICE` back, or exec fails with EPERM.
- **Environment.** `Paduraksa__DataDirectory=/data`, `ASPNETCORE_HTTP_PORTS`/`ASPNETCORE_URLS` blanked, `PATH` including `/app` (so `docker compose exec paduraksa paduraksa setup-token` works), Server GC with DATAS (`DOTNET_gcServer=1`, `DOTNET_GCDynamicAdaptationMode=1`), `DOTNET_TieredPGO=1`, diagnostics off. `EXPOSE 80/tcp 443/tcp 443/udp 9443/tcp`, `STOPSIGNAL SIGTERM`, `HEALTHCHECK --interval=30s --timeout=5s --start-period=30s --retries=3 CMD paduraksa doctor --liveness`.
- **Compose and systemd.** `deploy/compose.yaml` builds the local image from the checkout by default (`image: paduraksa:local`; M11's published image replaces the `build:` block), maps the public ports 1:1 (including `443:443/udp`) and publishes the admin as `127.0.0.1:9443:9443` (threat model G4: Docker's published ports sit ahead of the host firewall, so a bare mapping would offer the sign-in to every network; the LAN-address form is in a comment and install.md "Exposing the admin" explains the choices), uses one named volume for `/data`, sets a stable `hostname`, `init: true`, `stop_grace_period: 35s` and `restart: unless-stopped`, rotates its json-file log, documents the `cap_drop: [ALL]`/`NET_BIND_SERVICE` rule and the bind-mount `install -d -o 10001 -g 10001`, and uses the `PADURAKSA_MASTER_KEY_FILE` Docker secret by default (`./master.key`, created before the first start, host owner 10001: non-swarm Compose bind-mounts keep the host owner and mode). `deploy/paduraksa.service` is the bare-metal unit for a self-contained publish: `AmbientCapabilities=CAP_NET_BIND_SERVICE`, `LoadCredential=master.key` with `PADURAKSA_MASTER_KEY_FILE=%d/master.key`, `StateDirectoryMode=0700`, `UMask=0077`, `RestartPreventExitStatus=2 3` (startup failed, invalid configuration), a hardened sandbox and `TimeoutStopSec=40s`.
- **List settings.** `ConfigurationListOptions` normalises every `IList<string>` option (`AdminAllowedSources`, both PROXY protocol `TrustedSources`, `Admin:CertificateNames`, ACME `DnsPropagation:Resolvers`) from either indexed keys or one comma-separated scalar; values are trimmed and empty entries dropped, and a scalar beside indexed keys wins deterministically with a startup warning that the indexed entries were ignored.
- **Docs.** `README.md` has the short install section; `docs/install.md` the details: compose up, reading or reprinting the setup token, the master key as a Docker secret with the uid-10001 chown, trusting the local CA root from `root.pem` (PEM for `update-ca-certificates`; DER `root.crt` for Android/iOS), both list forms, opening `https://<host>:9443/`, the 1:1 ports, HTTP/3 and `443/udp`, bind-mount ownership and capabilities, where data lives and how to back it up, upgrade (`git pull && docker compose up -d --build`) and `vault rotate-kek` (stop, rotate, restart), the health endpoints, and the bare-metal path including libmsquic.
- **Test.** `ContainerSmokeTests` (trait `Category=Container`, off unless `PADURAKSA_CONTAINER_TESTS=1`) builds the image, runs it on an empty volume with random loopback ports, and asserts the healthcheck turns healthy, the setup banner and the HTTP/3 line are in `docker logs`, `docker exec paduraksa setup-token` works, `/api/v1/setup` answers 200 over HTTPS, ports 80/443 accept a connection, and `docker stop` exits 0 inside the 35-second grace period; a second run with `--cap-drop ALL --cap-add NET_BIND_SERVICE` covers the setcap hardening path. It removes its containers, volumes and image.

- **Backups:** `paduraksa backup`, which takes an online SQLite backup of both DBs + KEK handling instructions. **As built (M10):** one passphrase-encrypted archive of both databases and the last-known-good file, made online, that restores on a fresh machine under its own master key (§8.4); the `docker exec` and `docker compose run` forms and the homelab routine are in `docs/install.md` ("Backups"). The volume also holds `/data/run/server.lock`, the claim of the one running server (§8.4).

### 16.1 Fleet mode: control plane and data plane (after v0.1)

**Goal.** Run many proxy replicas (Kubernetes, or several hosts) that share certificates, ACME challenges, config and limits, without giving up what the single node is good at: a hot path with no I/O, keys that never leave a vault, and a proxy that keeps serving when everything behind it is down. Single-node (`--role all`) stays the default and gets none of the extra machinery.

#### Roles
One binary, `paduraksa serve --role all|control|data`.
- **Control plane** (`control`): admin app, REST/MCP/dashboard, config store and revisions, the authoritative vault, ACME orchestration and the renewal scheduler, audit, the rollout controller and fleet statistics. It serves no proxied traffic. One replica on SQLite is enough: data nodes never touch its database, so **Postgres is only needed for a highly available control plane** (phase F4).
- **Data plane** (`data`): the public app and the hot path only. It has no config database, no admin listener and no ACME client. Its state is a local **bundle cache** (an `emptyDir` or a small volume), so it scales horizontally as a plain Deployment.
- `all` is both roles in one process, wired in-process exactly as today (the sync below is the in-process `ConfigChangeFeed`/`SnapshotStore` path), so single-node users never run a sync protocol.

#### Sync: data nodes keep a fast local copy
Data nodes **connect out** to the control plane over one long-lived mTLS HTTP/2 stream (ASP.NET Core gRPC, first-party). Nothing connects into a data node except proxied traffic, so data nodes need no admin port and work behind NAT.
- **What is synced, content-addressed by SHA-256:**
  - **Config:** the canonical revision document (already canonical JSON with a hash, §6.1). Each node **compiles it locally** with the same compiler, so a snapshot is never shipped as an opaque blob and the node's own certificate table and YARP binding are built the normal way.
  - **Certificates and keys:** chains in plain form; private keys only sealed (see the trust boundary below).
  - **Secrets the hot path needs** (the PoW HMAC key; forward-auth holds none): sealed the same way.
  - **Artifacts:** Cloudflare IP ranges, the Geo DB and error pages. Each is fetched or built once at the control plane and distributed.
  - **ACME HTTP-01 tokens:** a priority channel (below).
- **Delta and resume.** A node reports what it holds (the applied revision, certificate versions, artifact hashes); the control plane sends only what is missing. Every message is idempotent, so a reconnect resumes rather than restarts.
- **Applying.** A node verifies hashes, compiles, publishes through its own `SnapshotStore`, then **acks** with the revision it is serving, or with the compile errors it hit. The control plane always knows which revision each node serves.
- **Static stability.** A node that loses the control plane keeps serving its last applied bundle, which survives a restart through the bundle cache (the same idea as the last-known-good file, §6.4). It reports staleness (`paduraksa_fleet_bundle_age_seconds`), and `/readyz` stays ready. Only certificate expiry limits how long a fleet can run headless, and the control plane alerts long before that.

#### Trust boundary across nodes
Keys still never exist in plaintext outside a vault.
- **Enrolment.** At first start a data node's vault generates a node key pair (X25519 or P-256) and joins with a **join token** (`pdk_join_...`, single-use, or reusable with expiry and a node-group scope for autoscaled pods). The control plane's internal CA issues the node's mTLS client certificate. Nodes get a typed ID, `node_` (UuidV7), and a label set.
- **Vault-to-vault transfer.** The control vault re-wraps a certificate's key for each node's public key (ECDH + HKDF + AES-GCM, AAD = node ID, certificate ID, version). Only the node's vault can unwrap it, and it re-seals locally under its own KEK. This is a new vault capability, `IVaultTransfer`, restricted to enrolled node keys, so the key-material ban list still holds everywhere else.
- **Least privilege.** A node receives only the keys of the certificates its node group serves, so a compromised edge node leaks only its own hosts. Removing a node revokes its certificate, stops its stream and rotates nothing else. Nodes not seen for a configurable time are expired automatically (autoscaled pods come and go).

#### ACME in a fleet
- Ordering, renewal and DNS-01 run only at the control plane; with an HA control plane, behind a leader lease (`ILeaderLease`, always "leader" on a single node).
- **HTTP-01 fan-out with acks.** The orchestrator publishes the token on the priority channel and tells the CA to validate **only after every healthy node that serves the name has acked it** (a bounded wait). Nodes that don't ack in time are listed in the order's step log. The CA's validation request can then land on any pod. Withdrawing the token fans out the same way.
- Renewed certificates are synced like any other certificate change. Certificates bypass rollouts (below): a renewal goes everywhere at once.

#### Rollouts: test config changes on part of the fleet
A commit no longer means "every node, now"; it sets a **target revision per node group**.
- **Node groups and labels:** `zone=eu`, `group=edge`, `canary=true`. A group can also **shard hosts**, serving only the hosts assigned to it, for example an EU edge serving EU hosts. That is also what scopes the keys above.
- **Rollout plans.** Each plan is an ordered list of stages, each a selector or a percentage of nodes plus a bake time, for example canary node → 10 % → 50 % → 100 %, with **gates** between stages:
  - **Manual:** "advance".
  - **Automatic (phase F3):** compare the stage's nodes with the rest of the fleet on gate errors, upstream 5xx rate, p99 latency, compile failures and health-check failures. Any node that fails to compile stops the rollout.
- **Outcomes.** A failed gate pauses the rollout, or rolls it back automatically (the target returns to the previous revision) if the plan says so. An operator can also pause, resume, abort or **apply now** for emergencies.
- **Defaults.** No plan means every node gets the revision immediately, which is today's behaviour. Each rollout start, stage advance, pause and rollback is audited.
- **What a rollout changes.** Only *config revisions* are staged. Certificate changes, ACME tokens, secret rotations and artifact updates go fleet-wide immediately, because holding them back would break something, not test it.
- **What users see.** While a rollout runs, different nodes serve different revisions. The dashboard shows the fleet split per revision. The simulator (§7.7) can target "the revision node X is serving", and access-log entries carry the revision that answered them.

#### Statistics: push the cheap things, pull the heavy ones
- **Push.** Each node sends aggregated **deltas** every 10 s on its sync stream: counters and histogram buckets from the fixed-size M9 metric slots, keyed by host/route/upstream ID, plus its upstream-health view. The payload is small, works through NAT, and gives the rollout gates their data even when nobody has the dashboard open. The control plane merges the deltas into the M9 rollups and exposes fleet-level `/metrics`.
- **Backpressure.** While the control plane is unreachable, a node keeps a bounded ring of deltas (for example 15 min). After that it drops the oldest deltas and counts the drops, so a reconnect backfills recent history and the hot path never blocks on statistics.
- **Pull on demand,** over the same stream, for things too heavy or too rare to push: live access-log tail from selected nodes (opened when a dashboard view asks), per-node destination health detail, connection and TLS debug, a node's current snapshot summary.
- **Prometheus-native too.** Each data node keeps its own `/metrics` on an internal port, so a cluster that already scrapes pods can ignore the fleet aggregate.

#### Rate limits across replicas
Local token buckets on each node, with each node's share of a limit (for example the limit divided by the number of healthy nodes in the group) pushed by the control plane as part of the bundle, so the hot path stays I/O-free. This is approximate by design. Exact shared limits are an option for later: local buckets reconciled asynchronously, gossiped between nodes or batched to Valkey/Redis every ~100 ms, so overshoot is bounded by the sync interval. Never a network round trip per request.

#### Upgrades and version skew
- **Upgrade order:** the control plane is upgraded first and serves data nodes of the same and the previous minor version.
- **Protocol and bundle versions:** the protocol is versioned, and bundles carry the document's `apiVersion` and a minimum compiler version. A node that can't compile a revision acks the error and keeps serving the old one, which also stops any rollout.
- **Migrations:** with an HA control plane they become expand/contract and run once, from the leader or a Job.

#### Kubernetes shape
- **Control plane:** a StatefulSet with 1 replica and a PVC (SQLite), and a Service for the admin UI and for the sync endpoint.
- **Data plane:** a Deployment with an HPA. It gets the join token from a Secret, an `emptyDir` bundle cache, a LoadBalancer Service for 80/443 tcp+udp (`externalTrafficPolicy: Local`, or PROXY protocol from the LB), and PodDisruptionBudgets.
- **Packaging:** a Helm chart with both roles, plus the declarative mode (§11.6) feeding the control plane.

#### Phasing
- **F1:** roles, enrolment, config/certificate/artifact sync with static stability, vault-to-vault key transfer, HTTP-01 fan-out with acks, pushed statistics and the fleet view in the dashboard.
- **F2:** node groups and labels, host sharding with scoped keys, rollout plans with manual gates.
- **F3:** automatic gates and auto-rollback.
- **F4:** an HA control plane (Postgres, leader lease, LISTEN/NOTIFY between control replicas) and exact shared rate limits.

#### Seams worth keeping in the single-node code
These are cheap now and expensive later. Fold them into the milestones that touch the code anyway.
1. **Config source.** The public side gets its snapshots through a config-source abstraction (today the in-process `ConfigService` publish), so a remote bundle source can replace it.
2. **Leader lease.** The ACME scheduler and any other singleton job run through `ILeaderLease`.
3. **Challenge publishing** goes through `IAcmeChallengeStore` publish/withdraw (already a seam) and can grow acks.
4. **Int64Ordered node ID.** The layout reserves bits for a node ID before any data exists, because changing it later is a data migration.
5. **Statistics.** M9 metric slots are designed as mergeable deltas (counters and fixed histogram buckets, no per-node-only aggregates).
6. **Database portability.** SQLite-specific behaviour (`BEGIN IMMEDIATE` serialization, `NOCASE`, rowid keys) stays inside Storage/Data behind intent-named helpers, so the control plane could run on Postgres.

---

## 17. Open questions (proposed resolutions, pending owner sign-off unless marked decided)

| # | Question | Proposed |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | License | **Decided: Apache-2.0** (`LICENSE`, `NOTICE`). Adoption matters more for infra tools; AGPL only pays off if closed forks are a real worry. |
| 2 | UI kit | **MudBlazor**; revisit only if it gets in the way. |
| 3 | Admin port default | **HTTPS 9443 with the internal CA** + root-CA download in the wizard + **plain-HTTP loopback-only fallback** (§3). Never plain HTTP on the LAN. |
| 4 | Geo DB | **DB-IP Lite** by default (no account), MaxMind GeoLite2 optional with a license key. **Not in M7**: countries come from `CF-IPCountry` only; the database is a gap with a named seam (§7.3, **As built (M7): country rules**). |
| 5 | NPM importer | **After v0.1**: the data-model mapping is bigger than it looks. |
| 6 | L4 streams | **After 1.0**. Keep the HTTP/YARP story clean first. |

Secrets, MAC keys, the authenticating HTTP handler (refusals are `SecretTransportRefusedException`) and the JWS signer. Certificates: staged keys for CSRs, chain attach with key, name, validity and signature checks, PEM/PFX import with KDF limits, self-signed and local-CA leaves capped at 825 days, one cached `SslStreamCertificateContext` per certificate, and a public-data catalog.

The placeholder is off in Release builds.

Extra trusted roots for private CAs.

Each user's password is a vault credential (a PBKDF2 verifier, and a verification cache whose entries live until five minutes after their last use), the document names its credential and nothing else, and a password check that finds no slot waits in a bounded line (32 places per slot, 16 per client, a 10-second wait) instead of being refused.   - **Request limits and rule sets (§7.3):** body, header and request-target limits per host with a body override per route, answered before the upstream sees any; rule sets (`rs_`): ordered path, method and user-agent rules (exact, prefix, a bit-parallel glob, a NonBacktracking regex) that deny, drop or allow, with three presets that are copied to edit.

Differential checks of the canonical path (also against real Kestrel), the glob automaton, rule-set and access-list verdicts, the simulator against the real gate, the forward-auth cache key and owned headers, the access-log formatter, and the config pipeline (a document that validates compiles; canonical JSON is idempotent and round-trips).

  - **Health (§7.4):** a watcher reads YARP's verdict per destination once a second, off the request path; a change must hold 10 s, a flapping destination is reported once, and a publish reports nothing. Each change is audited and shown as status dots on Upstreams, the hosts list and the overview.
  - **Notifications (§5.1):** ntfy, Gotify, Discord, a signed webhook and email (MailKit) channels, their secrets in the vault behind a lend-only capability; events for upstream down/up, certificate expiry at 30/14/7/1 days, ACME failure and recovery, and sign-in lockouts and new addresses; a bounded dispatcher with retries and storm digests; Settings > Notifications.

The tail shows only what the file would: one shared escaper, the file's query cap and redaction, and a host with logging off is in neither. The request path is unchanged at 0 B.
  - **Surfaces (§12, §11.1):** a Live tail page under Insight (filters, pause, batched rendering, an entry drawer that opens the simulator), and `GET /api/v1/logs/tail` as Server-Sent Events behind `logs:read`.
- **v0.28:** after M9: fuzz targets for M8/M9, quieter logs, an honest metrics timeline.
  - **Fuzzing (§15):** `error-page`, `upstream-error-replacement` and `access-log-tail` targets, config generators that reach the M8 settings, and two long rounds of all nine targets (no crash; one allocation in the access-log formatter found and fixed).
  - **Logs (§7.4):** a client that vanished mid-write is one Debug line and a counter (`paduraksa_connection_errors_total{kind="peer_gone"}`), not two Error stacks per connection.
  - **Metrics (§7.4):** every observed minute is stored with its observed seconds, so downtime is a gap and never stitched; observed time is monotonic and a stall that served requests counts as observed; graceful stop writes the partial minute and a minute written twice merges; the 1 h view is seeded from the rollups at boot; the charts break their lines at gaps.
  - **Dashboard (§12):** the hosts list keeps the maintenance and enable controls inside the row.
- **v0.29:** M10 interop (all but drafts) and traffic analytics.
  - **Backup and restore (§8.4):** an Owner-only, passphrase-encrypted (Argon2id, chunked AES-256-GCM) online archive of the config and the vault, its keys re-wrapped under a backup-only key so it restores on a fresh machine under that machine's master key; `paduraksa backup|restore`, `POST /api/v1/backup`, Settings > Backup; one server per data directory (`run/server.lock`).
  - **YAML import and export (§11.4, §11.5):** a friendly format with names and optional IDs that round-trips to the identical revision, merge and replace through the pipeline with dry-run, the API, the CLI (a client of the running instance) and Settings > Export and import; a `yaml-import` fuzz target.
  - **MCP in code mode (§11.2):** two tools, `search` and `execute`, running stock Jint in a child process that the parent limits from outside and kills on any breach (time, CPU, memory, requests, protocol, frames bound to their run); the request policy (refused list, confirm or dry-run, the token's identity) stays in the parent; off by default (`Paduraksa:Mcp:Enabled`).
  - **Analytics (§7.4, §12):** visitors and content, clients and bots, computed in the access-log drain off the request path (HyperLogLog visitors with a per-process salt, Space-Saving top-N, an own user-agent classifier that skips work when the drain falls behind, robots.txt parity for bots ignoring it), 5-minute, hourly and daily rollups, and an Analytics page.
  - **Tests (§15):** the CI-only flakes found on the 4-core runner fixed at their cause (fake clocks moved before a wait was armed, parallel test classes sharing process state, bUnit's 1 s default).
